Transverse momentum in double parton scattering: factorisation, evolution and matching Preprint: NIKHEF 2016-028, DESY 17-014
Maarten G. A. Buffing, Markus Diehl, and Tomas Kasemets

Abstract
We give a description of double parton scattering with measured transverse momenta in the final state, extending the formalism for factorisation and resummation developed by Collins, Soper and Sterman for the production of colourless particles. After a detailed analysis of their colour structure, we derive and solve evolution equations in rapidity and renormalisation scale for the relevant soft factors and double parton distributions. We show how in the perturbative regime, transverse momentum dependent double parton distributions can be expressed in terms of simpler nonperturbative quantities and compute several of the corresponding perturbative kernels at one-loop accuracy. We then show how the coherent sum of single and double parton scattering can be simplified for perturbatively large transverse momenta, and we discuss to which order resummation can be performed with presently available results. As an auxiliary result, we derive a simple form for the square root factor in the Collins construction of transverse momentum dependent parton distributions.



1
Introduction

Proton-proton collisions at high energies are sensitive to regions of phase
space where partons have small momentum fractions. This implies high parton
densities and thus increases the importance of events in which two or more
partons in each proton take part in a hard interaction. The most frequent
and best studied case of such multiple hard interactions is double parton
scattering (DPS). This mechanism can be especially prominent in cross
sections depending on transverse momenta in the final state. Its
theoretical description involves double parton distributions (DPDs), which
quantify the joint distribution of two partons inside a proton and contain a
wealth of information on correlations between the proton constituents.

Experimental measurements of double parton scattering contributions to
different final states dates back to experiments at the ISR

Akesson:1986iv
and SPS
Alitti:1991rd
. A wide range of DPS
processes has been investigated at the
Tevatron
Abe:1993rv
;
Abe:1997bp
;
Abe:1997xk
;
Abazov:2009gc
;
Abazov:2011rd
;
Abazov:2014fha
;
Abazov:2014qba
;
Abazov:2015fbl
;
Abazov:2015nnn

and in run I of the LHC
Aaij:2011yc
;
Aaij:2012dz
;
Aaij:2015wpa
;
Aad:2013bjm
;
Aad:2014rua
;
Aad:2014kba
;
Aaboud:2016dea
;
Aaboud:2016fzt
;
Chatrchyan:2013xxa
;
Khachatryan:2016ydm
. An overview of most of these
measurements can be found in figure 14 of
Aaboud:2016fzt
. The
importance of such processes will be even more pronounced at the full LHC
energy (first results from run II are reported in
CMS:2017jwx
) and at
future hadron colliders.

Theoretical and phenomenological analyses of DPS have a long history; an
overview of early work can be found in
Sjostrand:2004pf
. Following
the seminal papers
Paver:1982yp
and
Mekhfi:1983az
, significant
effort has been invested in recent years to achieve a systematic theory
description of DPS
Diehl:2011tt
;
Diehl:2011yj
;
Manohar:2012jr
;
Diehl:2015bca
, aiming at the same level of rigour as has
been achieved for the familiar single parton scattering (SPS) mechanism. A
formalism for combining the single and double parton scattering contributions
to the physical cross section without double counting was recently presented
in
Diehl:2017kgu
– a different scheme was proposed earlier

Manohar:2012pe
. However, there remain several gaps in our
understanding, and the present paper aims at closing some of them.

A crucial aspect for understanding DPS are correlations between the two
partons that are probed in the reaction. Correlations in spin and in colour
have been classified systematically

Mekhfi:1985dv
;
Diehl:2011yj
;
Manohar:2012jr
and will play an important
role in the present work. Their size is poorly known, but can be limited by
positivity bounds
Diehl:2013mla
;
Kasemets:2014yna
, which have similar
theoretical status as positivity constraints on single parton distribution
functions (PDFs). Quark model calculations
Chang:2012nw
;
Rinaldi:2013vpa
;
Rinaldi:2014ddl
;
Rinaldi:2016jvu
;
Rinaldi:2016mlk
;
Broniowski:2013xba
;
Broniowski:2016trx
;
Kasemets:2016nio
typically yield
strong correlations in the valence region, but for the region of small
momentum fractions
x
there is little guidance from models so far. The
decrease of spin correlations under evolution to higher scales has been
studied in
Diehl:2014vaa
, and their influence on final-state
distributions has been investigated for several processes

Manohar:2012jr
;
Kasemets:2012pr
;
Echevarria:2015ufa
. The generation of
parton correlations by the splitting of a single parton into two has been
investigated by several groups
Diehl:2011yj
;
Gaunt:2012dd
;
Blok:2011bu
;
Blok:2013bpa
;
Ryskin:2011kk
;
Ryskin:2012qx
; a simplified
implementation into the event generator Pythia is described in

Blok:2015rka
;
Blok:2015afa
.

At the level of integrated cross sections, double parton scattering is
suppressed by
\Lambda^{2}/Q^{2}
compared to single parton scattering, where
Q

denotes the scale of the hard scattering and
\Lambda
the scale of
nonperturbative interactions
Diehl:2011tt
;
Blok:2010ge
. However, there
are situations where DPS can nevertheless compete with SPS, for instance when
the latter is suppressed by higher powers in coupling constants. A prominent
example is same sign
W
pair production

Kulesza:1999zh
;
Gaunt:2010pi
;
Ceccopieri:2017oqe
, which is an important
search channel for physics beyond the Standard Model. A generic mechanism
enhancing DPS over SPS in processes involving small parton momentum fractions

x
is the fact that with decreasing
x
the density of two partons increases
roughly like the square of the single parton density.

If the transverse momenta
q_{T}
of the particles produced by a hard scattering
are small compared with
Q
, then DPS has the same power behaviour in
1/Q
as
SPS
Diehl:2011tt
;
Blok:2010ge
. If
q_{T}
is of order
\Lambda
, then one
needs information about the nonperturbative “intrinsic” transverse momentum
of partons. However, in the region
\Lambda\ll q_{T}\ll Q
one can reliably
compute the
q_{T}
spectrum in perturbation theory, provided that one resums
the large logarithms of
Q/q_{T}
that arise in this regime. This resummation
is intricately related with evolution in the rapidity of emitted gluons. For
SPS processes producing colourless particles, like Drell-Yan lepton pairs or a
Higgs boson, a powerful theoretical formalism has long been established

Collins:1984kg
;
Catani:2000vq
and been pushed to high perturbative
accuracy, see e.g.
Catani:2013tia
and references therein. Formulations
using soft-collinear effective theory (SCET) have been given in

GarciaEchevarria:2011rb
;
Becher:2012yn
;
Neill:2015roa
.
A precise theory for DPS at small measured
q_{T}
is of obvious interest. In

Blok:2015rka
;
Blok:2015afa
the DDT formalism
Dokshitzer:1978hw

has been extended to DPS processes. The analysis in

Diehl:2011tt
;
Diehl:2011yj
was based on transverse-momentum dependent
(TMD) factorisation in the original formulation of Collins and Soper

Collins:1981uk
, which also underlies the CSS resummation formalism

Collins:1984kg
. Since then, an improved version of TMD factorisation
for SPS processes has been formulated by Collins
Collins:2011zzd
; a
brief review is given in
Aybat:2011zv
;
Collins:2011ca
and the
differences between the old and new versions are described in

Collins:2017oxh
.

The aim of the present paper is twofold. Firstly, we complete the
formulation of DPS in
Diehl:2011tt
;
Diehl:2011yj
and adapt it to the
new factorisation formalism of
Collins:2011zzd
, providing a
systematic analysis of soft-gluon effects, rapidity evolution and colour
correlations. Secondly, we show how the theory simplifies for intermediate
transverse momenta
\Lambda\ll q_{T}\ll Q
, where transverse-momentum
dependent DPDs can be matched on transverse-momentum integrated
distributions, with the transverse-momentum dependence being computed in
perturbation theory. This significantly increases the predictive power of
the theoretical framework. Some of our main results have been reported in

Buffing:2016qql
;
Buffing:2017sxz
.

TMD factorisation in proton-proton collisions can be established to all orders
in perturbation theory for the production of colourless particles such as a
Higgs boson or electroweak gauge bosons
Collins:2011zzd
. Because of
serious complications from soft gluon exchange, it is not known if and how
the formalism could be extended to hard-scattering processes with coloured
particles in the final state, such as jets or heavy quarks

Rogers:2010dm
. We will therefore limit our discussion of TMD
factorisation in DPS to colourless final states as well. Important channels
are the production of two electroweak gauge bosons (often called the double
Drell-Yan process), of a Higgs boson and an electroweak gauge boson, or of a
Higgs boson pair. Instead of a heavy boson, one may also consider a photon
pair of large invariant mass.

This paper is organised as follows. In section
2
we
recall some of the concepts and results for TMD factorisation in single
parton scattering. In section
3
we discuss
properties of collinear matrix elements and of the soft factor, which are
the ingredients in the definition of transverse-momentum dependent and
transverse-momentum integrated DPDs, which we will call DTMDs and DPDFs,
respectively. Our definition generalises the combination of collinear and
soft factors in
Collins:2011zzd
to double parton distributions, and
it provides an alternative form of this construction for single parton TMDs.
The colour structure of DPS is significantly more complicated than the one
of SPS, and we show in section
4
how this structure can be
handled in a general and efficient way. We find significant simplifications
for transverse-momentum integrated quantities. In section
5
we
present the general factorisation formula for DPS at low
q_{T}
, its
combination with SPS, as well as the evolution equations for DTMDs and DPDFs
and their general solution. Section
6
is devoted to the
region
\Lambda\ll q_{T}\ll Q
. We establish the matching of DTMDs onto
different types of transverse-momentum integrated distributions. The
multi-scale nature of the problem leads to different matching regimes, which
we combine in a consistent way using a subtraction formalism. In section

7
we give one-loop expressions for perturbative quantities
that appear in the DPS cross section, extending previous work in the
literature and discussing several technical aspects of the computation. We
summarise our main results in section
8
. A variety of
technical details and results are given in the appendices.

2
Reminder: single TMD factorisation

To begin with, let us recall a few results from TMD factorisation, as laid
out in
Collins:2011zzd
. The cross
section depends on TMDs that describe the distribution of partons inside
the proton in both longitudinal and transverse momentum. Throughout this
work, we consider unpolarised protons. The factorisation formula for
Drell-Yan production then reads

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma}{dx\,d\bar{x}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{q}\hat{\sigma}_{q\bar{q}}(Q,\mu)\int\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\boldsymbol{z}}\,W_{q\bar{q}}(x,\bar{x},\boldsymbol{z};\mu)+\{q\leftrightarrow\bar{q}\}\,,

(1)

where
\hat{\sigma}_{q\bar{q}}
is the cross section for
q\bar{q}

annihilation into an electroweak gauge boson and

\displaystyle W_{q\bar{q}}(x,\bar{x},\boldsymbol{z};\mu)=f_{q}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\,f_{\bar{q}}(\bar{x},\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\bar{\zeta})

(2)

is the product of a quark and an antiquark TMD. The invariant mass of the
boson is
Q
and its transverse momentum is
\boldsymbol{q}
. The TMDs depend
on longitudinal momentum fractions (
x
or
\bar{x}
) that are fixed by
final-state kinematics, and on a transverse distance
\boldsymbol{z}
that is
Fourier conjugate to the transverse parton momentum (and often denoted by

\boldsymbol{b}
in the literature). They also depend on an ultraviolet
renormalisation scale
\mu
and on a rapidity parameter (
\zeta
or

\bar{\zeta}
) as we will review later. Notice that the rapidity
parameter dependence cancels in
W_{q\bar{q}}
.
The parton-level cross section
\hat{\sigma}_{q\bar{q}}
and the overall
cross section may be taken differential in additional variables if more than
one particle is produced in the hard scattering. An example is the angular
distribution of the leptons into which the electroweak gauge boson decays in
Drell-Yan production. In this case one must include the TMDs for transverse
quark and antiquark polarisation in (
2
).

The dependence of a TMD on the renormalisation scale is given by

Collins:2011zzd
;
Aybat:2011zv

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}\,f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)\,f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\,,

(3)

where
a=q,\bar{q},g
labels the parton type. The rapidity dependence
of the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{F,a}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}\,\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle={}-\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)\,,

(4)

where
\gamma_{K,a}
is called the cusp anomalous dimension. It depends on

\mu
via
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
, i.e.
\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)=\gamma_{K,a}\bigl(\alpha_{s}(\mu)\bigr)
. From (
4
) one readily finds

\displaystyle\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{a}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\mu}

(5)

with

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\mu^{2})\,.

(6)

The evolution of TMDs with the rapidity scale
\zeta
is governed by the
Collins-Soper equation
1
1

1

We generally follow the notation of

Collins:2011zzd
;
Collins:2017oxh
in the present paper. We
do however not use a tilde to denote quantities in transverse position
space, thus writing
f
and
K
instead of
\tilde{f}
and

\tilde{K}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}\,f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)\,f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\,,

(7)

whose kernel satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}\,{K_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)}={}-\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)\,.

(8)

If the transverse boson momentum
\boldsymbol{q}
is large compared with the
scale
\Lambda
of non-perturbative interactions, one can use a
short-distance expansion that connects a TMD
f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
at small

\boldsymbol{z}
with a conventional collinear PDF
f_{b}(x)
according to

\displaystyle f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b}{C}_{ab}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\underset{x}{\otimes}f_{b}(x^{\prime};\mu)\,,

(9)

with the convolution product defined by

\displaystyle C(x^{\prime})\underset{x}{\otimes}f(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{1}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}\,C(x^{\prime})\,f\biggl(\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}\biggr)\,.

(10)

The expansion (
9
) has power corrections in the
parameter
\Lambda|\boldsymbol{z}|
. Combining it with the solution of the
evolution equations in
\mu
and
\zeta
, one obtains

\displaystyle f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu}\frac{d\mu^{\prime}}{\mu^{\prime}}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a}(\mu^{\prime})-\gamma_{K,a}(\mu^{\prime})\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\mu^{\prime}}\biggr]+K_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu_{0})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\mu_{0}}\mskip 1.5mu\biggr\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{b}{C}_{ab}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z};\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{2})\underset{x}{\otimes}{f_{b}(x^{\prime};\mu_{0})}\,.

(11)

The short-distance coefficient
C
and the Collins-Soper kernel
K
should
be evaluated with a scale choice that avoids large logarithms, so that they
can be reliably calculated in fixed-order perturbation theory. In the
non-perturbative region of
\boldsymbol{z}
one needs a model ansatz for

f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
. The so-called
b^{*}
prescription

Collins:1981va
;
Collins:1984kg
can be used to smoothly interpolate
between such an ansatz and the perturbative result (
2
).

In the following sections, we will show how these results can be extended
to the case of double parton scattering. There are several aspects that
make this extension far from trivial. One is the larger number of
coloured particles involved in the process, which leads to a non-trivial
colour structure of DTMDs and DPDFs. As a consequence, even DPDFs depend
on the rapidity parameter
\zeta
, unlike PDFs for a single parton

Diehl:2011yj
;
Manohar:2012jr
. A second aspect is that DPS
involves several transverse distances, which makes the analogue of the
short-distance expansion (
9
) more complicated.

3
Defining double parton distributions

As reviewed in
Diehl:2015bca
, factorisation of DPS processes
involves separating the leading graphs for the cross sections into
subgraphs that are hard, soft, or collinear to one of the two incoming
protons. The treatment of the soft subgraph is intimately related with
the rapidity parameter
\zeta
mentioned in the previous subsection.

There are in fact different alternatives for such a treatment. The
analysis of the double Drell-Yan process in
Diehl:2011yj
followed
the original procedure for TMD factorisation by Collins and Soper

Collins:1981uk
and did not work out all relevant aspects of the
problem. In the present paper, we perform a systematic analysis of DPS in
the factorisation framework of Collins
Collins:2011zzd
.
We explicitly show how the soft factors relevant for the cross section can
be entirely absorbed into DTMDs or DPDFs, and we derive the resulting
evolution equations in
\zeta
, as well as the ones in
\mu
. Other schemes
to handle soft factors and rapidity dependence will briefly be discussed in section
8
.

The starting point of our discussion is an intermediate expression of the
DPS cross section, given in section 2.1 of
Diehl:2015bca
. The cross
section for the production of two sets of colourless particles involves a
term

\displaystyle H_{1,q\bar{q}}\,H_{2,q\bar{q}}\;F^{T}_{\text{us},\bar{q}\bar{q}}(v_{R})\,S_{qq}^{-1}(v_{L},v_{R})\,S_{qq}(v_{L},v_{R})\,S_{qq}^{-1}(v_{L},v_{R})\,F_{\text{us},qq}(v_{L})

(12)

for the annihilation of two quarks in one proton with two antiquarks in
the other proton, and corresponding terms for the other parton
combinations. For definiteness we have only written down the DPDs for
unpolarised partons; polarised terms have the same soft factor.
H_{i,q\bar{q}}

denotes the squared hard-scattering amplitudes, with appropriate spin
projections (see section 2.2 in
Diehl:2011yj
) but with the colour
structure removed as specified in (
116
) below.

F_{\text{us}}
denotes unsubtracted collinear matrix elements, and
S
is a
soft factor. The inverse of this factor removes contributions of soft
gluons from the unsubtracted collinear matrix elements, so that
S^{-1}\mskip 1.5muF_{\text{us}}
receives only contributions from collinear gluons.
For brevity we have omitted momentum fraction and position space arguments
in (
12
), as well as renormalisation and factorisation
scales and colour indices.
F_{\text{us}}
is a row vector in colour space
(with one index for each of the four parton legs), and
S
is a matrix
with two times four indices. The spacelike four-vectors
v_{L}
and
v_{R}

denote the directions of Wilson lines and will be specified later. As
discussed in
Collins:2011zzd
;
Diehl:2015bca
,
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
have to
be chosen such that the effects of so-called Glauber gluon exchange on the
cross section can be subsumed into the soft and collinear factors in
(
12
).

The aim of the following sections is to combine soft factors and
collinear matrix elements in such a way that the product (
12
)
takes on a simple form. After introducing the necessary notation, we derive a
number of symmetry properties of soft factors in section
3.2

and then discuss our central hypothesis for their rapidity dependence, given
in (
35
) and (
36
). This leads us to the definition
(
3.3
) of DPDs and to their rapidity evolution equation
(
45
) in section
3.3
. We discuss
renormalisation in section
3.4
and derive the basic evolution
equations (
3.4
) and (
55
) relevant for DTMDs.
Applying our construction to TMDs for a single parton in
section
3.5
, we are led to the definition
(
60
) and see that it is equivalent to the definition of
Collins
Collins:2011zzd
by virtue of the relation (
59
).

3.1
Collinear matrix elements

To begin with, let us recall the definitions of unsubtracted DTMDs and
DPDFs in terms of proton matrix elements. These will later be combined with
soft factors in order to define the double parton distributions that appear in
the cross section formula.

For two partons
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, the unsubtracted DTMDs are defined in
terms of matrix elements as
Diehl:2011yj
;
Diehl:2011tt

\displaystyle F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=2p^{+}(x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})^{-n_{1}}\,(x_{2}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})^{-n_{2}}\int\frac{dz^{-}_{1}}{2\pi}\,\frac{dz^{-}_{2}}{2\pi}\,dy^{-}\,e^{i\mskip 1.5mu(x_{1}z_{1}^{-}+x_{2}z_{2}^{-})\mskip 1.5mup^{+}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\langle\mskip 1.5mup\mskip 1.5mu|\,\mathcal{O}_{a_{1}}(y,z_{1})\,\mathcal{O}_{a_{2}}(0,z_{2})\,|\mskip 1.5mup\mskip 1.5mu\rangle\,,

(13)

where
n_{i}=1
if parton number
i
is a gluon and
n_{i}=0
otherwise. We
use light-cone coordinates
w^{\pm}=(w^{0}\pm w^{3})/\sqrt{2}
and the
transverse component
\boldsymbol{w}=(w^{1},w^{2})
for any four-vector
w
. The
definition (
3.1
) is natural for a proton moving to the right,
i.e. for
p^{3}>0
. For a left moving proton, i.e. for
p^{3}<0
, one would
interchange the roles of plus and minus coordinates. It is understood that

\boldsymbol{p}=\boldsymbol{0}
in both cases, and that the proton polarisation is
averaged over. Setting
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(
3.1
), one obtains DPDFs, which are relevant for collinear
factorisation. As we will discuss later, this changes the ultraviolet
behaviour of the operators.

The operators for quarks in a right moving proton read

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{a}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{q}\bigl(y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z\bigr)\,W^{\dagger}\bigl(y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z,v_{L}\bigr)\,\Gamma_{a}\,W\bigl(y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z,v_{L}\bigr)\,q\bigl(y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z\bigr)\Big|_{z^{+}=y^{+}=0}

(14)

with spin projections

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{+}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta q}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{+}\gamma_{5}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\delta q}^{j}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}i\sigma^{j+}\gamma_{5}\quad(j=1,2)

(15)

onto unpolarised quarks (
q
), longitudinally polarised quarks (
\Delta q
) and transversely polarised quarks (
\delta q
). We do not explicitly
display the transverse index
j
of the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\delta q}

and of the corresponding DPDs, unless it is needed. The field with
argument
y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z
in
\mathcal{O}_{q}(y,z)
is associated with a
quark in the amplitude of a double scattering process and the field with
argument
y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z
with a quark in the complex conjugate amplitude.
The Wilson lines are defined as

\displaystyle W(\xi,v)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{P}\exp\biggl[\mskip 1.5muig\mskip 1.5mut^{a}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!ds\,vA^{a}(\xi+sv)\mskip 1.5mu\biggr]\,,

(16)

where
\operatorname{P}
denotes path ordering, such that fields
t^{a}A^{a}(\xi+sv)
with smaller
s
stand further to the left in the expanded
exponential. Our convention for the strong coupling
g
is specified in
appendix
D
. Throughout this work, we only consider
the case
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{0}
. In the matrix element (
3.1
) for
a right-moving proton, one takes a direction
v_{L}
with
v_{L}^{-}\gg-v_{L}^{+}>0
, and in its analogue for a left-moving proton one has a direction

v_{R}
with
v_{R}^{+}\gg-v_{R}^{-}>0
. In both cases, the Wilson lines are
past-pointing.
Analogous operators are defined for antiquarks, with some sign changes as
specified in section 2.2 of
Diehl:2011yj
. For gluons, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{a}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{a}^{jj^{\prime}}\,G^{+j^{\prime}}\bigl(y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z\bigr)\,W^{\dagger}\bigl(y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z,v_{L}\bigr)\,W\bigl(y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z,v_{L}\bigr)\,G^{+j}\bigl(y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z\bigr)\Big|_{z^{+}=y^{+}=0}

(17)

with spin projections

\displaystyle\Pi_{g}^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\Delta g}^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=i\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\bigl[\Pi_{\delta g}^{kk^{\prime}}\bigr]{}^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,kk^{\prime}}

(18)

onto unpolarised gluons (
g
), longitudinally polarised gluons (
\Delta g
) and linearly polarised gluons (
\delta g
). The indices
j,j^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}=1,2
run over transverse components,
\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}
is the antisymmetric
tensor with
\epsilon^{12}=1
, and
\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,kk^{\prime}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,kk^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\Bigl(\delta^{jk}\delta^{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\delta^{jk^{\prime}}\delta^{j^{\prime}k}-\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\delta^{kk^{\prime}}\Bigr)\,.

(19)

The Wilson lines in (
17
) are in the adjoint representation
rather than in the fundamental one (see section
3.2
).
Making the colour indices of the operators explicit, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q,\,jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bar{q}_{k^{\prime}}\,(W^{\dagger})_{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\,\Gamma\,W_{jk}\,q_{k}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{g,\,aa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Pi\,G_{b^{\prime}}\,(W^{\dagger})_{b^{\prime}a^{\prime}}\,W_{ab}\,G_{b}

(20)

for quarks and gluons, respectively.

For the discussion of ultraviolet renormalisation and of the
short-distance expansion, it is useful to introduce operators that
correspond to a definite light-cone momentum fraction
x
of a parton:

\displaystyle O_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=2p^{+}\mskip 1.5mu(xp^{+})^{-n}\int\frac{dz^{-}}{2\pi}\,dy^{-}\;e^{ixz^{-}p^{+}}\,\mathcal{O}_{a}(y,z)\,.

(21)

Using translation invariance, one readily finds that unsubtracted single
and double parton TMDs are then given by matrix elements

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{+}-p^{\prime+})\,2p^{+}f_{\text{us},a}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\langle p^{\prime}|\,O_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z})\,|p\rangle\,,

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{+}-p^{\prime+})\,2p^{+}F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\langle p^{\prime}|\,O_{a_{1}}(x_{1},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\,O_{a_{2}}(x_{2},\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\,|p\rangle\,,

(22)

where
\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{p}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The corresponding collinear
distributions are obtained by setting
\boldsymbol{z}
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
to zero.

We note that in the DPS cross section, there are also distributions
describing the interference between different parton types in the process
amplitude and its complex conjugate, i.e. between quarks and antiquarks,
between quarks and gluons, or between quarks of different flavour

Diehl:2011yj
;
Manohar:2012jr
;
Diehl:2013mla
;
Kasemets:2014yna
. At low
values of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, such interference DPDs are expected to be
negligible, because they have no dynamic cross talk with gluon
distributions, which grow most strongly with decreasing
x
. Although we
do not consider interference DPDs in this work, they can be treated with
the methods presented in the following.

Let us remark that an analysis of transverse-momentum dependent DPDs in the
small-
x
limit has recently been given in
Golec-Biernat:2016vbt
. The
quantities considered in that work are Fourier transformed w.r.t. our
variable
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
and – more importantly – integrated over
\boldsymbol{y}
.
To make contact with the DPS cross section, one would need to restore the
dependence of these distributions on
\boldsymbol{y}
, or on the Fourier conjugated
momentum.

3.2
Soft factors

Before constructing the final DPDs, we must take a closer look at the soft
factor, which is defined as the vacuum expectation value of Wilson lines.
For reasons that will become clear later, we define an “extended soft
factor” with open colour indices of all Wilson lines as

\displaystyle\bigl[S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}_{j_{1}j_{1}^{\prime}j_{2}j_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl\langle\,0\,\big|\mskip 1.5mu\bigl[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}}_{j_{1}j_{1}^{\prime},k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}\,\bigr[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime},l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}_{j_{2}j_{2}^{\prime},k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime}}\,\big|\,0\,\bigr\rangle\,.

(23)

This factor appears in the cross section with two
q\bar{q}
annihilation
subprocesses, and the subscripts
q
refer to the right-moving parton in each
subprocess. The operator

\displaystyle\bigl[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{ii^{\prime},ll^{\prime}}_{jj^{\prime},kk^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=W_{ij}(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{L})\,W_{kl}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{R})\,W_{l^{\prime}k^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{R})\,W_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{L})

(24)

is a product of four Wilson lines, which are
defined as in (
16
), but with
\xi^{+}=\xi^{-}=0
, so that their
position arguments are only in the transverse plane and hence written in
boldface.
Primed and unprimed indices
j
and
k
in the Wilson lines of
(
3.2
) correspond to gluon fields
A(\xi)
at light-cone zero
(
\xi^{+}=\xi^{-}=0
), whereas primed and unprimed indices
i
and
l

correspond to gluon fields at light-cone infinity,
A(\xi-\infty v)
. A
pictorial representation of (
3.2
) is given in
figure
1
.

Figure 1:
Wilson lines and colour indices of
the extended soft factor defined by (
3.2
) and
(
3.2
). Wilson lines with subscripts
1
are
grouped into the operator
O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}_{1})
and those
with subscripts
2
into the operator
O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})
.

For right-moving antiquarks instead of quarks, one replaces
W_{ij}
with

W^{*}_{ij}=W^{\dagger}_{ji}
and vice versa, which corresponds to replacing

it^{a}_{ij}
with
(it^{a}_{ij})^{*}=-it^{a}_{ji}
in the exponential
(
16
). For gluons, one takes adjoint Wilson lines
W_{bc}
, obtained
by replacing
it^{a}_{ij}
in (
16
) with
f^{abc}
. This reflects the
fact that the generators
T^{a}
of the colour group in the adjoint
representation are given by
(T^{a})_{bc}=-if^{abc}
. We therefore have

\displaystyle S_{qq}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle S_{q\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}q}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle S_{qg}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}g}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle S_{gg}

\displaystyle=S_{gg}^{*}\,,

(25)

where we have omitted colour indices for brevity.
Note that, by construction, the Wilson line operators for all representations
are unitary, i.e.
W(\xi,v)\mskip 1.5muW^{\dagger}(\xi,v)=\mathbb{1}
, where
\mathbb{1}
is the
unit matrix in the relevant colour space.

In processes producing colourless particles, one needs
(
3.2
) with all index pairs
j
,
k
contracted, i.e.

\displaystyle\bigl[S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime},\,l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl\langle\,0\,\big|\mskip 1.5mu\bigl[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}}\,\bigr[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime},l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}\,\big|\,0\,\bigr\rangle

(26)

with

\displaystyle\bigl[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{ii^{\prime},ll^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl[W(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{R})\bigr]_{il}\,\bigl[W(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{R})\,W^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z},v_{L})\bigr]_{l^{\prime}i^{\prime}}\,.

(27)

We simply call this the “soft factor” (without the specification
“extended”). Regarding this as a matrix in the index pairs
(i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime})
and
(l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime})
, we define the
transposed matrix as

\displaystyle\bigl[S_{qq}^{T}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bigl[S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime},i_{1}i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}i_{2}^{\prime}}

(28)

and the Hermitian conjugate as
S_{qq}^{\smash{\dagger}\phantom{T}}=\bigl[S_{qq}^{T}\bigr]^{*}
. Corresponding definitions hold for
S_{gg}
,

S_{gq}
,
S_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}
etc.
There are a number of symmetry constraints on the soft factor:

1.

With the restrictions on the directions of Wilson lines in
S(v_{L},v_{R})
specified below (
16
), one can always perform a
longitudinal boost such that
v_{L}=(\alpha,\beta,\boldsymbol{0})
and
v_{R}=(\beta,\alpha,\boldsymbol{0})
. A parity transformation interchanges
plus- and minus components and thus, combined with the boost, exchanges

v_{L}\leftrightarrow v_{R}
. Parity and boost invariance therefore give

\displaystyle S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(-\boldsymbol{z}_{1},-\boldsymbol{z}_{2},-\boldsymbol{y};v_{R},v_{L})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{R},v_{L})\,,

(29)

where
a_{1},a_{2}
denote the parton types,
q,\bar{q},g
. In the final
step we used that the soft factor depends on position arguments only via
scalar products between
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
. This
follows from rotational and parity invariance, combined with the fact that
the Wilson line directions
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
are purely longitudinal.

2.

A combined parity and time reversal transformation reverses the
vectors
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
, as well as the position arguments. This gives

\displaystyle S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(-\boldsymbol{z}_{1},-\boldsymbol{z}_{2},-\boldsymbol{y};-v_{L},-v_{R})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};-v_{L},-v_{R})\,,

(30)

where in the last step we used the same symmetry argument as in
(
1
).

3.

Since the soft factor is constructed from products
\bigl[W(z,v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}(z,v_{R})\bigr]_{il}
for quarks,
\bigl[W(z,v_{R})\,W^{\dagger}(z,v_{L})\bigr]_{li}
for antiquarks, and

\bigl[W(z,v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}(z,v_{R})\bigr]_{ad}
for gluons,
we have

\displaystyle S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{R},v_{L})

\displaystyle=S_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y};v_{L},v_{R})\,,

(31)

where in the second step we have used (
1
). The soft matrix
is thus Hermitian in the groups of indices for the Wilson lines along the
different directions
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
.
Combining this result with the relations (
25
), we find

\displaystyle S_{qq}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}^{T}\,,

\displaystyle S_{q\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}q}^{T}\,,

\displaystyle S_{qg}

\displaystyle=S_{\bar{q}g}^{T}\,,

\displaystyle S_{gg}

\displaystyle=S_{gg}^{T}\,,

(32)

where the matrices on the left and the right hand side have identical
arguments. We can thus identify the product
F^{T}_{\text{us},\bar{q}\bar{q}}\,S_{qq}^{-1}=(S_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}^{-1}\,F_{\text{us},\bar{q}\bar{q}})^{T}
in
the expression (
12
) as the analogue of the product

S_{qq}^{-1}\mskip 1.5muF_{\text{us},qq}
.

From now on we restrict ourselves to spacelike Wilson line directions in

S(v_{L},v_{R})
that satisfy

\displaystyle v_{L}^{-}

\displaystyle>0\,,

\displaystyle v_{R}^{+}

\displaystyle>0\,,

\displaystyle Y_{L}\ll Y_{R}\,,

(33)

where the rapidity
Y_{v}
of the spacelike vector
v
is defined as

\displaystyle Y_{v}=\frac{1}{2}\log\biggl|\frac{v^{+}}{v^{-}}\biggr|\,.

(34)

Cases other than (
33
), such as
v_{L}^{-}<0,\,v_{R}^{+}<0
,
can be realised using the symmetry relations (
1
) and
(
2
). Owing to boost invariance and the fact that the Wilson lines

W(\xi,v)
are invariant under rescaling
v\to\lambda v
, the soft factor
depends on
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
only via the difference of the Wilson line
rapidities. We can hence write
S(v_{L},v_{R})=S(Y_{R}-Y_{L})
.

At this point we make an
assumption
on the properties of the soft
factor, which we cannot fully prove (we specify below what can be proven
at present).

For the soft factor
S(Y_{R}-Y_{L})
with
Y_{R}-Y_{L}\gg 1
(for brevity we
omit the indices
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
and arguments
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y}
), we assume that there exists a matrix function
s(Y)

satisfying the following three properties:

Property 1a:

The rapidity dependence is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\,s(Y)

\displaystyle=s(Y)\mskip 1.5muK

(35)

with
K=K^{\dagger}
independent of
Y
.

Property 1b:

s(Y)
is nonsingular. It is sufficient to establish
this property at an arbitrary value
Y_{1}
; its validity at any other
Y

then follows from the solution of (
35
).

Property 1c:

One has

\displaystyle S(Y)

\displaystyle=s(Y-Y_{0})\,s^{\dagger}(Y_{0})

\displaystyle\text{for $Y\gg 1$ and arbitrary
$Y_{0}$}\,.

(36)

It is easy to see that
s(Y)
is not unique, since properties 1a – 1c
remain true if one redefines
s(Y)\to s(Y)\,U
and
K\to U^{\dagger}KU
with a
Y
independent unitary matrix
U
. Conversely,
if properties 1a – 1c hold for two matrix functions
s(Y)
and
s^{\prime}(Y)
,
one can show that
s^{\prime}(Y)=s(Y)\,U(Y)
with a unitary matrix
U
(which
is not necessarily
Y
independent).

In appendix
A
we will show that properties 1a – 1c
are equivalent to the following two properties:

Property 2a:

S
satisfies the Collins-Soper equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\,S(Y)

\displaystyle=\widehat{K}\mskip 1.5muS(Y)

\displaystyle\text{for $Y\gg 1$}\,.

(37)

\widehat{K}
is not necessarily Hermitian, and its relation with
K
is
specified in (
332
). Both (
36
) and (
37
)
are meant to be approximations for
Y\gg 1
. We can define a matrix

\widehat{S}(Y)
that approximates
S(Y)
for
Y\gg 1
whilst being an
exact solution of the differential equation (
37
) at all
Y
.
This equation is then solved by

\displaystyle\widehat{S}(Y)

\displaystyle=e^{Y\widehat{K}}\,\widehat{S}(0).

(38)

Property 2b:

There is a value
Y_{1}
for which
\widehat{S}(Y_{1})
is
positive definite.

We show in appendix
A
that
\widehat{S}(Y)
is then
in fact positive definite for all
Y
. This guarantees that the matrix

S(Y)
at large
Y
has an inverse, which according to
(
12
) is needed in the cross section formula.

Evidence for the properties just discussed comes from perturbation theory,
which can be used to compute the soft factor when the distances

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
are all small.

•

Properties 2a and 2b are easily checked at one-loop order from our
explicit calculations in all colour channels, which are reported in
section
7
. In this case one finds
\widehat{S}(0)=\mathbb{1}
and, taking
U=\mathbb{1}
one has
K=\widehat{K}
according to
(
332
).

•

Given property 2a, one can motivate property 2b using the
perturbative expansions
S(Y)=\mathbb{1}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
and

\widehat{K}=\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
. One may worry that the expansion
of
S(Y)
has poor convergence, because higher orders in
\alpha_{s}
can
come with higher powers of the large rapidity
Y
. However, according
to (
38
) we have
\widehat{S}(0)=\exp(-Y\widehat{K})\,S(Y)
for sufficiently large
Y
. If the perturbative
expansions of
S(Y)
and
\exp(-Y\widehat{K})
are valid at least in a
formal sense, we get an expansion
\widehat{S}(0)=\mathbb{1}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
that is free of any large parameter. The
eigenvalues of
\widehat{S}(0)
then have a perturbative expansion
around
1
, which supports property 2b with
Y_{1}=0
, at least for
sufficiently small
\alpha_{s}
.

•

S_{qq}
and
S_{q\bar{q}}
have been calculated at two-loop order
in
Vladimirov:2016qkd
. The validity of properties 1a and 1c can
be explicitly verified from the results in sections 4.2 and 4.4 of that
work. This requires a translation between the rapidity regulator used
there and the one used here, which we discuss in
appendix
B
. We note that
Vladimirov:2016qkd

defines
S=s^{\dagger}s
, whilst we choose the order
S=ss^{\dagger}

in (
36
).

Property 1b can then be motivated by the perturbative expansion of

s(Y)
, arguing along the same lines as in the previous point.

•

An all-order derivation of the rapidity evolution equation of the
DPS soft factor has recently been given in
Vladimirov:2017ksc
. Our
equation (
37
) can be obtained from equation (5.17) in the arXiv
version 2 of
Vladimirov:2017ksc
using the relation (
336
)
between the regulator variables
\delta^{+},\delta^{-}
and
Y_{L},Y_{R}
.
However, more work is needed to establish whether the derivation in

Vladimirov:2017ksc
holds if one uses either of the associated
rapidity regulators.

A few more comments are in order.

•

While
S(0)=\mathbb{1}
by construction (the Wilson line pairs at equal
positions give unit matrices,
W^{\dagger}W=\mathbb{1}
), one generally has

\widehat{S}(0)\neq\mathbb{1}
. This implies that the evolution equation
(
37
) holds for
Y\gg 1
but not when
Y
becomes small. We
see this already at one loop: as follows from sections 3.3.1 and 3.2.2
of
Diehl:2011yj
, one has
S-\mathbb{1}\propto Y\tanh(Y)
for the
one-loop soft factor, whilst (
37
) truncated to

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
gives
S-\mathbb{1}\propto Y
.

•

For the soft factor needed in collinear factorisation, i.e. for

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
, we will see in
section
4.4
that
S(Y)
is diagonal in the basis of
colour representations for all
Y
. As a consequence,
\widehat{K}
is
diagonal as well. In appendix
A
we show that one
can then also choose
s(Y)
and
K
to be diagonal. For the soft factor
in collinear factorisation, all matrix multiplications in colour space
thus become trivial.

•

The matrix
s
replaces the square roots of soft factors in the
construction of single parton TMDs by Collins
Collins:2011zzd
, as
we will show in section
3.5
.

3.3
Definition of DPDs

According to (
12
), the DPS cross section involves a
product of soft matrices, which using (
36
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle S^{-1}(v_{L},v_{R})\,S(v_{L},v_{R})\,S^{-1}(v_{L},v_{R})

\displaystyle=s^{\dagger-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{C})\,s^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,,

(39)

where
Y_{C}
is a central rapidity,
Y_{L}\ll Y_{C}\ll Y_{R}
. Restoring
parton labels, we then define DPDs by

\displaystyle F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}s_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L})\,,

\displaystyle F_{b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{R}\to\infty\phantom{-}}s_{b_{1}b_{2}}^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{C})\,F_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{R})

(40)

for the distributions in a right-moving and a left-moving proton,
respectively. An analogous definition in the
\delta
regulator scheme was
put forward in
Vladimirov:2016qkd
.

From the construction in appendix
A
it follows that
the symmetry relations (
25
) for
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
imply corresponding
relations

\displaystyle s_{qq}=

\displaystyle s_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle s_{q\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=s_{\bar{q}q}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle s_{qg}

\displaystyle=s_{\bar{q}g}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle s_{gg}

\displaystyle=s_{gg}^{*}

(41)

if the matrices
U_{a_{1}a_{2}}
in (
331
) are chosen such that they
satisfy the relations (
334
). In the expression
(
12
) for double Drell-Yan production we can thus rewrite

\displaystyle F^{T}_{\text{us},\bar{q}\bar{q}}(Y_{R})\,s_{qq}^{\dagger-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{C})\,s_{qq}^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,F_{\text{us},qq}(Y_{L})

\displaystyle=F^{T}_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}(Y_{C})\,F_{qq}(Y_{C})\,,

(42)

where we have omitted the hard-scattering factors
H_{i,q\bar{q}}
for
brevity. A corresponding argument leads to the combination

F^{T}_{\bar{q}q}(Y_{C})\,F_{q\bar{q}}(Y_{C})
in the same process. The
production of one Higgs and one electroweak gauge boson involves a term

\displaystyle F^{T}_{\text{us},\bar{q}g}(Y_{R})\,s_{qg}^{\dagger-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{C})\,s_{qg}^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,F_{\text{us},qg}(Y_{L})

\displaystyle=F^{T}_{\bar{q}g}(Y_{C})\,F_{qg}(Y_{C})

(43)

with mixed quark-gluon and antiquark-gluon DPDs, and likewise one obtains the
product
F^{T}_{gg}(Y_{C})\,F_{gg}(Y_{C})
in double Higgs boson production.

In (
3.3
) we can take the limit of infinite Wilson line
rapidities
Y_{L}
and
Y_{R}
in the unsubtracted matrix elements. This
means that the Wilson lines in the parton operators
\mathcal{O}_{a}
are
along lightlike paths, which leads to important simplifications as
discussed in chapter 10.11 of
Collins:2011zzd
. Let us see that
this limit is well behaved in (
3.3
). The rapidities
Y_{L}
and

Y_{R}
in the cross section formula originate from Grammer-Yennie
approximations (see e.g.
Diehl:2015bca
), and one finds that their
precise values in (
42
) and (
43
) do not matter
as long as
|Y_{L}|,|Y_{R}|\gg 1
. One can thus take the limit of infinite

Y_{L}
and
Y_{R}
in these expressions, and thus also in the individual
factors in (
3.3
). By contrast, taking infinite Wilson line
rapidities in individual unsubtracted DPDs or in the soft factor would
lead to rapidity divergences.

The dependence of the DPDs on the central rapidity
Y_{C}
is easily
obtained from (
35
), which gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\,s^{-1}(Y)

\displaystyle=-K\mskip 1.5mus^{-1}(Y)

(44)

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y_{C}}\mskip 1.5muF(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=-KF(Y_{C})

(45)

for DPDs in a right-moving proton, whilst for a left-moving proton one has
the opposite sign on the r.h.s. of (
45
).

The preceding construction can be performed for DTMDs and DPDFs alike.
Important differences between the two types of distributions arise at the
level of ultraviolet renormalisation. In the next subsection, we discuss
this for the case of DTMDs, postponing the case of DPDFs to
section
5.2
.

3.4
Renormalisation of DTMDs

The ultraviolet (UV) renormalisation of TMDs arises from vertex and self
energy corrections associated with composite operators that contain fields
at the same transverse position. Interactions between fields at different
transverse positions do not give rise to UV divergences: the finite
spacelike distance acts as an effective UV cutoff in the corresponding
graphs. Operators that require renormalisation are therefore
[W(\xi)\,q(\xi)]_{i}
,
[W(\xi)\,G^{+i}(\xi)]_{a}
and their Hermitian conjugates
in the collinear matrix elements, as well as
[W(\xi,v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}(\xi,v_{R})]_{ij}
,
[W(\xi,v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}(\xi,v_{R})]_{ab}

and their Hermitian conjugates in the soft factor. Due to colour SU(3)
invariance, the corresponding renormalisation factors are all colour
independent. We could in principle choose a different renormalisation
scale for each of the four parton operators in
F_{\text{us}}
and for each of
the four corresponding Wilson line products in the soft factor. We choose
a slightly simpler variant, taking common renormalisation scales
\mu_{1}

and
\mu_{2}
for the operators associated with partons carrying momentum
fraction
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, respectively. Denoting bare quantities with a
subscript
B
, we then have
O_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})=Z_{\text{us},a}\,O_{B,a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
, where
Z_{\text{us},a}
is the product of
renormalisation factors for the composite operators at transverse
positions
\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}
and
\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}
.
Likewise, for the operator defined in (
3.2
) we have

O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})=Z_{S,a}\,O_{BS,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
,
where
Z_{S,a}
is the product of renormalisation factors for the Wilson
line pairs at equal transverse positions.
2
2

2

Our convention for
renormalisation factors
Z
of composite operators corresponds to the one in

Collins:1984xc
;
Collins:2011zzd
. Other authors, such as the
ones of
Peskin:1995ev
, use
Z^{-1}
instead.

Both
Z_{\text{us},a}
and
Z_{S,a}
are independent of spin and colour, but
they differ for quarks and gluons (being equal for quarks and antiquarks
due to charge conjugation invariance). They depend on a scale
\mu
and
on Wilson line rapidities, and
Z_{\text{us},a}
also depends on the
plus-momentum
xp^{+}
of the relevant parton. We thus have

\displaystyle F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};\mu_{i},Y_{L})

\displaystyle=Z_{\text{us},a_{1}}(\mu_{1},Y_{L},x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})\,Z_{\text{us},a_{2}}(\mu_{2},Y_{L},x_{2}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})\,F_{B,\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};Y_{L})\,,

\displaystyle S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\mu_{i},Y)

\displaystyle=Z_{S,a_{1}}(\mu_{1},Y)\,Z_{S,a_{2}}(\mu_{2},Y)\,S_{B,a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

(46)

with
Y=Y_{R}-Y_{L}
. Throughout this work we use the convention that a
function with arguments
x_{i}
(
\mu_{i}
) depends on both
x_{1}
and
x_{2}

(
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
). In this and the next subsection, we consider a
right-moving proton for definiteness, and we omit transverse position
arguments for brevity.

We now derive the renormalisation properties of the matrix
s_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)
.
Assuming that the
Y
dependence of
S_{B}(Y)
is given by (
37
) with
a bare kernel
\widehat{K}_{B}
and defining

\displaystyle\Lambda_{a}(Y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\,\log Z_{S,a}(Y)\,,

(47)

we readily get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\,S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\widehat{K}_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)\,S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

(48)

with

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{a_{1}}(Y)\,\mathbb{1}+\Lambda_{a_{2}}(Y)\,\mathbb{1}+\widehat{K}_{B,a_{1}a_{2}}\,,

(49)

where we have dropped the dependence on the renormalisation scales for
brevity. The renormalised soft factor hence satisfies (
37
) if

\Lambda_{a}
is independent of
Y
. This is easily shown for the

MS
scheme. Expanding
Z_{S}
in the renormalised coupling, we have

Z_{S}=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{s}^{n}Z_{S}^{(n)}(\epsilon)
with
coefficients
Z_{S}^{(n)}
being a finite sum of poles in
\epsilon
for
each
n
. The corresponding coefficients in
\Lambda=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{s}^{n}\Lambda^{(n)}(\epsilon)
are hence pure pole terms as well.
Since
S
is finite for
\epsilon=0
, the same holds for
\widehat{K}

according to (
48
), so that the
\epsilon
poles of
\Lambda

must cancel those of
\widehat{K}_{B}
in (
49
). Since the
latter are
Y
independent, the same holds for
\Lambda
. In the

\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme one can repeat the previous argument after
rescaling
\alpha_{s}
in the expansion of
Z_{S}
by a factor

S_{\epsilon}
(see section
7.3.3
for further explanation).

With
\Lambda_{a}
being
Y
independent, the solution of (
47
)
reads
Z_{S,a}(Y)=Z_{S,a}(0)\,e^{Y\Lambda_{a}}
. Using (
331
) and
(
332
), one finds that

\displaystyle K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{a_{1}}(\mu_{1})\,\mathbb{1}+\Lambda_{a_{2}}(\mu_{2})\,\mathbb{1}+K_{B,a_{1}a_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle s_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\mu_{i},Y)

\displaystyle=Z_{s,a_{1}}(\mu_{1},Y)\,Z_{s,a_{2}}(\mu_{2},Y)\,s_{B,a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

(50)

with
Z_{s,a}(Y)=\sqrt{Z_{S,a}(0)}\,e^{Y\Lambda_{a}}
, where we have
restored the
\mu
dependence of the factors. We thus find that the
renormalisation of
s(Y)
is multiplicative and independent of the colour
channel, just as the one of
S(Y)
. With bare and renormalised DTMDs
defined as in (
3.3
), we then have

\displaystyle F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};\mu_{i},Y_{C})

\displaystyle=Z_{F,a_{1}}(\mu_{1},Y_{C},x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})\,Z_{F,a_{2}}(\mu_{2},Y_{C},x_{2}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})\,F_{B,a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};Y_{C})

(51)

with

\displaystyle Z_{F,a}(\mu,Y_{C},xp^{+})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}Z^{-1}_{s,a}(\mu,Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,Z_{\text{us},a}(\mu,Y_{L},xp^{+})

(52)

for a right-moving proton, and correspondingly for a left-moving one. Note
that, as explained in chapter 10.11 of
Collins:2011zzd
, one should
first take the limit of infinite
Y_{L}
in the product (
52
) and
in
F_{B}(Y_{C})
, and then let
\epsilon
go to zero on the r.h.s. of
(
51
).

We can now derive the
\mu
dependence of the DTMDs and of their
Collins-Soper kernel. Defining

\displaystyle\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,Y_{C},xp^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}\,\log Z_{F,a}(\mu,Y_{C},xp^{+})\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}\,\Lambda_{a}(\mu)\,,

(53)

we obtain for a right-moving proton

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};\mu_{i},Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{F,a_{1}}(\mu_{1},Y_{C},x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})\,F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i};\mu_{i},Y_{C},)\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\mu_{i})

\displaystyle={}-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu_{1})\,\mathbb{1}\,,

(54)

and analogous relations for the
\mu_{2}
dependence, as well as the
additional condition

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y_{C}}\,\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,Y_{C},xp^{+})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)\,.

(55)

We note that
Z_{F,a}
and hence
\gamma_{F,a}
can depend on
Y_{C}
and

xp^{+}
only via the boost invariant combination
xp^{+}e^{-Y_{C}}
. This will
be used to introduce the rapidity parameter
\zeta
later on (see
(
61
) and (
125
)).

It is easy to repeat the above arguments with four different renormalisation
scales for the four parton legs of
F
. One then obtains analogues of
(
3.4
) for each scale, with
\gamma_{K,a}
replaced by

\gamma_{K,a}/2
and
\gamma_{F,a}
by
\gamma_{E,a}
or
(\gamma_{E,a})^{*}
,
where
\gamma_{E,a}+(\gamma_{E,a})^{*}=\gamma_{F,a}
. The anomalous
dimension
\gamma_{E,a}
has an imaginary part and is for a parton momentum
leaving
F
, whereas
(\gamma_{E,a})^{*}
is for a parton momentum entering

F
. This can be seen in the study of the quark form factor

Collins:2017oxh
(see section 10.12.2 of
Collins:2011zzd
for an
explicit calculation at leading order).

3.5
Definition of single parton TMDs

It is instructive to revisit the definition of single parton TMDs in the
framework we have just laid out. The colour structure is considerably
simplified in this case. The operator
O_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z})
in the
matrix element for an unsubtracted TMD in (
3.1
) must carry colour
singlet quantum numbers, which is achieved by contracting the operators in
(
20
) with
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}
or
\delta_{aa^{\prime}}
.
Likewise, the soft factor for single hard scattering involves the colour
singlet projection of the operator
O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z})
defined in
(
3.2
). One can easily adapt the
arguments in the previous subsections to this case: the matrices
S
,

\widehat{S}
,
s
,
K
and
\widehat{K}
then become single real valued
functions. One finds
K=\widehat{K}
, whilst the definition (
331
)
simplifies to

\displaystyle s(Y)

\displaystyle=e^{YK}\,\widehat{S}^{\,1/2}(0)\,,

(56)

which is positive and satisfies (
35
). This gives

\displaystyle S(Y)=\widehat{S}(Y)=e^{YK}\,\widehat{S}(0)=s(Y-Y_{0})\,s(Y_{0})

(57)

for
Y\gg 1
and arbitrary
Y_{0}
.
The ambiguity in the choice of
s
, due to unitary transformations in the
matrix case, is no longer present. Combining the two previous equations, we
deduce that

\displaystyle s(Y)=\sqrt{S(2Y)}\,.

(58)

The square-root factor in the construction of single parton TMDs by Collins,
given in equation (13.106) of
Collins:2011zzd
, can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{S(Y_{R}-Y_{C})}{S(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\,S(Y_{C}-Y_{L})}}

\displaystyle=s^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})

(59)

using (
57
) and (
58
). The final TMD is
then given by

\displaystyle f_{a}(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}s_{a}^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,f_{\text{us},a}(Y_{L})\,,

(60)

where we have restored the parton label
a
to denote quarks, antiquarks
and gluons. This form closely resembles the definition in

Echevarria:2015byo
;
GarciaEchevarria:2011rb
; we will expand on this
further in appendix
B
. A related discussion has been
given in
Collins:2012uy
, but the separation into different factors
made there differs from ours.

The renormalisation of single parton TMDs is done with the same factors

Z_{\text{us},a}
,
Z_{S,a}
and
Z_{F,a}
as in section
3.4
, and
hence involves the same anomalous dimensions. Coming back to our remark after
(
55
) and defining

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=2\mskip 1.5mu(xp^{+})^{2}\,e^{-2Y_{C}}

(61)

for a right-moving proton, we recover the familiar evolution equations given in
(
3
) to (
8
).

4
Colour

An essential element in the description of DPS is the colour structure, which
is much more involved than in single hard scattering. To deal efficiently
with matrices in colour space, we introduce projection operators and make use
of the fact that certain colour indices must couple to an overall colour
singlet. We derive the combination (
85
) of colour
projected quantities that enters the DPS cross section in
section
4.2
and establish a number of symmetry relations in
section
4.3
. In sections
4.4
and

4.5
we consider the case of collinear factorisation.
Projector identities such as the one in figure
2
lead
to dramatic simplifications in this case, with soft matrices that are diagonal
and depend on only three independent functions as specified in
(
108
) to (
110
). These simplifications also
allow us to derive the structure (
4.5
) of the DPS cross section
for produced particles carrying colour. A reader who is not interested in
technical details may skip the following derivations and will find the
principal results in the equations just mentioned.

4.1
Projection operators

For the fundamental representation of the group SU(
N
), we introduce the
operators

\displaystyle P_{1}^{ii^{\prime}\,jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mskip 1.5mu\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{8}^{ii^{\prime}\,jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2\mskip 1.5mut^{a}_{ii^{\prime}}t^{a}_{jj^{\prime}}\,,

(62)

which project the index pairs
ii^{\prime}
and
jj^{\prime}
onto a colour singlet or a
colour octet, respectively.
3
3

3

We use the term “octet” to denote
the adjoint representation, which of course has dimension 8 only for SU(3).

In these and all following projection operators, the four indices are
coupled to an overall colour singlet. We take
T_{F}=1/2
throughout this
paper and do not distinguish between upper and lower colour indices. We
will make repeated use of the colour Fierz identity

\displaystyle 2\mskip 1.5mut^{a}_{ii^{\prime}}t^{a}_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}j}-\frac{1}{N}\mskip 1.5mu\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\,.

(63)

For the adjoint representation we use

Bartels:1993ih
;
Mekhfi:1985dv
;
Keppeler:2012ih

\displaystyle P_{1}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}-1}\mskip 1.5mu\delta^{aa^{\prime}}\delta^{bb^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{A}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mskip 1.5muf^{aa^{\prime}c}f^{bb^{\prime}c}\,,

\displaystyle P_{S}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{N^{2}-4}\mskip 1.5mud^{aa^{\prime}c}d^{bb^{\prime}c}\,,

\displaystyle P_{D}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\delta^{ab}\delta^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}-\delta^{ab^{\prime}}\delta^{a^{\prime}b}\bigr)-P_{A}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{27}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\delta^{ab}\delta^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}+\delta^{ab^{\prime}}\delta^{a^{\prime}b}\bigr)-P_{S}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}-P_{1}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}\,.

(64)

The subscript on
P
always denotes the colour representation onto which index
pairs
aa^{\prime}
and
bb^{\prime}
are projected, with
A
denoting the antisymmetric and

S
the symmetric octet.
4
4

4

For
N>3
a further representation appears
when decomposing the product of two octets (see table 9.4 in

Cvitanovic:2008zz
). This representation is sometimes labelled
as
R=0
, since it has dimension
0
for
N=3
. The projector
P_{27}
in
(
4.1
) corresponds to
P_{27}+P_{0}
in the case of
general
N

Keppeler:2012ih
.

Useful relations for calculating with the
f
and
d

tensors are given in
MacFarlane:1968vc
.

The operator
P_{D}=P_{10}+P_{\;\smash{\overline{10}}}
projects on the
sum of the decuplet and antidecuplet representations. For any tensor

M^{aa^{\prime}bb^{\prime}}
that transforms as an overall colour singlet, the projections
on decuplet and antidecuplet are equal (and can hence be added without loss
of information). Following appendix A of
Kasemets:2014yna
, one can
show this by first decomposing
M^{aa^{\prime}bb^{\prime}}
on
P_{R}^{ab\,a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}
with

R=1,A,S,10,\overline{10},27
and then projecting on
P_{R}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}
.
This result can be traced back to the fact that
(P_{10})^{\mskip 1.5muaa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}-(P_{\;\smash{\overline{10}}})^{\mskip 1.5muaa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}
is odd but all

P_{R}^{ab\,a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}
are even under the simultaneous exchange

(a\leftrightarrow b,a^{\prime}\leftrightarrow b^{\prime})
.

We will further need projectors for mixed fundamental and adjoint indices:

\displaystyle P_{1}^{ii^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=P_{1}^{aa^{\prime}\,ii^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(N^{2}-1)}}\,\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{aa^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{A}^{ii^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=P_{A}^{aa^{\prime}\,ii^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{N}}\,t^{c}_{ii^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5muf^{aa^{\prime}c}\,,

\displaystyle P_{S}^{ii^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=P_{S}^{aa^{\prime}\,ii^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{2N}{N^{2}-4}}\,t^{c}_{ii^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mud^{aa^{\prime}c}\,.

(65)

The normalisation factors are chosen such as to yield the simple symmetry and
projection properties to be discussed next.

To ease our notation, we introduce double indices
\underline{i}=(ii^{\prime})
,
\underline{a}=(aa^{\prime})
. Here and in the following,
\underline{i},\underline{j},\underline{k}
are in the
fundamental representation,
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
are in the adjoint,
whereas
\underline{r},\underline{s},\underline{t},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}
can belong to
either representation. Repeated double indices are summed over, so that for
adjoint indices we have

\displaystyle M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{a}}\,M_{2}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,aa^{\prime}}M_{2}^{aa^{\prime}\,\underline{s}}\,.

(66)

Some care is required for indices of the fundamental representation. In
fundamental Wilson lines
W
and in projection operators
P_{R}
, the first
index always transforms as a triplet and the second index as an antitriplet.
For such cases, we define matrix multiplication with a transposition

\displaystyle M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{i}}\,M_{2}^{\underline{i}\,\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,ii^{\prime}}M_{2}^{i^{\prime}\!i\,\underline{s}}\,.

(67)

In soft matrices
S
and in the matrix elements giving DPDs, the order of
triplet and antitriplet indices depends on the parton channel (
q
or

\bar{q}
) and in
S
also on the direction of the Wilson lines (along
v_{L}

or
v_{R}
). Contraction of double indices should always be done such that a
triplet index is contracted with an antitriplet one. This ensures proper
behaviour of the result under gauge transformations.

With these definitions, the projection operators have the symmetry

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}=P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{r}}

(68)

and the projection property

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{t}}

(69)

for any pair of projectors
P_{R},P_{R^{\prime}}
with a common double index
\underline{s}

in either the fundamental or the adjoint representation. In the octet sector,
it is understood that

\delta_{8A}=\delta_{A8}=\delta_{8S}=\delta_{S8}=1
but

\delta_{AS}=\delta_{SA}=0
in (
69
). The normalisation is
given by

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,m(R)\,,

(70)

where

\displaystyle m(R)=P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{r}}

(71)

is the multiplicity of the representation, i.e.
m(1)=1
,
m(8)=m(A)=m(S)=8
,
m(D)=20
and
m(27)=27
for SU(3). Since the projectors
above form a basis of the space of rank-four tensors
M
that transform as an
SU(3) singlet, any such tensor
M
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle M^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}\frac{1}{m(R)}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,\bigl(P_{R}^{\underline{t}\,\underline{u}}M^{\underline{t}\,\underline{u}}_{\phantom{1}}\bigr)\,,

(72)

where the sum is over all representations
R
given in (
4.1
),
(
4.1
) and (
4.1
), as applicable. The contraction of
two colour singlet tensors is then given by

\displaystyle M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,M_{2}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}\frac{1}{m(R)}\,\bigl(P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}M_{1}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\bigr)\,\bigr(P_{R}^{\underline{t}\,\underline{u}}M_{2}^{\underline{t}\,\underline{u}}\bigr)\,.

(73)

For representation labels
R
, we will
not
use the summation
convention, i.e. summation over
R
will always be indicated explicitly.

4.2
Colour structure of the DPS cross section

The projection operators just introduced allow us to rewrite the DPS
cross section in a compact way. We start by defining DPDs that are
projected on a definite colour representation
R
. Using the general
decomposition in (
72
), we can write

\displaystyle F_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)}\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{a_{2}}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{m(R)}}\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}

(74)

with

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)\;\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{a_{2}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{m(R)}}\;P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}F_{a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{R}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}\!\!.

(75)

Note that the lower indices
a_{1},a_{2}
denote the parton species and
polarisation, not colour. The factors

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a}(R)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mskip 1.5mui&\text{if $a$ is a gluon and $R=A$}\\
\mskip 1.5mu1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(76)

ensure that the collinear distributions
{}^{A}\mskip-1.5muF_{qg}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
and

{}^{A}\mskip-1.5muF_{\bar{q}g}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
and their polarised counterparts are
real valued (rather than imaginary), as are the collinear distributions in
all other channels. This is shown in the next subsection. The
prefactors
\mathcal{N}_{a}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{q}=\mathcal{N}_{\bar{q}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{g}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N^{2}-1}

(77)

and likewise for polarised partons. They ensure that colour singlet
distributions
{}^{1}F_{a_{1}a_{2}}
involve a
sum
over the colours of
the two partons.
The definitions here coincide with the ones in

Diehl:2011yj
;
Kasemets:2014yna
, with the notational change

\displaystyle{}^{D}\mskip-1.5muF_{gg}\mskip 1.5mu\big|_{\text{here}}

\displaystyle={}^{10+\overline{10}}\mskip-1.5muF_{gg}\mskip 1.5mu\big|_{\text{Ref.\penalty\ \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Kasemets:2014yna}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl({}^{10}\mskip-1.5muF_{gg}+{}^{\overline{10}}\mskip-1.5muF_{gg}\bigr)\mskip 1.5mu\big|_{\text{Ref.\penalty\ \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Diehl:2011yj}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}}

(78)

and its analogues for polarised gluons.
For the discussion of renormalisation and the short-distance expansion, it is
useful to project the partonic operators (
21
) on definite colour
representations as well. Introducing

\displaystyle{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a\rule{0.0pt}{3.46594pt}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{a}(R)\;\mathcal{N}_{a}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}\underline{s}}\,O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{s}}_{a\rule{0.0pt}{3.46594pt}}

(79)

and defining unsubtracted DPDs
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}
in analogy to
(
75
), we obtain

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{+}-p^{\prime+})\,2p^{+}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m(R)}}\,\big\langle p^{\prime}\big|\,{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a_{1}}\;{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a_{2}}\,\big|p\big\rangle

(80)

from (
3.1
) and the projection property (
69
).

The soft factor for DPS producing colour singlet particles in the hard
interactions carries two times four indices. Its projection on different
representations is defined by

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)}{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R^{\prime})\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R^{\prime})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m(R)\,m(R^{\prime})}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,S_{a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{R}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}\,.

(81)

R
specifies the colour representation for the Wilson lines with positive
rapidity, and
R^{\prime}
the one for the Wilson lines with negative rapidity.
Notice that the
\varepsilon
factors cancel for
R=R^{\prime}
.
In analogy to (
81
) we define projections
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
and

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
of the matrix
s
and of the Collins-Soper kernel
K
, as
well as projections
{}^{RR^{\prime}}(S^{-1})
and
{}^{RR^{\prime}}(s^{-1})
of the
inverse matrices
S^{-1}
and
s^{-1}
in colour space.
Using the same argument as in (
73
), one can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5mu(S_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{-1})\;{}^{R^{\prime}R^{\prime\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}=\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5mu(s_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{-1})\;{}^{R^{\prime}R^{\prime\prime}}\!s_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime\prime}}\,.

(82)

We see that matrix multiplication in the space of four colour indices

(\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2})=(r_{1}r_{1}^{\prime}r_{2}r_{2}^{\prime})
turns into matrix
multiplication in the space of colour representations
R
. The matrix
decomposition (
36
) can thus be rewritten as

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime\prime}}\mskip-1.5muS_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5mus_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y-Y_{0})\;{}^{R^{\prime}R^{\prime\prime}}\!\bigl(s_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\dagger}(Y_{0})\bigr)\,,

(83)

and the definition (
3.3
) of DPDs in a right-moving proton as

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5mu\bigl(s_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\bigr)\;{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L}).

(84)

According to (
12
) and (
42
), the cross
section for the production of colour neutral particles involves the
product

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=H_{a_{1}b_{1}}\,H_{a_{2}b_{2}}\,F_{b_{1}b_{2}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,F_{a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{b}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}=\frac{H_{a_{1}b_{1}}}{\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{1}}}\,\frac{H_{a_{2}b_{2}}}{\mathcal{N}_{a_{2}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{2}}}\,\sum_{R}\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}\,.

(85)

Here we have combined the factors
\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b_{2}}(R)
into

\displaystyle\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon^{2}_{a_{2}}(R)}\,,

(86)

using that the only channels that produce colourless particles are
q\bar{q}

and
gg
annihilation.
Multiplied with a flux factor, the combinations

H_{ab}/(\mathcal{N}_{a}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b})
turn into the hard-scattering
cross sections in the final factorisation formula. The factor

\mathcal{N}_{a}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b}
is
N
for
q\bar{q}
annihilation and

N^{2}-1
for
gg
annihilation and ensures that one takes an
average

over the colour states of the two colliding partons, as is appropriate for a
parton-level cross section.

4.3
Symmetry properties

As already remarked, multiplication of matrices and vectors,
S
and
F
, in
the space of colour indices
(\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2})
turns into multiplication of
matrices and vectors in the space of representations
R
. For brevity, we
refer to the former as “colour space” and to the latter as “representation
space”. Note that our representation space only corresponds to the
part of colour space in which the indices

(\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2})
are coupled to an overall colour singlet. We now derive
some important properties of
S
and of the related matrices
s
and
K
in
representation space.

In (
3
) we have seen that the matrix
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
is Hermitian
in colour space,

\displaystyle S_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigl(S_{a_{1}a_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\bigr)^{*}\,.

(87)

Under Hermitian conjugation, an index pair
\underline{i}=(i,i^{\prime})
in
S
changes
its transformation properties: if
i
transforms as an SU(3) triplet in
S
,
it transforms as an antitriplet in
S^{\dagger}
and vice versa. The colour
projectors satisfy
P_{R}^{ii^{\prime}\,jj^{\prime}}=(P_{R}^{i^{\prime}\!i\,j^{\prime}\!j})^{*}
,

P_{R}^{ii^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}=(P_{R}^{i^{\prime}\!i\,aa^{\prime}})^{*}
and

P_{R}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}}=(P_{R}^{aa^{\prime}\,bb^{\prime}})^{*}
, so that the rule for contraction
with the colour projectors stated below (
67
) is satisfied if

S
is contracted with
P_{R}
and
S^{\dagger}
with
P_{R}^{*}
. We thus find
that

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left({}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5muS_{a_{1}a_{2}}\right)^{*}\,,

(88)

i.e.
S
is also Hermitian in representation space. In analogy, the fact
that the Collins-Soper kernel
K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
is Hermitian in colour space
implies that it is Hermitian in representation space as well.

We now turn to the relations between soft factors for quarks and antiquarks.
The relations (
25
) imply that

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R^{\prime})}{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)}\,\left({}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{\bar{a}_{1}\bar{a}_{2}}\right)^{*}=\frac{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R^{\prime})}{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)}\;{}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5muS_{\bar{a}_{1}\bar{a}_{2}}\,,

(89)

where
\bar{a}
denotes the antiparton of
a
(with the convention

\bar{g}=g
). The complex conjugation of projection operators implicit in
the first step corresponds to the fact that an index
i
transforming as a
triplet in
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
transforms as an antitriplet in

S_{\bar{a}_{1}\bar{a}_{2}}
and vice versa. Complex conjugation of the

\varepsilon
factors in the definition (
81
) gives the factors

\eta
in (
89
). In the second step we have simply used the property
(
88
).
We now recall that the relations (
25
) for
S
translate into
analogous relations (
41
) for
s
. By taking the rapidity
derivative, we find corresponding relations for
K
as well. In analogy to
(
89
) we can thus derive

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R^{\prime})}{\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)}\;{}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5muK_{\bar{a}_{1}\bar{a}_{2}}\,.

(90)

We will use this in section
5.1
when making rapidity evolution
explicit in the DPS cross section.
A different way to connect soft factors for quarks and antiquarks is to note
that the operator
O_{S,q}
in (
3.2
) is related with its analogue

O_{S,\bar{q}}
for antiquarks by

\displaystyle\bigl[O_{S,q}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{ii^{\prime},ll^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bigl[O_{S,\bar{q}}(\boldsymbol{y},-\boldsymbol{z};v_{L},v_{R})\bigr]^{i^{\prime}\!i,\,l^{\prime}\mskip-1.5mul}\,.

(91)

Multiplying with colour projectors according to the rule stated below
(
67
), one obtains

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{q\bar{q}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},-\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{\bar{q}q}(-\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}(-\boldsymbol{z}_{1},-\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})\,,

(92)

where the arguments
v_{L},v_{R}
are the same in all four expressions. Relations
analogous to (
4.3
) hold for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
, provided that

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muU_{a_{1}a_{2}}
satisfies the requirements (
A
).

Let us finally investigate the consequences of charge conjugation invariance.
Charge conjugation transforms

t^{a}A^{\mu a}(\xi)\to-(t^{a})^{*}A^{\mu a}(\xi)
, which can be derived from
the familiar transformation properties of quark fields and charge conjugation
invariance of the interaction term

-g\mskip 1.5mu\bar{q}\mskip 1.5mut^{a}\mskip-1.5muA^{\mu a}\gamma_{\mu}\mskip 1.5muq
in the QCD Lagrangian. As
a result, a fundamental Wilson line transforms as

\displaystyle W_{ij}\to W_{ij}^{*}\,.

(93)

For soft factors
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S
involving gluons, we rewrite all adjoint
Wilson lines as
Bomhof:2006dp

\displaystyle W^{ab}=2\operatorname{tr}(t^{a}Wt^{b}W^{\dagger})\,,

(94)

where the Wilson lines on the r.h.s. are in the fundamental representation,
express
f^{abc}
and
d^{abc}
as traces of products of matrices
t^{a}
,

t^{b}
,
t^{c}
and then eliminate all these matrices by repeated use of the
colour Fierz identity (
63
). After these steps, we have for
all parton combinations
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
a representation of

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
in terms of traces of fundamental Wilson lines
W

and
W^{\dagger}
, where thanks to the
\epsilon
factors in the definition
(
81
) no explicit factors of
i
appear. The transformation
(
93
) then readily implies that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
is real
valued, and with (
88
) we furthermore obtain that it is symmetric in
representation space, i.e. we have
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S={}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5muS
, where for
brevity we omit the subscripts
a_{1}a_{2}
.

To show that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
and
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
are also real valued, we
translate the derivation starting in (
327
) into representation
space, projecting all relevant matrices as specified in (
81
).
It is then easy to see that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\widehat{S}
and

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\widehat{K}
are real valued. To establish that

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!(\widehat{S}^{\,1/2})
is real valued, we note that

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\widehat{S}
has positive eigenvalues, which is readily seen by
projecting the eigenvalue equation
\widehat{S}v=\lambda v
from colour
space to representation space and recalling that in colour space

\widehat{S}
is a positive matrix. According to the discussion below
(
330
), the square root of a positive real matrix is real.
Choosing the matrix
U
in (
