331
) and (
332
) such that

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muU
is real, we find that both
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
and
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K

are real. Since
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
is Hermitian, it is also symmetric:

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K={}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5muK
.

Combining the symmetry relations (
89
) and (
4.3
) with the
fact that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S
is symmetric in
R
and
R^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{qq}(-\boldsymbol{z}_{1},-\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{y})\,.

(95)

An analogous relation holds for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{qq}
.

The quark and gluon operators in (
14
) and
(
17
) satisfy
\bigl[\mathcal{O}_{a}^{rr^{\prime}}\bigr]^{\dagger}(y,z)=\mathcal{O}_{a}^{r^{\prime}\!r}(y,-z)
, where the colour index
r
pertains to the
fields at
y+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z
and
r^{\prime}
to the fields at
y-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}z
. For colour
projected unsubtracted DPDFs this yields the relation

\displaystyle\bigl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{*}

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,.

(96)

Let us explicitly show this for
{}^{A}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},qg}
. Using that

\varepsilon_{q}(A)\mskip 1.5mu\varepsilon_{g}(A)=i
and

(P_{A}^{jj^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}})^{*}=-P_{A}^{j^{\prime}\!j\;a^{\prime}\!a}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl[{}^{A}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},qg}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad=c\int\!dz^{-}_{1}\,dz^{-}_{2}\,dy^{-}\,\Bigl[\mskip 1.5mui\mskip 1.5muP_{A}^{jj^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}e^{i\mskip 1.5mu(x_{1}z_{1}^{-}+x_{2}z_{2}^{-})\mskip 1.5mup^{+}}\,\langle p|\,\mathcal{O}^{jj^{\prime}}_{q}(y,z_{1})\,\mathcal{O}^{aa^{\prime}}_{g}(0,z_{2})\,|p\rangle\Bigr]^{*}

\displaystyle\qquad=c\int\!dz^{-}_{1}\,dz^{-}_{2}\,dy^{-}\,i\mskip 1.5muP_{A}^{j^{\prime}\!j\;a^{\prime}\!a}\,e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(x_{1}z_{1}^{-}+x_{2}z_{2}^{-})\mskip 1.5mup^{+}}\,\langle p|\,\mathcal{O}^{j^{\prime}\!j}_{q}(y,-z_{1})\,\mathcal{O}^{a^{\prime}\!a}_{g}(0,-z_{2})\,|p\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad={}^{A}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},qg}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,,\phantom{\int}

(97)

where in the last step we used that
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Here
c=c^{*}
is a product of kinematic and numerical factors not essential
for the argument. We see that the factor
\varepsilon_{g}(A)=i
compensates the
behaviour of
P_{A}^{jj^{\prime}\,aa^{\prime}}
under complex conjugation. One easily checks
that

\bigl[\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\mskip 1.5mu\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)\,P_{R}^{rr^{\prime}\,ss^{\prime}}\bigr]^{*}=\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\mskip 1.5mu\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)\,P_{R}^{r^{\prime}\mskip-1.5mur\;s^{\prime}\!s}
for all projectors, which ensures the
general validity of (
96
). Given that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
is real
valued, we finally find that

\displaystyle\Bigl[\mskip 1.5mu{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\mskip 1.5mu\Bigr]^{*}

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,.

(98)

In all colour channels and for all parton combinations, DPDFs are thus real
valued.

4.4
Simplification of soft factors in collinear factorisation

In the soft factors relevant for collinear factorisation, one should set

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
in the definitions
(
3.2
) and (
3.2
). Corresponding Wilson lines in
the scattering amplitude and its complex conjugate are then at the same
transverse position. This simplifies the colour structure significantly,
as we will now demonstrate. In the remainder of this subsection, it is
understood that all soft factors are taken at
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Figure 2:
Graphical representation of the
relation (
99
). The Wilson lines are understood to
be interacting, i.e. any number of gluons may be attached to them in a
Feynman graph.

The projection operators on Wilson lines obey the relation

\displaystyle W_{ij}\,P_{R}^{jj^{\prime},k^{\prime}k}\,W^{\dagger}_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=W_{jk}\,P_{R}^{ii^{\prime},j^{\prime}j}\,W^{\dagger}_{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}

(99)

for
R=1,8
, where it is understood that the Wilson lines
W
and

W^{\dagger}
are in the same direction and at the same position. For
R=1

this is trivial, and for
R=8
it readily follows from the colour Fierz
identity (
63
). A graphical representation of
(
99
) is shown in figure
2
.
Remarkably, an analogous relation holds also for adjoint Wilson lines

\displaystyle W_{ab}\,P_{R}^{bb^{\prime},cc^{\prime}}\,W^{\dagger}_{b^{\prime}a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=W_{bc}\,P_{R}^{aa^{\prime},bb^{\prime}}\,W^{\dagger}_{c^{\prime}b^{\prime}}

(100)

and for the mixed case

\displaystyle W_{ab}\,P_{R}^{bb^{\prime},k^{\prime}k}\,W^{\dagger}_{b^{\prime}a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=W_{jk}\,P_{R}^{aa^{\prime},j^{\prime}j}\,W^{\dagger}_{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}

(101)

in all relevant representations
R
. This can be shown by following the steps
in and after (
94
). For the extended soft factor, the
relations (
99
), (
100
) and (
101
) imply

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\;(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\cdots\,\underline{r}\,\cdots}_{\cdots\,\underline{s}\,\cdots}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\;(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\cdots\,\underline{s}\,\cdots}_{\cdots\,\underline{t}\,\cdots}\,,

(102)

where we have displayed only one of the four double index pairs

(\underline{r}_{i},\underline{s}_{i})
in

(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{r}_{3}\underline{r}_{4}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{s}_{3}\underline{s}_{4}}
for better readability, the other three pairs remaining
untouched.
Instead of making a colour projection for the fields at
\xi^{+}=\xi^{-}=0
,
one can thus make the same projection for the fields at infinity. This
yields an important relation between a specific projection of the extended
soft factor in (
3.2
) and the basic one in (
3.2
).
With (
102
) and the projection property (
69
) we have

\displaystyle P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}

\displaystyle=P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{v}_{1}}\,P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{t}_{1}\underline{v}_{1}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{v}_{2}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{t}_{2}\underline{v}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}

\displaystyle=P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{s}_{1}}\,P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{r}_{2}\underline{s}_{2}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{u}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}_{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2},\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2}}\,,

(103)

where
\underline{r},\underline{s},\ldots,\underline{v}
can each be in the fundamental or
adjoint representation. Projecting this on the remaining open indices, we
obtain

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}\,P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR_{1}}\,\delta_{RR_{2}}\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}_{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2},\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR_{1}}\,\delta_{RR_{2}}\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,m(R)\,{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS_{a_{1}a_{2}}\,,

(104)

where in the last step we have used the definition (
81
).
We furthermore have

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}\,\frac{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R^{\prime})\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R^{\prime})}{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)}\,\sqrt{m(R)m(R^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{2}\underline{s}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{t}_{2}\underline{s}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{1},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{1},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}\,,

(105)

which shows that at
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
the soft matrix is
diagonal in the colour representations
R
and
R^{\prime}
. Note that this is not
the case for nonzero
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
, as follows immediately from the
explicit one-loop expressions in section
7.2
.

The soft factors discussed so far correspond to the same parton species
entering a hard scattering process in the amplitude and its conjugate. As
already mentioned at the end of section
3.1
, there is also
interference between
q
and
\bar{q}
, between
q
and
g
, and between

\bar{q}
and
g
. The corresponding Wilson line products in the soft
factor are then
W_{ij}W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
,
W_{ij}W_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}
, etc. The relevant
projection operators for coupling colour indices in these cases are given
in equations (9) and (11) of
Mekhfi:1985dv
. Relations analogous to
(
99
) can be easily derived for these cases, where again the
colour projections for the gluon fields at
\xi^{+}=\xi^{-}=0
in the
Wilson lines can be traded for the colour projections of the fields at
infinity. From this, relations analogous to (
4.4
) and
(
4.4
) can be derived for the soft factors associated
with interference between parton species in the hard scattering.

We now relate soft factors in the adjoint representation with those in the
fundamental one. For the mixed quark-gluon soft factor we have

\displaystyle m(R)\,{}^{RR}\!S_{qg}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{a}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}\,(S_{qg})^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{a}_{2},\underline{k}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}_{\underline{j}_{1}\underline{b}_{2},\underline{j}_{1}\underline{b}_{2}}=P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{2}\underline{b}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{2}\underline{b}_{2}}\,(S_{qq})^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{i}_{1},\underline{k}_{1}\underline{k}_{1}}_{\underline{j}_{1}\underline{i}_{2},\underline{j}_{1}\underline{k}_{2}}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{2}\underline{j}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{2}\underline{j}_{2}}\,(S_{qq})^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{i}_{1},\underline{k}_{1}\underline{k}_{1}}_{\underline{j}_{1}\underline{i}_{2},\underline{j}_{1}\underline{k}_{2}}=P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{i}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{1}\underline{k}_{2}}\,(S_{qq})^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{i}_{2},\underline{k}_{1}\underline{k}_{2}}_{\underline{j}_{1}\underline{j}_{2},\underline{j}_{1}\underline{j}_{2}}

\displaystyle=m(R)\,{}^{RR}\!S_{qq}

(106)

if
R
is in the singlet or octet channels, where in the second and the forth
step we have used (
102
) for the indices associated with parton

a_{2}
in
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
. Writing

\displaystyle m(R)\,{}^{RR}\!S_{gg}\

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{a}_{1}\underline{a}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{c}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}\,(S_{gg})^{\underline{a}_{1}\underline{a}_{2},\underline{c}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}_{\underline{b}_{1}\underline{b}_{2},\underline{b}_{1}\underline{b}_{2}}

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{a}_{1}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{i}_{1}\underline{a}_{2}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{1}\underline{c}_{1}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{k}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}\,(S_{gg})^{\underline{a}_{1}\underline{a}_{2},\underline{c}_{1}\underline{c}_{2}}_{\underline{b}_{1}\underline{b}_{2},\underline{b}_{1}\underline{b}_{2}}

(107)

for
R=1,A,S
and using (
102
)
for all index pairs, one can reduce
S_{gg}
to
S_{qq}
in the colour
singlet and octet channels as well. The only independent soft matrix
elements for collinear DPS factorisation (in channels with the same parton
species in the amplitude and its conjugate) are therefore

\displaystyle{}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS

\displaystyle={}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS_{qq}={}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS_{qg}={}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS_{gq}={}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS_{gg}=1

(108)

and

\displaystyle{}^{88}\mskip-1.5muS

\displaystyle={}^{88}\mskip-1.5muS_{qq}={}^{AA}\mskip-1.5muS_{qg}={}^{SS}\mskip-1.5muS_{qg}={}^{AA}\mskip-1.5muS_{gq}={}^{SS}\mskip-1.5muS_{gq}={}^{AA}\mskip-1.5muS_{gg}={}^{SS}\mskip-1.5muS_{gg}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{DD}\mskip-1.5muS

\displaystyle={}^{DD}\mskip-1.5muS_{gg}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{27\,27}\mskip-1.5muS

\displaystyle={}^{27\,27}\mskip-1.5muS_{gg}\,,

(109)

where one can also replace one or both labels
q
with
\bar{q}
in the
soft factors involving quarks. We recall that each factor in
(
4.4
) depends on
\boldsymbol{y}
, on a rapidity difference, and
on two renormalisation scales
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. In the colour singlet
channel the soft matrix elements are unity because all Wilson lines are
contracted pairwise to
WW^{\dagger}=W^{\dagger}W=\mathbb{1}
.

As noted in
Vladimirov:2016qkd
, there is a remarkable identity

\displaystyle{}^{88}\mskip-1.5muS(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=S_{g}(\boldsymbol{y})\,,

(110)

where
S_{g}
is the soft factor for single TMD factorisation with a gluon
initiated hard scattering such as
gg\to H

Echevarria:2015uaa
. Its
definition reads

\displaystyle S_{g}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle 0\mskip 1.5mu\big|\mskip 1.5muP_{1}^{\underline{a}\,\underline{b}}\,\bigl[O_{S,g}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z})\bigr]^{\underline{a},\underline{b}}\mskip 1.5mu\big|\mskip 1.5mu0\bigr\rangle\,,

(111)

where
O_{S,g}
is obtained from (
3.2
) by replacing the fundamental
Wilson lines with adjoint ones. One can easily prove (
110
) by
applying the colour Fierz identity (
63
) to
{}^{88}\mskip-1.5muS
in
the quark representation and to
S_{g}
after converting adjoint Wilson lines
into fundamental ones using (
94
). It is understood that
the two renormalisation scales
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
in
{}^{88}\mskip-1.5muS(\boldsymbol{y})
must
be taken equal in (
110
).

4.5
Interlude: collinear factorisation for coloured particle production

Figure 3:
Graphical representation of colour index
contractions in the factorised double dijet cross section. The indices
in this figure correspond to the ones given in
(
4.5
). The corresponding primed indices in
the complex conjugate part of the graph are not shown.

In collinear factorisation, we may consider the production of coloured
particles, a prominent example for DPS being double dijet production. As
pointed out in section 2.1 of
Diehl:2015bca
, the steps for showing
factorisation in this case lead to the extended soft factor
(
3.2
). Instead of the product in (
12
),
the cross section then involves products of the form

\displaystyle X=H_{a_{1}b_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,H_{a_{2}b_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,

\displaystyle\bigl[F^{T}_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{R})\,S^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\bigr]^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,

\displaystyle\times\bigl[S(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\bigr]^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}\,\bigr[S^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\,F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L})\bigr]^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}\,.

(112)

A pictorial representation is given in figure
3
.
Colour indices
s,t
in the hard scattering factor
H^{ss^{\prime}tt^{\prime}}
are for
the incoming partons in the amplitude, whilst
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
are for the incoming
partons in the conjugate amplitude. These indices are contracted with colour
indices of the extended soft factor. The inverse soft factors
S^{-1}
in
(
4.5
) remove soft gluons from the unsubtracted collinear
factors
F_{\text{us}}
as usual; they are hence the soft factors with left and
right moving Wilson lines colour contracted as in (
3.2
). We
have omitted their parton labels, given that after colour projection
they are independent of the parton species as shown in the previous
subsection. In the extended soft factor, the representation of each Wilson
line must match the parton entering in the corresponding hard-scattering
subprocess. This includes combinations such as

W_{ab}(\boldsymbol{\xi},v_{L})\,W^{\dagger}_{kl}(\boldsymbol{\xi},v_{R})
, which
corresponds to a right-moving quark scattering on a left-moving gluon. It is
not difficult to generalise the relation (
4.4
) between the
extended and usual soft factors to such cases, so that we can omit parton
labels in the extended soft factor as well. Inserting projection operators as
in (
73
), we can thus rewrite (
4.5
) as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\sum_{R,R^{\prime},R_{1},R_{2}}\frac{P_{R_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,H_{a_{1}b_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,P_{R_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,H_{a_{2}b_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}}{m(R)\mskip 1.5mum(R^{\prime})\mskip 1.5mum(R_{1})\mskip 1.5mum(R_{2})}\;P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,\bigl[F^{T}_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{R})\,S^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\bigr]^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\delta_{RR_{1}}\,\delta_{RR_{2}}\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,m(R)\,{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\;P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}\,\bigr[S^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\,F_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L})\bigr]^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,H_{a_{1}b_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,H_{a_{2}b_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}}{m^{3}(R)}\;P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,F^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{R})\;{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{L})\,P_{R}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}\,F^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}(Y_{L})\,,

(113)

where in the last step we used that
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S^{-1}
is diagonal in

R
and
R^{\prime}
. Splitting
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS
as in (
83
) and using
that
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus
is real valued, we obtain colour projected DTMDs from
(
84
) and the analogue of (
75
) for
F_{\text{us}}
.
This yields the main result of this subsection:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{a_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b_{1}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{a_{2}}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b_{2}}(R)}\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{1}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{a_{2}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{2}}}\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,H_{a_{1}b_{1}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}\,H_{a_{2}b_{2}}^{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}}{m^{2}(R)}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(Y_{R})\,{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus^{-1}(Y_{R}-Y_{C})\,{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L})

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muH_{a_{1}b_{1}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muH_{a_{2}b_{2}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}\,.

(114)

The colour structure of the collinear cross section is thus surprisingly
simple: we have the same DPDs in (
4.5
) as in the formula
(
85
) for colour singlet production, and the only new
ingredients are the colour projected hard-scattering factors
5
5

5

The
factors
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muH
in (
115
) differ from the ones in
equation (2.11) of
Diehl:2015bca
. Here we project index
pairs in the
t
-channel (
s,s^{\prime}
etc.) onto a definite colour
representation
R
, whereas in
Diehl:2015bca
this was done
for index pairs in the
s
-channel (
s,t
etc.).

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muH_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{a}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b}(R)}\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{a}\,\mathcal{N}_{b}}\,\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\,H_{ab}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}}{m(R)}\,.

(115)

For the production of a colour singlet system, the hard scattering
subprocess has the structure

\displaystyle H_{ab}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{st}\,\delta_{s^{\prime}t^{\prime}}\,H_{ab}\,.

(116)

Using (
71
) we thus find

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\,H_{ab}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}

\displaystyle=m(R)\,H_{ab}\,,

(117)

so that
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muH_{ab}
reproduces
H_{ab}/(\mathcal{N}_{a}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b})
divided by
\varepsilon_{a}(R)\,\varepsilon_{b}(R)
, which is absorbed into
the sign factor
\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)
in (
85
).

5
Factorisation at small transverse momenta

We are now in a situation to give the TMD factorisation formula for DPS
processes.
Let us consider the production of two particles or groups of particles with
transverse momenta
\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
and invariant masses
Q_{1},Q_{2}
.
Both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are required to be large, and we denote their generic
size by
Q
for the purpose of power counting. Both transverse momenta, whose
generic size we denote by
q_{T}
, are required to be small compared with
Q
.
Up to power corrections in
\Lambda/Q
and
|\boldsymbol{q}_{i}|/Q
, the DPS cross
section with measured transverse momenta reads

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma_{\text{DPS}}}{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,d\bar{x}_{1}\,d\bar{x}_{2}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}=\frac{1}{C}\,\sum_{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}}\!\!\!\hat{\sigma}_{a_{1}b_{1}}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\,\hat{\sigma}_{a_{2}b_{2}}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}\;e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-i\boldsymbol{q}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}}\,W_{a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i},x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\nu)\,,

(118)

with

\displaystyle W_{a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i},x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\nu)=\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\,\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{R}\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\bar{\zeta})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

(119)

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}=\boldsymbol{y}\pm{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\,.

(120)

We recall that we write arguments
x_{i}
if a function depends on both

x_{1},x_{2}
, and likewise for all other variables. The combinatorial
factor
C
is equal to
2
if the systems produced by the two hard
scatters are identical and equal to
1
otherwise. The factor

\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)
was defined in (
86
); it is equal to
-1

if
R=A
and exactly one of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
is a gluon and equal to
1

otherwise. The subprocess cross sections are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{a_{1}b_{1}}(Q_{1}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2Q_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{H_{a_{1}b_{1}}(Q_{1}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})}{\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{1}}}\,

(121)

and likewise for index
2
. Here,
1/(2Q_{1}^{2})
is the standard flux
factor, and
1/(\mathcal{N}_{a_{1}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{N}_{b_{1}})
implements colour
averaging over the initial state as noted after (
85
).
The sum over
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}
in (
5
) runs over both parton
species and polarisations, following the notation specified in
section
3.1
. Hard-scattering cross sections and DPDs
carry transverse Lorentz indices for transverse quark and linear gluon
polarisation, which must be contracted appropriately. For later
discussion, it is useful to rewrite the integration measure in
(
5
) as

\displaystyle d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\;e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-i\boldsymbol{q}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}}

\displaystyle=d^{2}\boldsymbol{Z}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{+}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\;e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{Z}-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2})(\boldsymbol{y}_{+}-\boldsymbol{y}_{-})/2}

(122)

with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\,.

(123)

The function
\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm})
in (
5
) regulates the
ultraviolet region. It is intimately related with the cross talk between
double and single hard scattering and was introduced in the recent work

Diehl:2017kgu
. This is discussed in section
5.3
,
where we show how to combine the contributions from DPS, SPS and their
interference to obtain the physical cross section. Apart from this function,
the factorisation formula (
5
) is identical to the form that
emerged from the analysis of lowest-order graphs in section 2 of

Diehl:2011yj
. This is because our DPD definition in
section
3.3
absorbs all relevant effects of soft gluon
exchange in the cross section, leading to the crucial relations
(
42
) and (
43
).

Of course, the scale and rapidity parameter dependence of the DTMDs in
(
5
) does not arise from lowest-order graphs but from the full
analysis in section
3.4
. In the next section, we give
the final forms (
124
) and (
128
) of the corresponding
evolution equations, as well as their explicit solutions (
5.1
)
and (
5.1
) at the level of DPDs and of the cross section,
respectively. In section
5.2
we derive the evolution equations
(
139
) to (
142
) of DPDFs and give the explicit form
(
5.2
) of their rapidity dependence.

When combining SPS and DPS in the physical cross section, one must take
care of interference effects and of double counting. A systematic framework
for this has been proposed in
Diehl:2017kgu
, and in section

5.3
of the present paper we characterise the individual
terms in the master formula (
5.3
) for the cross section with
measured transverse momenta.

5.1
DTMD evolution: renormalisation scale and rapidity

Let us rewrite the evolution equations for the DTMDs using the projection
operators introduced in section
4
. The renormalisation group
equation (
3.4
) then reads

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{F,a_{1}}(\mu_{1},x_{1}\zeta/x_{2})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

(124)

for the scale
\mu_{1}
, and in analogy for
\mu_{2}
. Here we have introduced
the rapidity parameter

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=2x_{1}x_{2}\,(p^{+})^{2}\,e^{-2Y_{C}}\,,

(125)

where
p
is the proton momentum.
6
6

6

A different definition (going back
to
Ji:2004wu
) was used in
Diehl:2011yj
, namely

x_{1}x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta^{2}|_{\text{Ref.\penalty\ \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Diehl:2011yj}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}}=\zeta|_{\text{here}\,}
.

This extends the definition (
61
) for SPS to the case of DPS in
a way that is symmetric in the two momentum fractions
x_{1},x_{2}
. As
discussed after (
55
), the correct rapidity argument of

\gamma_{F,a}
in (
124
) is

x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}=2(x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+})^{2}e^{-2Y_{C}}
, since the corresponding UV
divergent subgraphs depend on the momentum of parton
1
(whose plus-component
is
x_{1}\mskip 1.5mup^{+}
), but not on the momentum of parton
2
. For the DPD of the
left moving proton with momentum
\bar{p}
we define accordingly

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}

\displaystyle=2\bar{x}_{1}\bar{x}_{2}\,(\bar{p}^{-})^{2}\,e^{2Y_{C}}\,.

(126)

Note that

\displaystyle\zeta\,\bar{\zeta}

\displaystyle=x_{1}\bar{x}_{1}x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bar{x}_{2}\,(2p^{+}\bar{p}^{\,-})^{2}=Q_{1}^{2}\,Q_{2}^{2}\,.

(127)

Rewriting the rapidity derivative as

\partial/\partial Y_{C}=-2\mskip 1.5mu\partial/\partial\log\zeta=2\mskip 1.5mu\partial/\partial\log\bar{\zeta}
, one obtains from (
45
) the
Collins-Soper equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\,{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

(128)

for colour projected DTMDs in a right moving proton. The Collins-Soper
equation for a left moving proton has the same form, with
\zeta
replaced by

\bar{\zeta}
. The scale dependence of the Collins-Soper kernel in
(
3.4
) now reads

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu_{1})\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}

(129)

and correspondingly for
\mu_{2}
. It is thus only the diagonal elements of

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
that have UV divergences and depend on the renormalisation
scales
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. Let us recall that the kernel
K_{a_{1}a_{2}}

and the anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{F,a}
and
\gamma_{K,a}
depend on the
colour representation of the parton (quarks or antiquarks vs. gluons) but not
on their flavour or polarisation.

It is easy to solve the rapidity and renormalisation scale evolution with
general starting scales
\mu_{01}
,
\mu_{02}
for
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
and

\zeta_{0}
for
\zeta
. Renormalisation scale evolution gives

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\exp\biggl[\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\gamma_{F,a_{1}}(\mu,x_{1}\zeta/x_{2})+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\gamma_{F,a_{2}}(\mu,x_{2}\zeta/x_{1})\biggr]\,.

(130)

Using the rapidity evolution in (
128
) we write

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\biggl[K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta_{0})\,,

(131)

where
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp
is to be understood as a matrix exponential, i.e.

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp(M)

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}+{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\,\sum_{R_{2},\ldots,R_{n}}\,\frac{{}^{RR_{2}}\mskip-1.5muM\,\cdots\,{}^{R_{n}R^{\prime}}\!M}{n!}\,.

(132)

We now split

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,\bigl[{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1})+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2})\bigr]+{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,,

(133)

where
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
is the kernel in the Collins-Soper equation
(
7
) for a single parton TMD. The colour singlet label
1
on
K
is
given for consistency with our later notation in section
6.2.2
.
By virtue of (
8
) and (
129
), the matrix

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M
is independent of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. Combining
(
5.1
) and (
131
) with (
133
), we obtain
the relation between the DTMD at initial and final scales in the form

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 15.00002pt+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\,\biggl[M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]\,{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{01},\mu_{02},\zeta_{0})\,,

(134)

where we have used the explicit form (
5
) of
\gamma_{F,a}
.
The exponential in the second and third lines is just the generalisation to two
partons of the evolution factor for a single parton TMD, as is readily seen by
solving the system of equations in (
3
) to
(
8
). It resums both double and single logarithms. The last
line in (
5.1
) describes the mixing between different
colour representations
R
under rapidity evolution and involves a single
logarithm. The double logarithms in the evolution of

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)
are thus the same
as those for a product of two single TMDs with appropriate arguments.

The symmetry relation (
90
) for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
translates into an
analogous relation for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M
. Using that
b_{i}
is the antiparton of

a_{i}
in the cross section formula (
5
) and that
M_{a_{1}a_{2}}

does not depend on parton spins, we can thus rewrite

\displaystyle\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\,\Biggl[M_{b_{1}b_{2}}\log\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\zeta}\rule{0.0pt}{9.04166pt}}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\Biggr]\,{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{b_{1}b_{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R^{\prime})\;{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{b_{1}b_{2}}\mskip 1.5mu{}^{R^{\prime}\!R}\mskip-1.5mu\exp\,\Biggl[M_{a_{1}a_{2}}\log\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\zeta}\rule{0.0pt}{9.04166pt}}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\Biggr]

(135)

when inserting the evolved form of
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{1}}
into the expression
(
5
) of
W
. This allows us to combine the matrix exponentials
associated with the two DPDs. Further using that one has equal anomalous
dimensions
\gamma_{a_{i}}=\gamma_{b_{i}}
and
\gamma_{K,a_{i}}=\gamma_{K,b_{i}}
and equal Collins-Soper kernels
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{i}}={}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{b_{i}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}}

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{Q_{1}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})\log\frac{Q_{1}Q_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 15.00002pt+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{Q_{2}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02})\log\frac{Q_{1}Q_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\,\biggr\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\,\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})\sum_{RR^{\prime}}\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{01},\mu_{02},\zeta_{0})\,

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 15.00002pt\times{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\,\biggl[M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\log\frac{Q_{1}Q_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\,\biggr]\,{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{01},\mu_{02},\zeta_{0})\,.

(136)

We have used the relation (
127
) for combining the arguments
of the logarithms and expressing them in terms of the physical scales
Q_{1}

and
Q_{2}
. The variables
\zeta
and
\bar{\zeta}
have thus completely
disappeared from the physical cross section. This is not the case for the
factorisation scales
\mu_{i}
in (
5.1
) and in the hard scattering
cross sections
\hat{\sigma}
. As is well known, the dependence on these
scales only cancels up to un-calculated higher orders in
\alpha_{s}
.

5.2
DPDF evolution

We now turn to DPDFs, which appear not only in the factorisation formula for
DPS with integrated transverse momenta, but also in the short-distance
expansion of DTMDs, as we will see in section
6.2
. We have
derived in section
4.4
that the soft factor

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
at
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
is diagonal
in
R
and
R^{\prime}
and independent of the parton species. Following the
construction in appendix
A
, one finds that the matrix

s
in (
36
) is also diagonal in
R
and
R^{\prime}
and satisfies the
relation

\displaystyle{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus(Y)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS(2Y)}\,,

(137)

which is the analogue of (
58
) for SPS.
The definition of DPDFs from unsubtracted collinear matrix elements in
(
3.3
) thus turns into

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\rightarrow-\infty}{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus^{-1}(Y_{C}-Y_{L})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(Y_{L})

(138)

for a right moving proton, and correspondingly for a left moving one. The
kernel
K=\widehat{K}
controlling the
Y
dependence of
S(Y)
and

s(Y)
is of course also diagonal in
R
and
R^{\prime}
. We denote it by

{}^{R}\!J
in order to avoid confusion with the evolution kernel for
TMDs. In terms of the variable
\zeta
, the rapidity evolution of DPDFs
is then given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)\,,

(139)

where we have explicitly given all arguments of the functions. Since in the
colour singlet channel
{}^{11}\mskip-1.5muS=1
, one has
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muJ=0
for the
corresponding evolution kernel, i.e. the collinear colour singlet DPDFs are
independent of the rapidity parameter
\zeta
.

Let us now discuss renormalisation. Setting

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
in the soft factor
S
and in the
hadronic matrix elements
F_{\text{us}}
induces additional UV divergences compared
to the case where these distances are finite. The graphs requiring
renormalisation are now not only vertex corrections and self energies, but
also involve the exchange of hard gluons between partons or Wilson lines
associated with the scattering amplitude and its complex conjugate. This is
of course well known from the renormalisation of the twist-two operators that
define ordinary PDFs, which is not purely multiplicative but involves a
convolution in the momentum fraction
x
and mixing between quark and gluon
operators. In the renormalisation group equations for PDFs, splitting kernels

P_{ab}
take the role of the anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{F,a}
in the TMD
case.

In the case of DPDFs we must pay particular attention to colour.
Taking the twist-two operators (
21
) at
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
projecting their colour indices on a definite representation
R
as in
(
79
), we obtain the colour projected matrix element

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us}}
according to (
80
). Due to gauge
invariance, operators
{}^{R}O_{a}
belonging to different colour
representations do not mix under renormalisation.
To obtain the soft factor
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS
, we evaluate the Wilson line products
(
3.2
) at
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
and project them on definite colour
channels
R
as
P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,O_{S,a}^{\underline{r},\underline{s}}
. Again,
operators with different
R
do not mix under renormalisation. The
renormalisation of the soft factor remains multiplicative, because it is
identical for quarks and gluons and because it does not depend on
x
.

With this in mind, one can repeat the arguments we
developed in section
3.4
for the renormalisation of DTMDs and
of their Collins-Soper kernels. One then obtains

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=-{}\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu_{1})

(140)

for the Collins-Soper kernel and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{b_{1}}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime};\mu_{1},x_{1}\zeta/x_{2})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}a_{2}}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

(141)

for DPDFs, as well as analogous equations for the
\mu_{2}

dependence.
7
7

7

Regarding factors of 2, our
convention for splitting kernels follows
Collins:2011zzd
, so
that
2P
takes the place of the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{F,a}
in TMD
evolution. The kernel
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP
in (
141
) should not
be confused with the colour projector
P_{R}
.

Let us emphasise that the evolution equation (
141
) for DPDFs
depending on
\boldsymbol{y}
is homogeneous. As discussed in

Diehl:2011yj
;
Diehl:2017kgu
, an additional term for the splitting of
one parton into two arises on the r.h.s. if one integrates the distributions
over
\boldsymbol{y}
or Fourier transforms them w.r.t. that variable. The
resulting inhomogeneous equation has been extensively studied in the
literature
Kirschner:1979im
;
Shelest:1982dg
;
Snigirev:2003cq
;
Gaunt:2009re
;
Ceccopieri:2010kg
.

By construction, the kernels
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muP_{a_{1}b_{1}}
for the colour singlet
sector are the ordinary DGLAP kernels for the evolution of PDFs. They are
hence independent of
\zeta
, which is not the case in the other colour
channels. Indeed, combining (
139
), (
140
),
(
141
) and requiring equality of the derivatives

\partial/(\partial\log\mu_{i})\,\partial/(\partial\log\zeta)\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF
and

\partial/(\partial\log\zeta)\,\partial/(\partial\log\mu_{i})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF
, we
find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)

(142)

and thus

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\mu}+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu,\mu^{2})\,.

(143)

These are the analogues of the relations (
4
) and (
5
)
for the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)
in TMD evolution.

We can make the
\zeta
dependence of the DPDFs fully explicit in the form

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl[-\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu}-\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\mskip 1.5mu\biggr]\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\,,

(144)

where
\widehat{F}
is defined by the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu_{1}}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=2\sum_{b_{1}}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime};\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{2})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{b_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

(145)

and its analogue for
\mu_{2}
, with the initial condition

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,.

(146)

Unlike
F
, the distribution
\widehat{F}
evolves with
\zeta
independent
kernels.
The correct scale dependence of (
5.2
) can be verified by
inserting (
142
) into (
141
), and its
\zeta

dependence can be verified by using (
139
) and
(
140
).

The integrals over
\mu
in (
5.2
) give rise to double
logarithms, which have been exponentiated to a Sudakov factor in the solution
of the evolution equation. Since the anomalous dimensions

{}^{R}\gamma_{J}
are positive for all
R\neq 1
(see
section
7.2.1
), the contribution of all colour non-singlet DPDs
to the cross section is Sudakov suppressed for sufficiently large hard scales.
This was recognised long ago in
Mekhfi:1988kj
and confirmed in

Manohar:2012jr
, where a simple numerical estimate was also given. In

Manohar:2012jr
, scale and rapidity evolution was discussed for
collinear DPS factorisation, keeping unsubtracted DPDs and an explicit soft
factor in the cross section. The individual evolution equations hence differ
between their approach and ours, but must of course give the same effects at
the cross section level.

5.3
Combining DPS with SPS

The DPS cross section given in (
5
) is not a physical observable,
because the same final state can also be produced by SPS. Dimensional
analysis readily shows that DPS and SPS contribute to the TMD cross section at
the same power in
1/Q
. The distinction between the two mechanisms is in
fact nontrivial, since there are Feynman graphs that contribute to both of
them. These are graphs in which one parton splits into two, as shown in
figure
5
a. The kinematic region where the
q\bar{q}
pairs are
nearly collinear with their parent gluon is naturally described as DPS, with
the splitting being included in the DPDs. By contrast, the region where the
quarks and antiquarks have large transverse momenta (and hence large
virtualities) is adequately described as a loop correction to SPS, with gluon
TMDs describing the initial state of the hard scattering.

The interference between SPS and DPS has the same power behaviour in
1/Q
as
SPS and DPS and hence should also be included in the cross section. Again,
there is a double counting problem, since graphs like the one in
figure
5
b contribute to either the interference term or to DPS,
depending on whether the splitting takes place in the collinear or in the hard
momentum region.
Note that not all graphs are affected by double counting. The graphs in
figure
5
for instance have no overlap with DPS, whereas there
is overlap between SPS and the SPS/DPS interference in
figure
5
b.

Figure 4:
Graphs that contribute to several terms in the
cross section (
5.3
). The rectangular boxes
and oval blobs indicate the relevant hadronic matrix elements, depending
on whether the
g\to q\bar{q}
splittings are collinear or hard.

Figure 5:
(a) A graph for the SPS/DPS interference that
does not have overlap with DPS. (b) An SPS graph that has no overlap with
DPS but with the SPS/DPS interference.

A systematic formalism for separating the different contributions and for
adding them in the cross section without double counting has been presented in

Diehl:2017kgu
. In the following, we briefly recapitulate the
essentials of this scheme for TMD factorisation, referring to that work for
details. DTMDs and other hadronic matrix elements are used in the transverse
position (rather than transverse momentum) representation. This is very
convenient for treating Collins-Soper evolution, which is then multiplicative
(rather than involving convolution integrals). The distinction between
collinear and hard parton splitting then corresponds to the transverse
distance between the partons being large or small, respectively.

Figure 6:
Transverse positions of the parton fields in parton
splitting contributions to (a) the quark-antiquark DTMD and (b) the
twist-three TMD
D_{q\bar{q}|g}
. Due to translation invariance, the latter
depends only on
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
. The positions in (b) correspond
to equation (3.9) in
Diehl:2017kgu
.

As can be seen in figure
6
a, the relative distance between the
two partons with momentum fractions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
in a DTMD is
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}

for the partons in the amplitude and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
for those in the conjugate
amplitude, with the distances defined in (
120
). The contribution
from small distances to the DPS cross section
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
is removed
by the factors
\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})
and
\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})
in
(
5
), which we discuss further at the end of this section.
Corresponding factors are inserted in the interference

\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}+\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}
between SPS and DPS. The
SPS cross section
\sigma_{\text{SPS}}
is not modified in this scheme and
computed as usual from TMDs and hard scattering cross sections, as described
for instance in
Collins:2011zzd
. The master formula for the overall
cross section then reads

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\text{DPS}\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}+\left[\mskip 1.5mu\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}-\sigma_{\text{DPS},\,y_{-}\to 0\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}+\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}-\sigma_{\text{DPS},\,y_{+}\to 0\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}\mskip 1.5mu\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\mskip 1.5mu\sigma_{\text{SPS}\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}-\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},\,y_{+}\rightarrow 0}-\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS},\,y_{-}\rightarrow 0}+\sigma_{\text{DPS},\,y_{\pm}\rightarrow 0\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}\mskip 1.5mu\right]\,,

(147)

where all terms should be taken differential in
dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,d\bar{x}_{1}\,d\bar{x}_{2}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
. In this formula, the double
counting problem between different contributions is solved by subtraction
terms, which we will discuss shortly.

Let us start with the interference between DPS and SPS. As the colour and
polarisation of the partons are not the main focus of the present section, we
will not display these quantum numbers explicitly. The following equations
hold for specific colour and helicity of each parton on the l.h.s. and with
appropriate sums on the r.h.s. The indices
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
will just
denote the parton species. Up to kinematic and numeric factors, the
interference cross section is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}}{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,d\bar{x}_{1}\,d\bar{x}_{2}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}\,\propto\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\alpha_1,
\alpha_2,\alpha_0}{\beta_1,\beta_2,\beta_0}}\!\!\!H_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\,H_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\,H^{*}_{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{Z}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{+}\;e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\boldsymbol{Z}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\boldsymbol{y}_{+}/2}\;\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\,D_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}|\beta_{0}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z})\,D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z})

(148)

for DPS in the amplitude, and by

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}}{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,d\bar{x}_{1}\,d\bar{x}_{2}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}\,\propto\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\alpha_0,
\alpha_1,\alpha_2}{\beta_0,\beta_1,\beta_2}}\!\!\!H_{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}\,H^{*}_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\,H^{*}_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{Z}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\;e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\boldsymbol{Z}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times e^{i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\boldsymbol{y}_{-}/2}\;\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})\,D_{\beta_{0}|\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{-},\boldsymbol{Z})\,D_{\alpha_{0}|\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{-},\boldsymbol{Z})

(149)

for DPS in the complex conjugate amplitude.
Here
D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}
is a twist-three TMD with two partons

\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
in the amplitude and one parton
\alpha_{0}
in the
conjugate amplitude. Its definition is similar to the one of DTMDs, with
three instead of four parton operators in the hadronic matrix element. The
transverse positions of the partons are as indicated in
figure
6
b. In
D_{\alpha_{0}|\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}
, the roles of
amplitude and conjugate amplitude are interchanged.
H_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}
and

H_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}
are the hard scattering amplitudes for the DPS
processes, whilst
H_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}
is the amplitude for the hard SPS
interaction.

\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}
receives a contribution from the graph in
figure
5
b without the rectangular boxes. The quark loop
in the conjugate amplitude includes an integration over the full phase
space, including the region where the
g\to q\bar{q}
splittings become
collinear. By a Fourier transform, one can rewrite the transverse part of
the loop integration as an integral over
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
, after which the
region of collinear splitting corresponds to large
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
. This
contribution is already included in
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
, so that a
subtraction term is necessary to prevent double counting. It reads

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{-}\to 0}}{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,d\bar{x}_{1}\,d\bar{x}_{2}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}\,\propto\,\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\alpha_1,
\alpha_2}{\beta_1,\beta_2}}H_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\,H_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\,H^{*}_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\,H^{*}_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{Z}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{+}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\,\,e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\boldsymbol{Z}-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2})(\boldsymbol{y}_{+}-\boldsymbol{y}_{-})/2}\;\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\,\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times F_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2},\mskip 1.5muy_{-}\to 0}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm},\boldsymbol{Z})\,F_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\mskip 1.5muy_{-}\to 0}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm},\boldsymbol{Z})\,.

(150)

Compared with the DPS cross section given by (
5
) and
(
5
), we have traded the arguments
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
of the
DTMDs for
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
,
\boldsymbol{Z}
and correspondingly changed the
integration according to (
122
). For simplicity we write

y_{-}\to 0
instead of
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\to\boldsymbol{0}
in subscripts. The DTMDs in
(
5.3
) correspond to the rectangular boxes in
figure
5
b and are given by

\displaystyle F_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\,y_{-}\to 0}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm},\boldsymbol{Z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{l^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{2}}\,\bigl[U^{l^{\prime}}_{\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(x_{i})\bigr]^{*}\,D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3/2})\,.

(151)

This notation reflects that in the limit
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\to\boldsymbol{0}
the DTMD
is dominated by the contribution where the two partons
\alpha_{1}
and

\alpha_{2}
in the conjugate amplitude originate from the splitting of
a single parton
\alpha_{0}
. The lowest order perturbative splitting kernel

U
includes a factor of
\sqrt{\alpha_{s}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}}
. For given

\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, the parton species
\alpha_{0}
is fixed at this
order, but its helicity must be summed over.
As shown in
Diehl:2017kgu
, the combination

\sigma_{\text{DPS}\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}+\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}-\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{-}\to 0\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}
correctly treats the
contribution of graph
5
b to the cross section, for small and
for large
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
, and without double counting. We will see in more
detail how this happens in section
6.5
. The other
subtraction term in the first line of (
5.3
) is given by
expressions analogous to (
5.3
) and (
151
),
with
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and the roles of amplitude and
conjugate amplitude being interchanged.

The subtraction terms
\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},y_{+}\rightarrow 0}
and

\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS},y_{-}\rightarrow 0}
in the second line of
(
5.3
) are obtained from (
5.3
) and
(
5.3
) by replacing all distributions
D
with their perturbative
splitting approximation. For the case shown in figure
6
b,
this is given by

\displaystyle D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0},\,y_{+}\to 0}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}}\;U_{\alpha_{0}\rightarrow\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{l}(x_{i})\,f_{\alpha_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3/2})\,,

(152)

where on the r.h.s. we have a single parton TMD and the same splitting kernel

U
as in (
151
). An analogous expression holds for the limit

\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\to\boldsymbol{0}
of
D_{\alpha_{0}|\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}
. The
combination

\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}+\sigma_{\text{SPS}\rule{0.0pt}{3.91806pt}}-\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},y_{+}\rightarrow 0}
correctly represents
graph
5
c in the region where the quark loop in the conjugate
amplitude is hard.

Finally, the term
\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{\pm}\rightarrow 0}
in
(
5.3
) ensures the correct treatment of the case when both quark
loops in graph
5
a are in the collinear region. This term is
given by the DPS cross section with each DTMD approximated
for the regime where both parton pairs are produced by perturbative
splitting:

\displaystyle F_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\,y_{\pm}\to 0}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm},\boldsymbol{Z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{l^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{2}}\,U^{l}_{\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(x_{i})\,\bigl[U^{l^{\prime}}_{\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(x_{i})\bigr]^{*}\,f_{\alpha_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,.

(153)

This is illustrated in figure
6
a and can be obtained by
inserting the expression (
152
) into (
151
).
Notice that
\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{\pm}\rightarrow 0}
appears with a plus rather
than a minus sign in (
5.3
). This is a consequence of the
recursive nature of double counting subtractions, as explained in section 4.2
of
Diehl:2017kgu
.

To make contact with our notation in section
6.3.1
, we
rewrite (
153
) in terms of a single splitting kernel
as
8
8

8

The convention for
T^{jj^{\prime}}
here is the same as in

Diehl:2017kgu
(arXiv version 2) and differs from the one in

Diehl:2011yj
as specified in
section
7.4
.

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2},\,y_{\pm}\to 0}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{l^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{2}}\,\sum_{a_{0}}\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi^{2}}\;{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}^{ll^{\prime}}\biggl(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{2}}\biggr)\,\frac{f_{a_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z})}{x_{1}+x_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,,

(154)

where we have restored the dependence on the colour representation and the
notation with labels
a_{i}
that specify parton species and polarisation.
For transverse quark or linear gluon polarisation, the parton distributions
and the kernel
T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}
carry additional transverse indices, which
are not displayed here (see section
7.4
).
The sum over
a_{0}
reflects the fact that there are TMDs for these
polarisation states, even if the proton is unpolarised.

We can now discuss the role of the function
\Phi
introduced earlier.
The short-distance behaviour of the DTMDs and twist-three distributions,
given in (
151
) and (
152
), results in an integral

\displaystyle\int\limits_{|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/\Lambda}\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{+}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}}\;e^{-i\mskip 1.5mu(\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}_{+}/2}\;\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})

(155)

and its analogue for
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
in the cross section formulae for DPS and
for the SPS/DPS interference. The function
\Phi
must satisfy

\Phi(\boldsymbol{u})\to 0
for
\boldsymbol{u}\to\boldsymbol{0}
to ensure that these
integrals converge at small distances (rather than having a logarithmic
divergence). In order to keep the regions of large
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and

\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
unaffected, one should furthermore have
\Phi(\boldsymbol{u})\to 1

for
|\boldsymbol{u}|\gg 1
. A simple choice for
\Phi(\boldsymbol{u})
is a step
function in
|\boldsymbol{u}|
, which corresponds to a hard cutoff on the

\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
integrals.

The integral (
155
) goes like
\log(\nu/q_{T})
and thus depends on the
artificial parameter
\nu
that controls which distances
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
are
included in what we
define
to be DPS rather than SPS. The
\nu

dependence of the different terms in the cross section (
5.3
)
cancels to the perturbative order of the calculation, in close analogy with
the familiar case of renormalisation and factorisation scale dependence. As
discussed in
Diehl:2017kgu
, a suitable choice for
\nu
is the lowest
hard scale,
\text{min}(Q_{1},Q_{2})
. With this choice,
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}

contains a squared logarithm
\log^{2}(Q/q_{T})
, where we recall that
Q

denotes the generic size of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
. After the subtractions in the
first line of (
5.3
), the SPS/DPS interference contains only a
single
\log(Q/q_{T})
and the subtracted SPS term in the second line has
no such logarithm at the corresponding order in
\alpha_{s}
. This has an
important consequence. If one is satisfied with leading logarithmic accuracy,
then the terms in square brackets in (
5.3
) can be neglected at
the order in
\alpha_{s}
where they have overlap with DPS, because at that
order it is
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
that has the highest power of

\log(Q/q_{T})
.

We should note that the structure of the higher-order terms in
(
151
), (
152
) and (
153
) is
currently unknown. In particular, it cannot be excluded that singularities
will appear at points other than
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}=\boldsymbol{0}
and

\boldsymbol{y}_{-}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The treatment of ultraviolet divergences and of the
associated double counting issues may thus be more involved beyond the leading
order discussed here.

To conclude this section, we briefly discuss the scale evolution of the
twist-three TMDs
D
. As already noted, they are defined in terms of hadronic
matrix elements, with three parton fields and the corresponding Wilson lines
instead of the four fields we have in the unsubtracted DTMDs in
(
3.1
). The three parton fields are at different transverse
positions (if all three positions are equal, one has collinear twist-three
distributions). It is easy to see that the analysis of factorisation for the
SPS/DPS interference will yield a soft factor, with three products of Wilson
lines associated with the three parton fields in the hadronic matrix elements
(rather than four products of Wilson lines as in
figure
1
). One can readily adapt the discussion in
sections
3.2
to
3.4
to this case and absorb
the soft factor into the twist-three distributions. The colour algebra is
much simpler than in the DPS case, with a single colour representation in the

q\bar{q}g
channel, and two for the channel with three gluons (one
constructed with
f^{abc}
and the other with
d^{abc}
). The rapidity
dependence of
D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}
is finally given by a
Collins-Soper equation as in (
128
) with a kernel

K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}
. If one takes different renormalisation
scales
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{0}
for the three parton legs, then the
dependence of
D
and
K
on these scales is given by the analogues of
(
124
) and (
129
), with
\gamma_{K,a}
replaced by

\gamma_{K,a}/2
and
\gamma_{F,a}
by
\gamma_{E,a}
or
(\gamma_{E,a})^{*}

as discussed at the end of section
3.4
.
The rapidity parameter
\zeta
is defined by (
125
) and must be
properly rescaled in the argument of
\gamma_{F,a}
, namely by

x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}
,
x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}
and
(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/(x_{1}x_{2})
for
the partons with momentum fractions
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{1}+x_{2}
, respectively.

6
Matching for small but perturbative transverse momenta

In the multi-scale regime
\Lambda\ll q_{T}\ll Q
, where the transverse
momenta
|\boldsymbol{q}_{i}|
are small compared with
Q_{i}
but large compared with
the scale
\Lambda
of nonperturbative physics, the DPS cross section, as well
as its combination with SPS can be considerably simplified. The reason is
that the additional scale
q_{T}
allows for more perturbative calculations,
which greatly enhances the predictive power of the theory.

We start our analysis by discussing the different transverse distance
regions in the DPS factorisation formula and find that two distinct regions of

\boldsymbol{y}
are relevant. The corresponding short-distance expansions of DTMDs
are derived in sections
6.2
and
6.3
,
respectively. In section
6.4
we discuss the master
formula (
206
) for combining the two types of expansion in the
cross section, where care must be taken to avoid double counting. After this,
we revisit the combination between DPS and SPS just discussed in
section
5.3
. In section
6.6
we
go through the perturbative ingredients that are necessary for a given
resummation order as specified in table
3
; their availability
determines the perturbative accuracy of cross section computations.

6.1
Regions of transverse momenta and distances

In the DPS cross section (
5
), contributions from distances

|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\gg 1/|\boldsymbol{q}_{i}|
are suppressed by oscillations of the
Fourier exponent
e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-i\boldsymbol{q}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}}
. If

\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
are sufficiently large, this keeps

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
in the region where perturbation theory has
predictive power for the dependence on these variables.

Care is required if
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
are perturbatively large but

|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
is of order
\Lambda
. According to
(
122
) the sum
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
can then reach
large values of order
1/\Lambda
, as long as
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|

remains small. The individual oscillations of
e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}}

and
e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{2}}
cancel in that case. Likewise,

|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
can become as large as
1/\Lambda
if
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}

and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
are perturbatively large but
|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
is
of order
\Lambda
.
The perturbative splitting form (
154
) of DTMDs has a factorised
dependence on the variables
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
, and the short-distance expansion
(
193
) we will derive from it requires both distances to be
small.
We therefore do not consider the particular phase space regions just
mentioned and require
|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}|
and
|\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
as well as

|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
and
|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
to be much
larger than
\Lambda
. The dominant contribution to the
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
integrals in the cross section formula (
5
) is
then in the perturbative region.
Even with this requirement, one could still consider a region where

|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
is much smaller than
|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}|
and

|\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|
. This multi-scale regime has been discussed in section 5.2.1
of
Diehl:2011yj
and in
Blok:2011bu
;
Blok:2013bpa
. We shall not
investigate it in the present work.

Other observables in which sensitivity to large
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
can be avoided are obtained by integrating the cross section
over one or both of
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
up to
q_{\text{max},1}
and

q_{\text{max},2}
respectively. In

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}\;\theta\bigl(q_{\text{max}}^{2}-\boldsymbol{q}^{2}\bigr)\int\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\;e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\boldsymbol{z}}\,W(\boldsymbol{z},\ldots)

\displaystyle=q_{\text{max}}\int\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{z}}{2\pi|\boldsymbol{z}|}\,J_{1}\bigl(q_{\text{max}}|\boldsymbol{z}|\bigr)\,W(\boldsymbol{z},\ldots)

(156)

the region of
|\boldsymbol{z}|\gg 1/q_{\text{max}}
is damped by oscillations,
as it is by the exponential factors in the differential cross section. To
avoid that the product of the two Bessel functions has a
non-oscillating component for
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|\approx|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
, one
must take
q_{\text{max},1}\neq q_{\text{max},2}
.

We henceforth assume that both
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|
and
|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
are small,
namely of order
1/q_{T}
. Since
\boldsymbol{y}
is integrated over without any
Fourier exponent in (
5
) we must still consider the two cases

|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim 1/\Lambda
and
|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim 1/q_{T}
, which are referred
to as large
\boldsymbol{y}
and small
\boldsymbol{y}
in the following. For even
smaller
|\boldsymbol{y}|\ll 1/q_{T}
, the approximations for DPS are not valid and
the subtraction formalism sketched in section
5.3
comes
into play. The short-distance expansion of DPDs in the two DPS regions just
mentioned is quite different and will now be discussed in turn.

6.2
The large-
\boldsymbol{y}
region

For small
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
and large
\boldsymbol{y}
, the DTMD

F(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
can be computed in terms of DPDFs

F(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
, in close analogy to the case of single parton
distributions. This is commonly referred to as “matching”. For DTMDs there
are complications from colour, but our results from
section
4.4
lead to considerable
simplifications. Generalising the relations (
9
) and
(
2
) between TMDs and PDFs, we can derive the general
matching equation (
6.2.1
) and the explicit solution
(
6.2.2
) of the evolution equations for DTMDs. This solution
is promoted to the cross section level in (
6.2.2
). We emphasise
that the simple structure of (
6.2.2
) and (
6.2.2
) is
due to the additive form (
171
) of the Collins-Soper matrix
kernel in the short-distance limit considered here.

6.2.1
Short-distance expansion

We now derive the approximation of DTMDs in the region
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll 1/\Lambda
, with
\boldsymbol{y}
kept fixed at a value such
that
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
. Corrections to this
approximation are suppressed by powers of
|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\Lambda
and of

|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|/|\boldsymbol{y}|
. To make the analogy between DTMDs and single
parton TMDs transparent, we formulate the matching in terms of operator
product expansions around
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Let us start with the expansion of the soft factor. We need the
small-
\boldsymbol{z}
limit of the operators
O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
defined
in (
3.2
), which contain two Wilson line pairs separated by a
transverse distance
\boldsymbol{z}
. The colour structure of the short-distance
expansion can be deduced from the corresponding Feynman graphs. To minimise
the number of eikonal propagators that carry large momentum, interactions at
the hard scale
1/|\boldsymbol{z}|
must take place closest to the points where the
left- and right-moving Wilson lines meet, as shown in
figure
7
. In terms of the operators in (
3.2
) and
(
3.2
), we thus find the structure

\displaystyle\bigl[{O}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}

\displaystyle=C_{S,a}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}(\boldsymbol{z})\;\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}_{\underline{s},\underline{t}}

(157)

with a short-distance coefficient
C_{S,a}
that can be computed in
perturbation theory. Here and in the following, the absence of the
argument
\boldsymbol{z}
in operators or functions means that one has set

\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The analogue of this convention will be used for
arguments
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
. Introducing colour projected coefficients

{}^{R}C_{S,a}=P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\,C_{S,a}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\big/m(R)
and using (
72
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}{}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})\,P_{R}^{\underline{s}\,\underline{t}}\,\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}_{\underline{s},\underline{t}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}{}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,P_{R}^{\underline{u}\,\underline{t}}\,\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{\underline{s},\underline{t}}_{\underline{v},\underline{v}}\,,

(158)

where the second step is completely analogous to the derivation of the
relation (
4.4
) between the extended soft
factor and the usual one. Taking the vacuum expectation value of

O_{S,a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\,O_{S,a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})
with
appropriate colour projections, we obtain

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{R}C_{S,a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\,{}^{R}C_{S,a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\,{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS(\boldsymbol{y})\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,.

(159)

For computing the short-distance coefficients, it will be convenient to
contract (
6.2.1
) with
P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{u}}
, which
gives

\displaystyle P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{u}}\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}

\displaystyle={}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})\,P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{u}}\,\bigl[O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{\underline{r},\underline{u}}\,.

(160)

Figure 7:
Interactions of Wilson lines in the operator

O_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
in the limit of small
\boldsymbol{z}
.
Short-distance interactions are located in the box at the centre, whereas
gluons outside the box indicate possible interactions with other Wilson
lines at distances much larger than
\boldsymbol{z}
. In the soft factor
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
these distances are of order
\boldsymbol{y}
for
small
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
.

For approximating the unsubtracted collinear matrix element
F_{\text{us}}
, we need
the small-
\boldsymbol{z}
limit of the parton operators in (
21
). We use
their projections (
79
) on definite colour representations,
which cannot mix with each other due to gauge invariance. The short-distance
expansion involves a convolution integral over momentum fractions and
reads
9
9

9

The lower limit of the convolution in
(
161
) is
x^{\prime}=0
. When matrix elements between hadrons
with plus momentum
p^{+}
are taken, the lower limit becomes
x^{\prime}=x
, as in
(
10
).

\displaystyle{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b}{}^{R}C_{\text{us},ab}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z})\,\underset{x}{\otimes}{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{b}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y})\,.

(161)

If
a
or
b
refer to transverse quark or linear gluon polarisation, then
the associated operators carry additional transverse indices, and the same
holds of course for
C_{\text{us},ab}
. Taking matrix elements as in
(
80
), we obtain an approximation

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{1},b_{2}}{}^{R}C_{\text{us},a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{\text{us},a_{2}b_{2}}(x_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\underset{x_{2}}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y})

(162)

of unsubtracted DTMDs in terms of unsubtracted DPDFs and perturbative
coefficients
C_{\text{us},ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
.

We now combine the soft factor with the unsubtracted DTMD. Using the relation
(
137
) between
s
and
S
, we obtain

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5mus_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1},2Y)}\,\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2},2Y)}\;{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)\,\delta_{RR^{\prime}}

(163)

from (
159
), where we have restored the dependence on
renormalisation scales and rapidities. Combining this with (
162
)
and using the definition (
84
) of DPDs, we get

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{1},b_{2}}{}^{R}C_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1},Y_{C})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{a_{2}b_{2}}(x_{2}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2},Y_{C})

\displaystyle\qquad\underset{x_{2}}{\otimes}\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y_{L})\big/{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y_{C}-Y_{L})

(164)

for a DPD in a right moving proton, where the short-distance coefficients
are defined as

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,Y_{C})

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}\frac{{}^{R}C_{\text{us},ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,Y_{L})}{\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,2Y_{C}-2Y_{L})\rule{0.0pt}{8.1805pt}}}\,.

(165)

Switching variables from
Y_{C}
to
\zeta
and again using the definition
(
84
) of DPDs, we obtain the final form of the matching equation:

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{1},b_{2}}{}^{R}C_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1},x_{1}\zeta/x_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{a_{2}b_{2}}(x_{2}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2},x_{2}\zeta/x_{1})\underset{x_{2}}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}\!\mskip-1.5mu\smash{{}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)\,.

(166)

The rescaling factors
x_{1}/x_{2}
or
x_{2}/x_{1}
of
\zeta
in the
short-distance coefficients arise for the same reason as discussed after
(
125
). Note that they involve the parton momentum fractions

x_{1}
or
x_{2}
and not the integration variables
x_{1}^{\prime}
or
x_{2}^{\prime}
of the
convolution (
10
).

Let us emphasise that (
6.2.1
) involves mixing between quark and
gluon distributions. Therefore, the combination of
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus(\boldsymbol{y})

and
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{us},b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
into a DPDF works only
because
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muS(\boldsymbol{y})
is the same for quarks and gluons (for
representations
R
accessible to quarks).

Let us finally set
R=1
and show that in the colour singlet channel, the
coefficient
{}^{1}C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
in (
6.2.1
) is identical
to the coefficient
C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
in the small-
\boldsymbol{z}
expansion
(
9
) of a single parton TMD. The corresponding identity
for the coefficient
{}^{1}C_{\text{us},ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
readily follows from
taking the matrix element of the operator relation (
161
)
between two proton states, which is related to the unsubtracted TMD

f_{\text{us},a}
via the first relation in (
3.1
). For

{}^{1}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})
the identity is obtained by taking the vacuum
expectation value of (
160
), which gives the soft factor

S_{a}(\boldsymbol{z})
relevant for constructing the TMD
f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
via
(
58
) and (
60
). The short-distance
coefficient for
f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
is then obtained as in (
165
).

Notice that the vacuum expectation value of the colour projected operator on
the r.h.s. of (
160
) gives unity for
R=1
, because the
Wilson lines along both
v_{L}
and
v_{R}
appear in the combination
WW^{\dagger}=\mathbb{1}
. This reflects the fact that no soft factor appears in
the definition of ordinary PDFs. We thus find

\displaystyle S_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,Y)={}^{1}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,Y)

(167)

for small
\boldsymbol{z}
, i.e. the soft factor for single TMD factorisation
is purely perturbative at small distances.

6.2.2
Evolution equations and their solution

Let us now establish the consequences of the short-distance expansion
(
6.2.1
) for scale and rapidity evolution. From
(
163
) we deduce that
