\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\log\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1},2Y)}+\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\log\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2},2Y)}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\log{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)-\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\log{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5mus(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)

\displaystyle\quad={}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})-{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\phantom{\frac{\partial}{\partial}}

(168)

with the kernels
{}^{RR}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}a_{2}}
and
{}^{R}\!J
from
(
35
) and (
139
). The rapidity derivative of

{}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,2Y)
must thus be independent of
Y
.
Defining

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial Y}\log{}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,Y)\,,

(169)

we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\zeta}\,{}^{R}C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muC_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

(170)

for the coefficients in (
165
). Inserting this into
(
6.2.1
), we find

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,\bigl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1})+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2})+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\bigr]\,.

(171)

This represents a significant simplification of the Collins-Soper equation
for DTMDs in the small
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
limit, both in the colour structure and
in the separation of the three distance variables.

Taking the derivative
\partial/\partial\log\mu_{1}
of (
171
)
and using the renormalisation group equations (
129
) and
(
140
) for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
and
{}^{R}\!J
, we deduce
that

\displaystyle\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)

\displaystyle={}^{R}\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)+{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)

(172)

for all
R
, where we have introduced the anomalous dimension of the kernel

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle={}-{}^{R}\gamma_{K,a}(\mu)\,.

(173)

In the colour singlet sector, we have
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muJ=0
and hence

{}^{1\,}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}=0
, which implies
{}^{1\,}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,a}=\gamma_{K,a}
. Indeed,
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
is identical to the
ordinary Collins-Soper kernel
K_{a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
in (
7
), which is
readily shown by taking the rapidity derivative of (
167
).

Taking the
\mu_{1}
derivative of the short-distance expansion
(
6.2.1
) and using the evolution equations (
124
)
and (
141
) for DTMDs and DPDFs, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}{}^{R}C_{ac}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{b}{}^{R}C_{ab}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\underset{x}{\otimes}\Bigl[\delta_{bc}\,\delta(1-x^{\prime})\,\gamma_{F,c}(\mu,\zeta)-2\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{bc}(x^{\prime};\mu,\zeta)\Bigr]\,.

(174)

Having computed the short-distance kernel
{}^{R}C_{ab}
at a certain
order in
\alpha_{s}
, we can use this relation to reconstruct the evolution
kernel
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}
at the same order. We will do this in
section
7.3.5
.

With the short-distance limit (
171
) of the Collins-Soper
kernel, the general solution (
5.1
) of the evolution
equations for DTMDs takes the form

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu}\biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 35.50008pt+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu}\biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 35.50008pt+\Bigl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02})+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\Bigr]\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\biggr\}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0})\,,

(175)

where different colour channels no longer mix with each other. Using
(
170
), we can rewrite the rapidity dependence of the
short-distance kernels as

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01},x_{1}\zeta_{0}/x_{2})

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta_{0}/x_{2}}}{\mu_{01}}\biggr]\,{}^{R}C_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01},\mu_{01}^{2})

(176)

and likewise for the index
2
, which reduces the number of independent
scales in
C_{ab}
. Using the short-distance matching (
6.2.1
)
for
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0})
in
(
6.2.2
), we obtain our master formula for DTMDs at small

\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
and large
\boldsymbol{y}
:

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu_{01}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;\;+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02})\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu_{02}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;\;+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\quad\;\times\sum_{b_{1},b_{2}}{}^{R}C_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01},\mu_{01}^{2})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{a_{2}b_{2}}(x_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02},\mu_{02}^{2})\underset{x_{2}}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0}).

(177)

In the colour singlet channel, the result (
6.2.2
) is a
simple copy of its analogue (
2
) for a single-parton
TMD, with separate short-distance coefficients
C_{ab}
and Sudakov
exponentials for each parton. For colour non-singlet channels, the
Collins-Soper kernels
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z})
acquire a colour dependence,
and there is an additional term
{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y})
in the exponent. The
latter is also present in collinear DPS factorisation: in fact one simply
has

\displaystyle\exp\,\biggl[{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta)

(178)

according to (
139
). In this sense, the value of
\zeta_{0}
in
(
6.2.2
) is irrelevant (contrary to the choice of
\mu_{0i}
,
which appears in quantities that are computed using fixed-order perturbation
theory). The choice of
\zeta_{0}
does matter when this is the scale at
which one formulates an ansatz or model for the DPDFs (which is of course
inevitable for concrete calculations).

After inserting (
6.2.2
) and its counterpart for the left
moving proton into the definition (
5
) of
W
, we can combine the
logarithms of
\zeta
and
\bar{\zeta}
in the exponentials, as we did in
the generic expression (
5.1
). We thus obtain

\displaystyle W_{\text{large $y$}}=\sum_{R}\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{01}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{1}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})\log\frac{{Q_{1}^{2}}}{\mu_{01}^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 44.50003pt+\int_{\mu_{02}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{2}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02})\,\log\frac{{Q_{2}^{2}}}{\mu_{02}^{2}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2}}{}^{R}C_{b_{1}d_{1}}(\bar{x}_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01},\mu_{01}^{2})\underset{\bar{x}_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{b_{2}d_{2}}(\bar{x}_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02},\mu_{02}^{2})\,

\displaystyle\hskip 51.00014pt\underset{\bar{x}_{2}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{a_{1}c_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01},\mu_{01}^{2})\underset{x_{1}}{\otimes}{}^{R}C_{a_{2}c_{2}}(x_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{02},\mu_{02}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\underset{x_{2}}{\otimes}\bigl[\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{2}\,\exp\,\biggl[{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\mskip 1.5mu\log\frac{Q_{1}\mskip 1.5muQ_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\,\biggr]\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{d_{1}d_{2}}(\bar{x}_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{c_{1}c_{2}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i},\zeta_{0})\,,

(179)

which is the main result of this section. The product of regulator
functions
\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})
has been
approximated as appropriate for
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
.
Note that the dependence on
\boldsymbol{y}
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
is
completely factorised in (
6.2.2
). The
\boldsymbol{y}
integral can
hence be performed separately. For unpolarised or longitudinally polarised
partons, the short-distance coefficients
C
are independent of the
direction of
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
by rotation invariance. The angular
part of the
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
integrations can then readily be performed and
turns the Fourier exponentials
e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}{}_{i}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}
into Bessel
functions. The situation for transverse quark or linear gluon polarisation,
where
C
(as well as
F
and
\hat{\sigma}
) carries transverse indices,
can be discussed along similar lines.

Each of the two partonic cross sections
\hat{\sigma}_{i}
in
(
5
) and the four short-distance kernels
{}^{R}C
in
(
6.2.2
) has an
\alpha_{s}
expansion starting at order

\alpha_{s}^{0}
. It is natural (although not mandatory) to truncate their
product to the highest order in
\alpha_{s}
at which the individual factors
are computed.

To evaluate the kernels
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK
and
{}^{R}C
in (
6.2.2
) in
fixed-order perturbation theory, one should choose the scales
\mu_{0i}

such that no large logarithms appear at higher orders. A standard choice
for single hard scattering is to take
\mu_{0i}^{2}=b_{0}^{2}\mskip 1.5mu/\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{2}
, which makes the one-loop expression of

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z}_{i};\mu_{0i})
vanish. Here

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle=2e^{-\gamma_{E}}

(180)

with
\gamma_{E}
being the Euler constant. While the cross section
(
5
) is dominated by
|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\sim 1/|\boldsymbol{q}_{i}|
due
to the Fourier exponentials
e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}{}_{i}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}
, one still must
integrate over the full range of these distances. To avoid evaluating the
DPDs at unreasonably small factorisation scales
\mu_{0i}
, one may modify
the above scale choice and take instead
\mu_{0i}=\mu_{z_{i}}
, where for
any transverse distance vector
\boldsymbol{b}
we define

\displaystyle\mu_{b}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{\boldsymbol{b}^{*2}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{with \penalty\ $\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{b})\xrightarrow[\boldsymbol{b}\to\boldsymbol{0}]{}\boldsymbol{b}$\penalty\ and \penalty\ $|\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{b})|\xrightarrow[|\boldsymbol{b}|\to\infty]{}b_{\text{max}}$}\,,

(181)

where
b_{\text{max}}
is chosen such that
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
remains in the
region where one trusts perturbative theory even when
\boldsymbol{b}
becomes
large. A possible choice for
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{b}}{\sqrt{1+\boldsymbol{b}^{2}/b_{\text{max}}^{2}}}\,,

which was proposed long ago
Collins:1981va
;
Collins:1984kg
and is
extensively used in TMD phenomenology, but other functional forms have also
been explored
Bacchetta:2015ora
. We note that it is not mandatory to
take factorisation scales proportional to inverse distances, referring to

Becher:2011xn
;
Becher:2012yn
and

DAlesio:2014mrz
;
Echevarria:2015uaa
for examples of scale setting in
momentum space and to section 7.1.2 of
Ebert:2016gcn
for a brief
synopsis. Most results in our paper do not depend on choosing scales as in
(
181
). An exception is the discussion of scale setting in
section
6.4
and appendix
C
, which needs to
be adapted if a different choice is made.

In the colour nonsinglet sector, the DPDs
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF
in (
6.2.2
)
still involve the different scales
\mu_{0i}
and
\zeta_{0}
. To disentangle
this dependence further, one may use (
5.2
) and
(
178
) to rewrite
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF
in terms of the distribution

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}
, whose DGLAP evolution does not involve a separate
rapidity scale, and which is therefore better suited to make the separation
of scales explicit. At the cross section level, one can then replace the
last line of (
6.2.2
) by

\displaystyle\bigl[\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y})\bigr]^{2}\,\exp\,\biggl[-\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{01}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\;{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{1}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}-\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{02}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\;{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{2}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\log\frac{Q_{1}\mskip 1.5muQ_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\,\biggr]\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{d_{1}d_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(\bar{x}_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{c_{1}c_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})\,,

(182)

where we have explicitly indicated that the two renormalisation scales in

{}^{R}\!J
are taken equal. It is natural to take the starting scale of
evolution as
\mu_{0}=\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}
. A particular choice is
\mu_{0}=\mu_{y}
, which for large
\boldsymbol{y}
saturates at a hadronic scale
\mu_{0}=b_{0}/b_{\text{max}}
. This choice ensures that the initial conditions for
DPD evolution do not involve widely different scales as
\boldsymbol{y}
becomes
small.

The factor
{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0i})
in (
6.2.2
) is a
nonperturbative function in the large-
\boldsymbol{y}
region, but we can ensure
that it has the correct perturbative small-
\boldsymbol{y}
behaviour by copying
part of the so-called
b^{*}
trick formulated for ordinary TMDs in

Collins:1981va
;
Collins:1984kg
. We thus write

\displaystyle{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{01},\mu_{02})

\displaystyle=-\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{J}(\boldsymbol{y})+{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}\mskip-1.5mu;\mu_{y},\mu_{y})-\int_{\mu_{y}}^{\mu_{01}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\;{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)-\int_{\mu_{y}}^{\mu_{02}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\;{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,,

(183)

where the nonperturbative information is contained in

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{J}(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}\mskip-1.5mu;\mu_{i})-{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\,.

(184)

Because of (
140
), the dependence on the renormalisation scales
drops out in
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{J}
, which satisfies
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{J}(\boldsymbol{0})=0
by
construction. The term
{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}\!;\mu_{y},\mu_{y})
in
(
183
) can be evaluated in fixed-order perturbation theory;
according to the results in section
7.2
it is zero up to
terms of
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
. Of course, the form
(
183
) with
\mu_{0i}
replaced by
\mu_{0}
can be used
in (
6.2.2
).

In the colour octet sector, the relation (
110
) implies that

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5muJ(\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu)

\displaystyle=K_{g}(\boldsymbol{y};\mu)\,,

(185)

where
K_{g}
is the Collins Soper kernel for single gluon TMDs. While little
is known about this kernel for nonperturbative distances
\boldsymbol{y}
, one may
construct a model by connecting it with its counterpart
K_{q}
in the
quark-antiquark channel, for which there is a considerable body of
phenomenology from single Drell-Yan production and from semi-inclusive DIS,
see e.g.
Aidala:2014hva
;
Collins:2014jpa
and references therein. A
simple possibility would be to assume Casimir scaling,
K_{g}/C_{A}=K_{q}/C_{F}
. This scaling holds perturbatively up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
, as
can for instance be seen in appendix D of

Echevarria:2016scs
.
10
10

10

Just recently it has been found

Moch:2017uml
that Casimir scaling for the cusp anomalous
dimension,
\gamma_{K,g}/C_{A}=\gamma_{K,q}/C_{F}
, is broken at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{4})
. Equation (
8
) implies the
breaking of Casimir scaling for
K_{a}
at the same order. For a related
calculation see
Grozin:2017css
.

6.2.3
Extrapolation to large
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}

If
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
are not sufficiently large to ensure
dominance of small
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
in the TMD cross section, then a more
realistic description of the integrand for large
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
is necessary.
Of course, a corresponding statement holds already for single hard
scattering. A widely used procedure in that case is the
b^{*}
trick of

Collins:1981va
;
Collins:1984kg
. Let us briefly show how it can be
adapted to DPS. We recall the solution (
131
) of the rapidity
evolution equation,

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\biggl[K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta_{0})

and introduce

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{F,a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\zeta)

\displaystyle=\log\,\frac{{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}^{*}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)}{{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!g_{K,a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}^{*}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})-{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

(186)

with
\boldsymbol{z}^{*}
defined by (
181
). Both
g_{F}
and
g_{K}
are
independent of
\mu_{i}
according to (
124
) and
(
129
), and both functions depend on the parameter

b_{\text{max}}
via
\boldsymbol{z}^{*}_{i}
. By construction, both functions vanish
at the point
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
. We can then write

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\biggl[{}-g_{K,a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,+K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{*},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\bigl[{}-{}^{R^{\prime}}\!g_{F,a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\zeta_{0})\bigr]\,{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}^{*}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta_{0})\,.

(187)

In this expression,
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
and
{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2}}
can
be evaluated using the short-distance expansion discussed in the previous
subsection, provided that
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}^{*}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}^{*}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
. As
a consequence,
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
is diagonal in
R
and
R^{\prime}
. By
contrast, the functions
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!g_{K,a_{1}a_{2}}
and

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mug_{F,a_{1}a_{2}}
are entirely nonperturbative and need to be modelled.
This is a daunting task, because they depend on several transverse
variables, there are many functions in the different colour channels and there
is less guidance from data. Using the
b^{*}
trick for computing DPS processes
with
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
in the nonperturbative region would
therefore require additional theory input, or strong simplifying assumptions.

The
b^{*}
trick is not the only way to handle the region of large
transverse distances, and for TMD factorisation in SPS a variety of other
methods have been employed

Laenen:2000de
;
Kulesza:2002rh
;
Bozzi:2005wk
;
Qiu:2000hf
;
Becher:2011xn
;
Becher:2012yn
;
DAlesio:2014mrz
;
Echevarria:2015uaa
;
Scimemi:2016ffw
;
Scimemi:2017etj
. It would
be interesting to study if and how they could be adapted to DPS. This is
however beyond the scope of the present work, where we focus on transverse
momenta in the perturbative region.

6.3
The small-
\boldsymbol{y}
region

We now consider the region where
|\boldsymbol{y}|
is of the same order as

|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|
and
|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
, with all distances being small compared
with
1/\Lambda
. Again, we will derive double parton analogues of the
relations (
9
) and (
2
) between TMDs and
PDFs. They are given in (
193
), (
194
) and
(
6.3.2
) and involve collinear PDFs and twist-four
distributions as nonperturbative input, which also appear in the expansion
(
6.3.1
) of DPDFs at small
\boldsymbol{y}
. At the cross section
level, we then obtain the expression (
6.3.2
) in which all large
logarithms are explicitly resummed. An important finding is that the
different combinations of collinear PDFs and twist-four distributions have
different power behaviour in the cross section, specified in
(
195
) and compared with the contribution from large
\boldsymbol{y}

in (
196
).

6.3.1
Short-distance expansion

Let us first establish that the soft factor, and hence the Collins-Soper
kernel
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
, can be fully computed in perturbation theory
when all transverse distances become small. Repeating the arguments that lead
to (
157
), we obtain

\displaystyle(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{|}}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{0})\,,

(188)

which involves the extended soft factor (
3.2
) with all distance
arguments set to zero and a short-distance coefficient
C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}}

describing interactions between all Wilson lines. Inserting colour projectors
as in (
73
), we get

\displaystyle(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{RR^{\prime}}P_{R}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}\,C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{|}}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2},\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{t}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{0})}{m(R)\,m(R^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{RR^{\prime}}P_{R}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}\,C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{|}}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2},\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\,\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{s}_{1}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{t}_{1}}\,(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{s}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{t}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}_{\underline{s}_{2}\underline{s}_{2},\underline{t}_{2}\underline{t}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{0})}{m(R)\,m(R^{\prime})}\,,

(189)

where in the second step the identity (
102
) has been used for the
indices associated with parton
a_{1}
in
S_{a_{1}a_{2}}
. The sum over
\underline{s}_{2}

on the r.h.s. now ties together pairs of conjugate Wilson lines along the
same direction and at the same transverse position, which results in unit
matrices according to
W(\boldsymbol{\xi},v)W^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\xi},v)=\mathbb{1}
. For
S_{qq}
this
is represented pictorially in figure
8
. The same holds
for the sum over
\underline{t}_{2}
. The extended soft factor thus collapses to a
product of unit matrices, and we have

\displaystyle(S_{a_{1}a_{2}})^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2},\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{RR^{\prime}}P_{R}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}\,C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}\phantom{|}}^{\underline{v}_{1}\underline{v}_{2},\underline{w}_{1}\underline{w}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2}}\,P_{R^{\prime}}^{\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2}}}{m(R)\,m(R^{\prime})}\,.

(190)

Projecting this on definite representations for
(\underline{r}_{1}\underline{r}_{2})
and
for
(\underline{u}_{1}\underline{u}_{2})
, we simply obtain

\displaystyle{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)

\displaystyle={}^{RR^{\prime}}\!C_{S,a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},Y)\,,

(191)

where the projection
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muC
is defined in analogy to (
81
)
and where we have restored all arguments. In analogy to the case
(
167
) for SPS, we thus find that when all transverse distances
become small, the soft factor for DPS is equal to its matching coefficient and
thus can be entirely computed in perturbation theory. The same then holds for
the Collins-Soper kernel
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
and for the matrix
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
,
which can be constructed from
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!S
as shown in
appendix
A
. An explicit check of this result is the
fact that the one-loop expression for
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K
in
section
7.2
is free of infrared divergences, as is the
two-loop soft factor for DPS computed in
Vladimirov:2016qkd
.

Figure 8:
Simplification in the extended soft factor

S_{qq}
with all Wilson lines at the same transverse position, using the
identity in figure
2
and the unitarity of
Wilson lines. Analogous relations hold for indices in the adjoint colour
representation.

We now turn to the short-distance limit of the collinear matrix elements,
which can be discussed in close analogy to the small-
\boldsymbol{y}
limit of
collinear DPDs in section 3.3 of
Diehl:2017kgu
. In the limit of
small
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
, the unsubtracted distributions

{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{\text{us}}
are given by short-distance coefficients times
proton matrix elements of operators with all fields at transverse
position
\boldsymbol{0}
. The latter are given by collinear parton distributions
of different twist. Multiplying with the perturbative expression of

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!s
, one obtains the expansion of
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF
. Adapting the
notation in
Diehl:2017kgu
, we write
11
11

11

The distributions on
the r.h.s. of (
192
) are respectively denoted
by
F_{\text{spl,pt}}
,
F_{\text{int,pt}}
and
F_{\text{tw3,pt}}
in

Diehl:2017kgu
. For brevity we omit the specification
“pt” here.

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{spl}}+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{tw3}}+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{int}}\,,

(192)

where the short-distance expansion of the terms on the r.h.s. involves
proton matrix elements of operators with twist two, twist three and twist
four, respectively. Following the discussion in section 2.1 of

Diehl:2017kgu
, we neglect the contribution with collinear
twist-three distributions, which for unpolarised protons are chiral odd.
They have no dynamic cross talk with gluon distributions and are therefore
expected to be small compared with twist-two and twist-four distributions
at small
x
.

The splitting contribution
F_{\text{spl}}
describes the case where a
single parton splits into partons
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, as shown in
figure
6
a at lowest order in
\alpha_{s}
. It can be obtained
from (
154
) by taking the limit of small
\boldsymbol{Z}
. For the

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
term, this simply corresponds to replacing
the TMD
f_{a_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z})
by the PDF
f_{a_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2})
. Since
the proton is unpolarised, the parton
a_{0}
in the PDF is unpolarised as
well. We thus obtain

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{spl}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{l^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{2}}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi^{2}}\;{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}^{ll^{\prime}}\biggl(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{2}}\biggr)\,\frac{f_{a_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2};\mu)}{x_{1}+x_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,,

(193)

where we have restored the dependence on the UV renormalisation scale,
taken equal for the partons with momentum fraction
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. A

\zeta
dependence appears only at order
\alpha_{s}^{2}
. To avoid
large logarithms of the higher-order terms in the region of small

|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|\sim|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\sim|\boldsymbol{y}|
, any choice where
1/\mu

and
1/\sqrt{\zeta}
are of the order of these distances will do. A
particular choice is given in (
200
) below.

The term
F_{\text{int}}
in (
192
) is referred to as the
“intrinsic” contribution to the DPD and may be thought of as describing
parton pairs
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
in the “intrinsic” proton wave function. It
is the only contribution starting at order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
and reads

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{int}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle={}^{R}G_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{1};\mu)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(194)

where
{}^{R}G
denotes a collinear twist-four distribution. The
lowest-order term is simply obtained by setting

\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{0}
in the matrix element (
3.1
)
(this must be done before renormalisation and hence in
D=4-2\epsilon

dimensions). The
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
term involves short-distance
interactions and is briefly discussed in section 3.3 of

Diehl:2017kgu
.

Inserting (
192
) with (
193
) and
(
194
) into the cross section formula (
5
), we
can derive the power behaviour of the contribution from the region

|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|\sim|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\sim|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim 1/q_{T}
, using that
the twist-four distribution scales like
G\sim\Lambda^{2}
. For the
scaled DPS cross section we obtain

\displaystyle Q^{4}\,\frac{d\sigma_{\text{DPS}}}{d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}\bigg|_{\text{small $y$}}

\displaystyle\sim\begin{cases}\alpha_{s}^{2}/q_{T}^{2}&\text{from $F_{\text{spl}}\times F_{\text{spl}}$ \penalty\ (1v1)}\\
\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5mu\Lambda^{2}/q_{T}^{4}&\text{from $F_{\text{spl}}\times F_{\text{int}}$ \penalty\ (2v1)}\\
\Lambda^{4}/q_{T}^{6}&\text{from $F_{\text{int}}\times F_{\text{int}}$ \penalty\ (2v2)}\\
\end{cases}

(195)

where we have also indicated the lowest order
in
\alpha_{s}
for each contribution. For the contribution from large

|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim 1/\Lambda
discussed in section
6.2
, we
have

\displaystyle Q^{4}\,\frac{d\sigma_{\text{DPS}}}{d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}}\bigg|_{\text{large $y$}}

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda^{2}/q_{T}^{4}\,,

(196)

as follows from the small-distance expansion (
6.2.1
) and the power
behaviour
F\sim\Lambda^{2}
of a DPDF at large
\boldsymbol{y}
. We note that
(
195
) and (
196
) do not hold in the kinematic
region

|\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}|\sim\Lambda
, which we have excluded from our
considerations, and which has a different power counting as seen from table 1
in
Diehl:2011yj
. Notice also that some parton combinations, such as

(a_{1}a_{2})=(d\bar{u})
, cannot be obtained by parton splitting at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
. In this case,
F_{\text{spl}}
starts
at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
and the powers of
\alpha_{s}
in
(
195
) must be adjusted accordingly.

The three contributions in (
195
) are referred to as 1v1, 2v1
and 2v2 terms as indicated. The 1v1 (read “one versus one”) term is
associated with graphs as in figure
5
a, where one parton in
each proton initiates a sequence of short-distance interactions (a splitting
at scale
q_{T}
followed by hard scattering at scale
Q
). The 2v2 term
corresponds to graphs where two “intrinsic” partons in each proton
directly enter the hard subprocesses, as shown in figure
9
a. In
the 2v1 term one starts with two “intrinsic” partons in one proton,
whereas in the other proton one starts with one parton that splits into two
at scale
q_{T}
. This is shown in figure
9
b. It is natural to
associate the term in (
196
) with 2v2 as well, since at large

\boldsymbol{y}
one cannot identify any perturbative splitting contribution.

Figure 9:
Counterparts to the 1v1 graph in
figure
5
a. In the 2v2 graph (a) there is no
perturbative splitting, and in the 2v1 graph (b) one has a perturbative
splitting in one of the two protons. The box encloses the DTMD of the
upper proton and indicates that the splittings take place at transverse
distances of order
1/q_{T}
.

Among the terms in (
195
) and (
196
), only the
term with
F_{\text{spl}}\times F_{\text{spl}}
is leading as far as powers
of
\Lambda^{2}/q_{T}^{2}
are concerned. However, the other terms involve fewer
powers in
\alpha_{s}
, and they rise more strongly as the momentum fractions

x
become small, because one roughly expects the intrinsic part of the DPDF
to grow like the square of a single PDF in that limit. From this perspective,
one may still discard the term
F_{\text{int}}\times F_{\text{int}}
, which
is power suppressed compared with the large-
\boldsymbol{y}
contribution but has
the same power in
\alpha_{s}
and a similar small
x
behaviour. However, the
inclusion of this term in the cross section will in general not require much
additional effort, and it renders the combination of the contributions from
small and large
\boldsymbol{y}
more straightforward (see
section
6.4
).

The expansion of the DPDFs
F(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
for small
\boldsymbol{y}
proceeds
in exactly the same manner. It can be obtained from
(
193
) and (
194
) by setting

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
before performing UV renormalisation
and setting
D=4
. This gives

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{spl}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}^{l}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}^{l^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{y}^{4}}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi^{2}}\;{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}^{ll^{\prime}}\biggl(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+x_{2}}\biggr)\,\frac{f_{a_{0}}(x_{1}+x_{2};\mu)}{x_{1}+x_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,,

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{int}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle={}^{R}G_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{1};\mu)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(197)

where for
F_{\text{int}}
the difference between (
194
) and
(
6.3.1
) only appears at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
. We note that
only the symmetric part of
T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}^{ll^{\prime}}
survives the
contraction with
\boldsymbol{y}^{l}\mskip 1.5mu\boldsymbol{y}^{l^{\prime}}
. As a consequence,
combinations of
a_{1},a_{2}
with exactly one longitudinal polarisation give
zero, since one then has
T^{ll^{\prime}}\propto\epsilon^{ll^{\prime}}
(see
section
7.4
).

Little is known in phenomenology about twist-four distributions. The
computation using models of light-cone wave functions in
Braun:2011aw

can give guidance at large but not at small
x
. If one has a model for

{F}_{\text{int}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
and is content with leading-order accuracy
in
\alpha_{s}
, one may combine (
194
) and (
6.3.1
)
to approximate

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{int},\,a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{int},\,a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\mu,\zeta)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})

(198)

and use the intrinsic part of the DPDF on the r.h.s. in the small-
\boldsymbol{y}

contribution to the cross section, without further approximation. The
scales
\mu
and
\zeta
should of course be chosen appropriately.

6.3.2
Evolved DPDs and cross section

Inserting the decomposition (
192
) of the DTMDs into
(
5.1
) and neglecting twist-three distributions, we
obtain

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i},\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}\zeta/x_{2}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{0})\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 15.00002pt+\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{x_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/x_{1}}}{\mu}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{0})\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{R^{\prime}}{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\,\biggl[M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\mskip 1.5mu\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}}\,\biggr]\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\Bigl[{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{spl}}({x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})+{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{int}}({x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\Bigr]

(199)

as our master formula for DTMDs at small
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
and small
\boldsymbol{y}
.
For the scales in the DTMDs we take

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\mu_{Z}\,,

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}

\displaystyle=\mu_{Z}^{2}

(200)

with
\mu_{Z}
defined as in (
181
). We can then use the
short-distance approximations (
193
) and
(
194
) for
F_{\text{spl}}
and
F_{\text{int}}
. The
choice (
200
) will turn out to be well suited for
combining the different contributions to the overall cross section.
In contrast to (
6.2.2
), we have taken the two
renormalisation scales
\mu_{01}
and
\mu_{02}
equal in
(
6.3.2
), given that we do not have an optimised
short-distance expansion of
{F}
for different scales. One can however
optimise the resummation of logarithms in the exponent by writing

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{0})

\displaystyle={}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{01})+\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{01}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)

(201)

and likewise for
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}
. A natural choice for the additional
scales is
\mu_{0i}=\mu_{z_{i}}
, as already suggested for
(
6.2.2
).
Inserting the evolved DTMDs into (
5.1
), we have

\displaystyle W_{\text{small $y$}}

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl\{\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{1}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{1}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\mu_{0})\,\log\frac{Q_{1}\mskip 1.5muQ_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;+\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\biggl[\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)-\gamma_{K,a_{2}}(\mu)\,\log\frac{{Q_{2}^{2}}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr]+{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\mu_{0})\,\log\frac{Q_{1}\mskip 1.5muQ_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{RR^{\prime}}\Bigl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2},\,\text{spl}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{b_{1}b_{2},\,\text{int}}(\bar{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{+})\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y}_{-})\,\eta_{a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!\exp\,\biggl[M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\log\frac{Q_{1}\mskip 1.5muQ_{2}}{\zeta_{0}}\,\biggr]\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigl[{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{spl}}({x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})+{}^{R^{\prime}}\!F_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\text{int}}({x}_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\Bigr]

(202)

at the cross section level.

At present, one can only use the leading order (LO) expressions
(
193
) and (
194
) of the DTMD expansions,
because the next-to-leading order (NLO) terms have not been computed for
either case. The leading
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
term of
F_{\text{spl}}

depends on
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
but not on
\boldsymbol{Z}
, and the leading

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{0})
expression for
F_{\text{int}}
is independent
of any transverse distance. A
\boldsymbol{Z}
dependence via
\mu_{0}
and

\zeta_{0}
cancels in
W_{\text{small $y$}}
, up to orders in
\alpha_{s}

beyond the accuracy of the calculation. However,
W_{\text{small $y$}}

acquires a dependence on
\boldsymbol{Z}
through the
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
dependence of the Collins-Soper kernels

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{0})
,

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{0})
and

M_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
. Since these kernels start at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
, the corresponding
\boldsymbol{Z}
dependence comes
with a large logarithm
\log(Q_{1}Q_{2}/\zeta_{0})
for each power of
\alpha_{s}

and is thus enhanced compared to the
\alpha_{s}
corrections in

F_{\text{spl}}
and
F_{\text{int}}
, so that it is consistent to keep
the former while neglecting the latter. In this way, rapidity evolution
provides a nontrivial
\boldsymbol{Z}
dependence and thus a nontrivial
dependence on the conjugate momentum
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
in the
cross section, even if one uses the short-distance expansions
(
193
) and (
194
) at leading order. In
physical terms, this is because the exponential in
(
6.3.2
) resums graphs for the soft factor that have a
real gluon emission enhanced by large rapidity logarithms, even though the
graphs giving the leading-order expansions of
F_{\text{spl}}
and

F_{\text{int}}
have no real gluon emission into the final state.

6.4
Combining large and small
\boldsymbol{y}

As we have seen in the previous subsections, the different regions of

\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
contributing to the cross section
involve different approximations, whose results are given in (
6.2.2
)
and (
6.3.2
). An important point is that the approximated
expressions give leading-power contributions not only in the regions for which
they are designed, but also outside these regions. With the short-distance
behaviour
F(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y})\sim 1/\boldsymbol{y}^{2}
following from
(
6.3.1
) we see for instance that the integral of

W_{\text{large $y$}}
over
\boldsymbol{y}
extends down to the smallest
values allowed by the cutoff function
\Phi(\nu\boldsymbol{y})
, where the
approximations used to obtain the expression are clearly invalid.

To deal with this problem we adapt the subtraction formalism discussed in
chapter 10 of
Collins:2011zzd
, which we briefly sketch now. The
formalism is formulated for a given Feynman graph
\Gamma
in momentum space.
It starts with the smallest regions of loop momenta and works its way towards
increasingly larger regions. In this context, a region
r^{\prime}
is called smaller
than
r
(i.e.
r^{\prime}<r
) if hard momenta in
r
are collinear or soft in
r^{\prime}
,
or if collinear momenta in
r^{\prime}
are soft in
r
. A more general definition of
the relation
r^{\prime}<r
is discussed in chapters 5.4.1 and 10.1.3 of

Collins:2011zzd
. For each region
r
that gives a leading contribution
to the cross section, a set of approximations
T_{r}
is applied to the graph.
In each approximated term
T_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma
one still integrates over

all
loop momenta, thus avoiding momentum cutoffs. This leads to double
counting of contributions from the overlap between different regions, which
are recursively removed by subtraction terms. The full graph is then
approximated by

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{r}C_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma

with

\displaystyle C_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma

\displaystyle=T_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma-\sum_{r^{\prime}<r}T_{r}\mskip 1.5muC_{r^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma\,.

(203)

C_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma
provides a valid approximation of
\Gamma
in the region
r

and in all smaller regions
r^{\prime}<r
, thanks to the subtraction terms

T_{r}\mskip 1.5muC_{r^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma
, which are obtained by applying the approximations
for both
r
and the smaller regions. In the case where one has only two
regions
r^{\prime}<r
, the subtraction term is simply
T_{r}\mskip 1.5muT_{r^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma
. In

Collins:2011zzd
it was shown that if the approximation
T_{r}
is good up
to power corrections in
\Lambda/Q
in its design region
r
, then

\sum_{r}C_{r}\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma
is good with the same accuracy for the full graph.

In
Diehl:2017kgu
, this formalism was applied to the double counting
problem between single and double parton scattering, leading to the
formulation we already presented in section
5.3
. At
variance to the original formulation in
Collins:2011zzd
, momentum
regions in (
203
) were replaced by regions of the transverse
distances
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
, starting from the region of large
distances and going towards small ones in the recursive construction of
subtraction terms.

We now use the same procedure to remove the double counting between the
regions of “large” and “small”
\boldsymbol{y}
as characterised in
table
1
. The treatment of the regions where

|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|
or
|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|
are of order
1/Q
is postponed to the next
subsection. It is always understood that
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|
and
|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|

are in the perturbative region, of order
1/q_{T}
, as discussed in
section
6.1
.

region

power counting

approximations

DPS, large
\boldsymbol{y}

|\boldsymbol{y}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\sim 1/\Lambda

|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|,1/\Lambda

DPS, small
\boldsymbol{y}

|\boldsymbol{y}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\sim 1/q_{T}

|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|,|\boldsymbol{y}|\ll 1/\Lambda

SPS

|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\sim 1/Q

|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\ll|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\ll 1/\Lambda

Table 1:
Regions of
\boldsymbol{y}
discussed in the
text. For power counting,
|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\sim 1/q_{T}
is always assumed.

According to the general construction, the subtraction term for large

\boldsymbol{y}
in the small-
\boldsymbol{y}
term is obtained by applying the
approximations in the first and second row of table
1
,
i.e. by using both
|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
and
|\boldsymbol{y}|\ll 1/\Lambda
. For a given Feynman graph (and thus for any finite sum over
graphs) the order in which these approximations are made does not matter.
This suggests that we can start from either
W_{\text{large $y$}}
or

W_{\text{small $y$}}
and then apply the approximation for the other
region. At this point, we must however note that the expressions of
W
are
not for fixed-order graphs but contain logarithms resummed to all orders in
the strong coupling. This is reflected in the scale dependence of

\alpha_{s}
and of the nonperturbative functions. We must therefore make
sure that the choice of scales in the different terms is compatible with the
way in which the subtraction formalism works. In
W_{\text{small $y$}}
,
this concerns the scales
\mu_{0}
and
\zeta_{0}
at which one evaluates the
short-distance expansions (
193
) and (
194
) of
DTMDs at fixed order in
\alpha_{s}
.

We now define the double counting subtraction term as

\displaystyle W_{\text{sub}}

\displaystyle=W_{\text{small $y$}}\,\bigl|_{\text{approx.\penalty\ for}\penalty\ |\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|}

(204)

with the small-
\boldsymbol{y}
expression for
W
given in (
6.3.2
). We
should take the limit
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
in all
parts of that expression. In particular, this means replacing

\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
with
\boldsymbol{y}
in
\Phi
and in the leading-order term of
the short-distance expansion (
193
) of
F_{\text{spl}}
, and
replacing
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M_{a_{1}a_{2}}
with
\delta_{RR^{\prime}}\,\bigl[{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}+{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}-{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{1}}-{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a_{2}}+{}^{R}\!J\bigr]

according to (
133
) and (
171
).

We note at this point that the integral of (
6.3.2
) over
\boldsymbol{y}

is actually divergent at large
\boldsymbol{y}
. Up to logarithmic corrections,
the 2v2 term
F_{\text{int}}\times F_{\text{int}}
is constant and the 2v1
term
F_{\text{spl}}\times F_{\text{int}}
goes like
1/\boldsymbol{y}^{2}
in that
limit, and for
R=R^{\prime}=1
there is no large-
\boldsymbol{y}
suppression from the
exponential since
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muJ=0
. The construction (
204
)
ensures that the region
|\boldsymbol{y}|\gg 1/q_{T}
cancels in
W_{\text{small
$\boldsymbol{y}$}}-W_{\text{sub}}
. When evaluating the cross section
numerically, one may want to use an upper cutoff of order
1/\Lambda
on the
integral over
|\boldsymbol{y|}
, thus avoiding to evaluate terms in a region
where they cancel anyway.

For the subtraction formalism to work, we need that

\displaystyle W_{\text{sub}}

\displaystyle\approx W_{\text{large
$\boldsymbol{y}$}}

for
|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|

(205)

up to terms in
\alpha_{s}
that are beyond the accuracy of the calculation. In
the specified region of
\boldsymbol{y}
, we can use the short-distance limit of

{J}(\boldsymbol{y})
and of the DPDFs in (
6.2.2
), the latter being
obtained by adding
F_{\text{spl}}
and
F_{\text{int}}
from
(
6.3.1
). If one retains only
the leading-order terms for
F_{\text{spl}}+F_{\text{int}}
on both sides of
(
205
), one finds manifest equality of the two expressions
up to differences due to scale dependence, which we will discuss shortly.

The full DPS cross section is then obtained from

\displaystyle W_{\text{DPS}}(\nu)

\displaystyle=W_{\text{large $y$}}(\nu^{\prime})-W_{\text{sub}}(\nu^{\prime})+W_{\text{small $y$}}(\nu)\,,

(206)

where we make explicit the choice of cutoff parameters for the
\boldsymbol{y}

integration, taking
\nu^{\prime}\sim q_{T}
and
\nu\sim Q
. In the region

|\boldsymbol{y}|\gg|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
the last two terms cancel by
virtue of (
204
), and one is left with the first term, which
is designed to give a correct approximation of the cross section there. For

|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|
, the first and second terms
cancel according to (
205
), and the third term gives a correct
approximation of the cross section. In this way,
W_{\text{DPS}}
leads to a
correct approximation of the cross section for
|\boldsymbol{y}|
of order
1/q_{T}

and larger.

We can take a more restrictive cutoff parameter
\nu^{\prime}\sim q_{T}
in the first
two terms, because according to (
205
) the sum of these terms
is already suppressed for
|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim 1/q_{T}
, and the dependence on the
exact value of
\nu^{\prime}
is thus beyond the accuracy of the calculation. Taking

\nu^{\prime}
lower than
Q
is also more economical for numerical calculations since
it excludes a
\boldsymbol{y}
region that is not needed. By contrast,
\nu
in the
last term should be of order
Q
(see the next subsection).

Returning to the issue of scale dependence, we should first specify what is
to be used for the nonperturbative quantities in the expression
(
6.2.2
) of
W_{\text{large $y$}}
. For the Collins-Soper kernel
we take the form in (
183
) with
J(\boldsymbol{y}^{*};\mu_{y},\mu_{y})

and
\gamma_{J}
evaluated at fixed perturbative order; this ensures the
correct small-
\boldsymbol{y}
limit. Using the DGLAP equations, the DPDFs are to
be evolved from
\mu_{0i}
to
\mu_{0}=\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}=\mu_{y}
, and it is at
that scale that one makes an ansatz which at small
\boldsymbol{y}
tends to the
fixed-order form specified by (
6.3.1
).
12
12

12

An explicit
example for such an ansatz in the colour singlet sector can be found in
section 9.2 of
Diehl:2017kgu
.

As usual, this scale choice ensures that higher-order corrections are not
accompanied by large logarithms.

We now recall that in
W_{\text{small $y$}}
, and hence by construction
also in
W_{\text{sub}}
, we take
\mu_{0}=\sqrt{\zeta_{0}}=\mu_{Z}
for the
scales at which we perform the short-distance expansion of the DTMDs. The
difference between the two sides in (
205
) is thus due to
different scales of the short-distance matching and to the different scales at
which the DPDs and the Collins-Soper kernels are evaluated in
(
6.2.2
) and (
6.3.2
). However, all these scales,
\mu_{y}
,

\mu_{z_{i}}
and
\mu_{Z}
are of the same order in the region

|\boldsymbol{y}|\sim|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|
where we need (
205
). Using
the same methods as in section 6.2 of
Diehl:2017kgu
, one can show that
the approximation (
205
) holds up to higher-order terms in

\alpha_{s}
. These terms are not accompanied by large logarithms and beyond
the accuracy of the calculation.

6.5
Combining DPS with SPS at short distances

In section
5.3
we recalled how to combine DPS, SPS
and their interference in TMD factorisation at generic values of
q_{T}
. We
now derive the form of the double counting subtraction terms for the case
where
q_{T}\gg\Lambda
. We restrict ourselves to the lowest order of

\alpha_{s}
in the perturbative short-distance coefficients. An extension to
higher orders is beyond the scope of the present work.

Let us start by discussing the relevant transverse distance scales and the
approximations they imply. We recall that the DPS cross section for
q_{T}\gg\Lambda
is dominated by distances
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll 1/\Lambda
thanks to the Fourier exponentials in (
5
). In full
analogy, the Fourier exponentials in (
5.3
) and
(
5.3
) ensure that
\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}
is dominated by

|\boldsymbol{Z}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/\Lambda
and
\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}
by

|\boldsymbol{Z}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\ll 1/\Lambda
. Likewise,
\sigma_{\text{SPS}}

is dominated by
|\boldsymbol{Z}|\ll 1/\Lambda
, where in the following we use
the variable
\boldsymbol{Z}
instead of
\boldsymbol{z}
in the SPS formula
(
1
).
According to the formalism recalled in the previous subsection, the
subtraction terms for the overlap between double and single parton
scattering are obtained by applying the approximations appropriate for the
SPS region to the DPS term in the cross section. A corresponding
prescription holds for the SPS/DPS interference. The limits
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}\to\boldsymbol{0}
,
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}\to\boldsymbol{0}
or
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}\to\boldsymbol{0}
in the
subtraction terms hence imply that these distances are taken to be much
smaller than
1/q_{T}
, because
q_{T}
is set to zero when one computes the
hard scattering process for SPS.
We thus obtain the approximations given in table
2
.
The constraint on
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
in the third row results from
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}=\boldsymbol{y}_{-}+(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2})
and the constraints on
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
,

\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}
. We thus find that all terms in the overall
cross section (
5.3
) are dominated by distances in the
perturbative region, except for
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
.

partial cross section

approximations

\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}

|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/\Lambda

\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},\,y_{+}\to 0}

|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/q_{T}

\sigma_{\text{DPS},\,y_{-}\to 0}

|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\ll 1/q_{T}
,
|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/\Lambda

\sigma_{\text{DPS},\,y_{\pm}\to 0}

|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\ll 1/q_{T}

Table 2:
Conditions on
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
for different
terms in the overall cross section (
5.3
) if
q_{T}\gg\Lambda
. Analogous relations hold for
\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}
and
the associated subtraction terms. In all cases one has
|\boldsymbol{Z}|\ll 1/\Lambda
.

In all terms that are dominated by short distances, we can use the
perturbative splitting expressions (
152
) and
(
153
) of the twist-three TMDs and the DTMDs. Notice that
the LO terms in these expressions have a factorised dependence on

\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
,
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
, so that they do not depend on the
relative size of these distances (this will no longer hold at NLO).
Moreover, we can take the short-distance limit of the TMDs

f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{Z};\mu,\zeta)
. At LO accuracy we can simply replace them with
collinear PDFs
f_{a}(x;\mu)
, provided that
\mu^{2}\sim\zeta\sim 1/\boldsymbol{Z}^{2}
. Collinear PDFs are then the only nonperturbative input
necessary to compute the cross section, apart of course from the DPDs and
Collins-Soper kernels
J
in
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
. In a complete
treatment, one would also have collinear twist-three distributions, but we
neglect these as explained in section
5.3
.

From the dependence on
\boldsymbol{y}_{\pm}
of the splitting expressions
(
152
) and (
153
) and from the cross section
formulae (
1
), (
5.3
) and (
5.3
) we deduce
that the SPS cross section, the SPS/DPS interference and all associated
subtraction terms have the same power behaviour as the 1v1 term of the DPS
cross section in (
195
), namely

Q^{4}\,d\sigma/(d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\,d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2})\sim 1/q_{T}^{2}
.

We must now take a closer look at the scale dependence of the distributions.
Let us denote by
\mu_{h}
the hard scale chosen in the computation of the SPS
amplitude, and by
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
the scales for the two DPS amplitudes.
To simplify the presentation, we first assume that all hard scales are
equal,
\mu=\mu_{h}=\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
. The adaptation to the case of
different hard scales is discussed in appendix
C
.
As we have explicitly shown for single and double parton TMDs, the evolution
in
\mu
and
\zeta
of these distributions is multiplicative:

\displaystyle f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=E_{2;a}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta;\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,f_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,,

\displaystyle F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=E_{4;a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu,\zeta;\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,F_{a_{1}a_{2}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,,

(207)

where it is understood that
F
is a vector and the evolution factor
E_{4}
a
matrix in the space of colour representations
R
. From the discussion at the
end of section
5.3
if follows that for the twist-three
TMDs, we have

\displaystyle D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=E_{3;\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu,\zeta;\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\,D_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu_{0},\zeta_{0})\rule[-7.74998pt]{0.0pt}{0.0pt}

(208)

and an analogous equation for
D_{\alpha_{0}|\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}
.

Let us now specify the evolution and scale dependence of the distributions
appearing in
\sigma_{\text{SPS}}
, the interference terms and

\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
in the region where all transverse distances are small
compared with
1/\Lambda
. In a schematic notation, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{SPS}}:

\displaystyle f(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu)

\displaystyle\sim E_{2}(\mu;\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}:

\displaystyle D(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{+},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu)

\displaystyle\sim E_{3}(\mu;\mu_{Z})\,U(\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}:

\displaystyle D(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{-},\boldsymbol{Z};\mu)

\displaystyle\sim E_{3}(\mu;\mu_{Z})\,U^{*}(\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS}}:

\displaystyle F(x_{i},\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu)

\displaystyle\sim E_{4}(\mu;\mu_{Z})\,U(\mu_{Z})\,U^{*}(\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})+F_{\text{int}}(\mu)\,,

(209)

where
f(\mu_{Z})
on the r.h.s. is a collinear PDF. We have specified only
the splitting part
F_{\text{spl}}
of
F
, because the detailed form of

F_{\text{int}}
will not be needed. For brevity we have omitted parton
labels, as well as momentum fraction and transverse position arguments on
the r.h.s. The factors
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}/\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{l^{\prime}}/\boldsymbol{y}_{-}^{2}
which accompany the splitting kernels
U(\mu_{Z})
and

U^{*}(\mu_{Z})
have been omitted as well.
For brevity, we display neither the rapidity parameter
\zeta
in the
evolved distributions nor the starting value
\zeta_{0}
. For the latter, we
follow (
200
) and take
\zeta_{0}=\mu_{Z}^{2}
. Since
\zeta

is defined by (
125
), it must be rescaled by
(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta/(x_{1}x_{2})
when used in
f(x_{1}+x_{2},\boldsymbol{Z})
and in
E_{2}
. The same
forms as in (
6.5
) are to be taken for the distributions
in the left-moving proton, with
x_{i}
replaced by
\bar{x}_{i}
and
\zeta
by

\bar{\zeta}
.

The subtraction terms should be such that, up to higher orders in
\alpha_{s}

and up to power corrections, the overall cross section (
5.3
)
is given by

\displaystyle d\sigma_{\text{DPS}}

\displaystyle\text{ \,for\, }|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\sim|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\gg 1/\nu\,,

\displaystyle d\sigma_{\text{SPS}}

\displaystyle\text{ \,for\, }|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\sim|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\sim 1/\nu\,,

\displaystyle d\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}

\displaystyle\text{ \,for\, }|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\gg|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\sim 1/\nu\,,

\displaystyle d\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}

\displaystyle\text{ \,for\, }|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\gg|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\sim 1/\nu\,,

(210)

where it is understood that
\nu\sim Q
. In the subtraction terms,
renormalisation and rapidity scales should be chosen such that the
cancellations required to avoid double counting are not spoiled by large
logarithms at higher orders.

To achieve this, we take so-called profile scales, which vary smoothly
between
\mu_{Z}
and
\mu
as a function of the appropriate transverse
distance. We therefore introduce a profile function
p
that satisfies

\displaystyle p({u};\mu_{a},\mu_{b})

\displaystyle\approx\mu_{a}

for
{u}\sim 1
,

\displaystyle p({u};\mu_{a},\mu_{b})

\displaystyle\approx\mu_{b}

for
{u}\gg 1
;

(211)

a concrete example is given below. For the subtraction terms between DPS
and the SPS/DPS interference, we now take

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},y_{+}\to 0}

\displaystyle:D\sim E_{3}(\mu;\hat{\mu})\,U(\hat{\mu})\,E_{2}(\hat{\mu};\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})

\displaystyle\mbox{with }\hat{\mu}=p\bigl(\nu\mskip 1.5mu|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|;\mu_{Z},\mu\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS},y_{-}\to 0}

\displaystyle:D\sim E_{3}(\mu;\hat{\mu})\,U^{*}(\hat{\mu})\,E_{2}(\hat{\mu};\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})

\displaystyle\mbox{with }\hat{\mu}=p\bigl(\nu\mskip 1.5mu|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|;\mu_{Z},\mu\bigr)\,.

(212)

This corresponds to a two-step matching: at the scale
\mu_{Z}
a twist-two
TMD is matched onto a PDF. The twist-two TMD is then evolved up to the
scale
\hat{\mu}
, where a twist-three TMD is matched onto the twist-two TMD
using the lowest order term in (
152
). The twist-three TMD is
then evolved up to the final scale
\mu
. It is understood that at the
intermediate scale
\hat{\mu}
the rapidity parameter is taken equal to

\displaystyle\widehat{\zeta}

\displaystyle=p(u;\mu_{Z}^{2},\zeta)\,,

(213)

where the first argument
u
is the same as the first argument in

\hat{\mu}
.
We thus find that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS},y_{+}\to 0}\approx\begin{cases}\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}&\mbox{for $|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\sim 1/\nu$}\\
\sigma_{\text{SPS}}&\mbox{for $|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\gg 1/\nu$}\end{cases}

(214)

where we have used that for distances
|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|\gg 1/Q
the hard
scattering amplitude for SPS can be written as

\displaystyle H_{\alpha_{0}\beta_{0}}\big|_{y_{+}\gg 1/Q}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{k}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{l}}{\boldsymbol{y}_{+}^{2}}\;U^{k}_{\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\,U^{l}_{\beta_{0}\to\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\,H_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\,H_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}

(215)

at leading order in
\alpha_{s}
. To make this relation explicit, one needs to
Fourier transform the appropriate transverse momentum in the SPS graph to
position space. For diagrams that have kinematic overlap between SPS and the
SPS/DPS interference, but not with DPS, such as the one in
figure
5
b, the behaviour in (
214
) and in its
analogue for
\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS},y_{-}\to 0}
solve the double counting
problem completely. If there is also overlap with the DPS region, as in the
three graphs of figure
5
, then we need the DPS subtraction
terms, where we choose scales as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{-}\to 0}

\displaystyle:F\sim E_{4}(\mu;\hat{\mu})\,U^{*}(\hat{\mu})\,E_{3}(\hat{\mu};\mu_{Z})\,U(\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{+}\to 0}

\displaystyle:F\sim E_{4}(\mu;\hat{\mu})\,U(\hat{\mu})\,E_{3}(\hat{\mu};\mu_{Z})\,U^{*}(\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{\pm}\to 0}

\displaystyle:F\sim E_{4}(\mu;\hat{\mu})\,U(\hat{\mu})\ U^{*}(\hat{\mu})\,E_{2}(\hat{\mu};\mu_{Z})\,f(\mu_{Z})

(216)

with

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}

\displaystyle=p\bigl(\nu\min\{|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|,|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|\};\mu_{Z},\mu\bigr)\,.

(217)

In all three terms we have a two-step matching, with a twist-three TMD
at the intermediate stage in the first two cases and with a twist-two TMD in
the third case. If both
|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|
and
|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|
are large
compared with
1/\nu
, we thus have

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{-}\to 0}

\displaystyle\approx\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{+}\to 0}

\displaystyle\approx\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{DPS},y_{\pm}\to 0}

\displaystyle\approx\sigma_{\text{SPS}}

(218)

so that the sum in (
5.3
) leaves only
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
,
as it should.
It remains to discuss the regions where one or both of
|\boldsymbol{y}_{+}|
and

|\boldsymbol{y}_{-}|
is of order
1/\nu
. In each of these regions,

\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
can be obtained from
W_{\text{small $y$}}
, because

|\boldsymbol{y}|\ll 1/\Lambda
. Furthermore, the integral over
W_{\text{small
$y$}}
in these regions is dominated by the 1v1 term, with the 2v1 and
2v2 terms being power suppressed by at least a relative factor
\Lambda^{2}/(\nu q_{T})
. This follows from the dependence of
F_{\text{spl}}
and

F_{\text{int}}
on
\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
. Finally, one finds that
all three terms in (
6.5
) are approximately equal to
the 1v1 term in
\sigma_{\text{DPS}}
, which is thus cancelled in the
combination (
5.3
). This leaves us with

\sigma_{\text{SPS}}
,
\sigma_{\text{DPS/SPS}}
or

\sigma_{\text{SPS/DPS}}
as appropriate. Overall we thus have achieved a
correct description of the cross section in all relevant regions of

\boldsymbol{y}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-}
.

Profile scales have been used in other contexts, see
e.g.
Ligeti:2008ac
;
Abbate:2010xh
. For our purpose here, a suitable
profile
p
for
\hat{\mu}
is given in equation (6.33) of

Diehl:2017kgu
. An alternative choice is

\displaystyle p(u;\mu_{a},\mu_{b})=\begin{cases}\mu_{a}&\mbox{ \,for\, }u\leq u_{a}\\
\bigl[1-\frac{1}{2}(1-\cos u^{\prime}\pi)\bigr]\mskip 1.5mu\mu_{a}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\cos u^{\prime}\pi)\,\mu_{b}&\mbox{ \,for\, }u_{a}<u<u_{b}\\
\mu_{b}&\mbox{ \,for\, }u\geq u_{b}\\
\end{cases}

(219)

with

\displaystyle u^{\prime}=\frac{u-u_{a}}{u_{b}-u_{a}}\,.

(220)

The transition points for the
u
dependence should be such that
u_{a}\sim 1
and
u_{b}\gg 1
. A natural choice is
u_{a}={\nu}/{\mu}
and
u_{b}={\nu}/{\mu_{Z}}
.

6.6
Perturbative accuracy

Equations (
2
), (
6.2.2
) and
(
6.3.2
) express TMDs in terms of collinear distributions
and perturbative quantities, and they permit the resummation of large
logarithms in the cross section. In the following, we give a brief overview
of the order in
\alpha_{s}
at which the different quantities are available.
We also take a closer look at the type of logarithms that are resummed at
all orders. To this end, we count all large scales
\mu_{i},\zeta,\nu
as
order
Q
. Logarithms of
|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|
turn into logarithms of

|\boldsymbol{q}_{i}|
after Fourier transformation, and we count
\mu_{i0}\sim 1/|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\sim q_{T}
.

An important point in this context is that a variety of schemes for TMD
factorisation and for the associated resummation are used in the literature.
Some care is needed when converting perturbative expressions from one scheme
to another. A systematic discussion of this issue is given in

Collins:2017oxh
, along with a comprehensive list of higher-order
results for the quark channel in the scheme we use here (referred to as CSS2
in that paper).

SPS

The SPS cross section can be calculated within the single TMD formalism
outlined in section
2
. To compute the spectrum in

\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
, one needs the relevant parton-level cross
sections, e.g. for gauge boson pair production, as a function of the
relative transverse momentum
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
. Logarithms of the
net transverse momentum
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
are resummed by the
exponential in (
2
).

Perturbative ingredients in the cross section are the anomalous dimensions

\gamma_{K}
,
\gamma_{a}
, the Collins-Soper kernel
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK
, the
short-distance (or matching) coefficients
{}^{1}C
, and finally the
coefficient
C_{H}^{2}
of the hard-scattering cross section, defined as

\hat{\sigma}
divided by its tree-level value.
13
13

13

For definiteness we
include the colour-singlet labels on
K
and
C
here, although this is the
only colour channel that appears in SPS.

In table
3
we specify different levels of accuracy,
following the scheme of
Abbate:2010xh
;
Berger:2010xi
.
After expanding
\alpha_{s}(\mu^{\prime})
in
\gamma_{a}(\mu^{\prime})
and
\gamma_{K}(\mu^{\prime})

around
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
, the integral over
\mu^{\prime}
in (
2
)
gives powers of
\log(\mu/\mu_{0})
. We thus find that
\gamma_{K}
goes with
double logarithms
\alpha_{s}(Q)\log^{2}(Q/q_{T})
, whilst
\gamma_{a}
and

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK
go with single logarithms
\alpha_{s}(Q)\log(Q/q_{T})
. One
therefore requires one order higher for
\gamma_{K}
than for
\gamma_{a}
and

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK
(see table
3
). Likewise, one finds that one
needs the same perturbative order for the
\beta
function as for

\gamma_{K}
. At accuracy N
k
LO one then has control over all terms
from
\alpha_{s}^{n}\log^{n+1}
to
\alpha_{s}^{n+k}\log^{n+1}
in the
exponent.
\gamma_{K}
,
\gamma_{a}
and
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK
all start at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
and are known up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
, both
in the quark and in the gluon channel. A compilation of these results can
for instance be found in appendix D of
Echevarria:2016scs
. The
notation in that work is related to the one we are using by

\displaystyle\gamma_{K,a}

\displaystyle=2\mskip 1.5mu\Gamma_{\text{cusp}}^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}

\displaystyle={}-\gamma_{V}^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{a}(\mu,\zeta)

(221)

and

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}(\boldsymbol{z},\mu)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{D}^{a}(\mu,\boldsymbol{b}_{T})

\displaystyle\text{with }\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{b}_{T}\,,

(222)

where
a=q,g
and it is understood that the strong coupling is taken at scale

\mu
. Results for the quark channel are also given in

Collins:2017oxh
, where the same notation as here is used (except that
the Collins-Soper kernel is denoted by
\tilde{K}
instead of
K
).

\gamma_{K}

\gamma_{a}

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK
,
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M

{}^{R}C
,
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muC_{\text{int}}

C_{H}^{2}\rule[-4.73611pt]{0.0pt}{4.30554pt}

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP

LL

\alpha_{s}

-

-

\alpha_{s}^{0}

\alpha_{s}^{0}

\alpha_{s}

NLL

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}^{0}

\alpha_{s}^{0}

\alpha_{s}

NLL
′

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

NNLL

\alpha_{s}^{3}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

NNLL
′

\alpha_{s}^{3}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}^{2}

\alpha_{s}^{3}

Table 3:
Different levels of accuracy and the
associated orders of
\alpha_{s}
for the perturbative ingredients in the SPS
cross section. The order required for the
\beta
function is the same as
for
\gamma_{K}
.

At lowest order one has
{}^{1}C_{ab}=\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)
and

C_{H}^{2}=1
. These coefficients are not associated with the resummation of
large logarithms, and conventionally one requires them at one order in

\alpha_{s}
lower than
\gamma_{a}
. This corresponds to counting

\log(Q/q_{T})
as order
1/\alpha_{s}
. Taking them at the same order as

\gamma_{a}
is indicated by a prime in the table. For unpolarised quarks or
gluons,
{}^{1}C
has been computed up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
in

Echevarria:2016scs
and
Gehrmann:2014yya
. With unpolarised
protons this is sufficient for
q\bar{q}
annihilation processes, whereas
for
gg
initiated processes one also requires the coefficient

{}^{1}C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}
for linear gluon polarisation in the TMDs.
This is well known from the process
gg\to H
(see
e.g.
Echevarria:2015uaa
and references therein).

To the best of our knowledge, the hard-scattering coefficient
C_{H}^{2}
has
not been given in the literature for final states relevant in our context,
such as
W^{+}W^{-}
,
ZH
,
HH
, etc. It should however be possible to
extract it from the virtual corrections to the relevant amplitudes, which
are known up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
in some channels (for gauge boson
pair production see e.g.
Gehrmann:2015ora
;
Caola:2015ila
and
references therein). Without this additional effort, the SPS contribution
to the cross section can currently be evaluated at NLL accuracy.

There is one more type of large logarithms hidden in (
2
),
namely the logarithms of
q_{T}/\Lambda
that are resummed by DGLAP evolution
of the PDFs on the r.h.s., from a typical starting scale of order
\Lambda

up to
\mu_{0}\sim 1/|\boldsymbol{z}|
. A customary choice is to take the DGLAP
kernels
{}^{1}P
at one power in
\alpha_{s}
higher than the matching
coefficients
{}^{1}C
, as shown in the table. This amounts to using LO
evolution together with LO matching coefficients, etc. The perturbative
accuracy of
{}^{1}C
is then retained even if
\log(q_{T}/\Lambda)
is as
large as
1/\alpha_{s}
. For unpolarised PDFs, the DGLAP kernels are known up
to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})

Moch:2004pa
;
Vogt:2004mw
.

Interference between SPS and DPS

The perturbative ingredients required for computing the SPS/DPS interference
term, as laid out in section
6.5
, are the Collins-Soper
kernels
K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}
for twist-three TMDs, the splitting
kernels
U
appearing in their short-distance approximation, and the
interference between SPS and DPS hard-scattering amplitudes for the process
under investigation. None of these have been calculated so far. It is easy
to compute the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
expressions of

K_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}|\alpha_{0}}
and
U
by adapting the corresponding
calculations for DPDs in
Diehl:2011yj
. Combining the known
hard-scattering amplitudes for the relevant SPS and DPS processes is a
straightforward exercise (but can be tedious, especially for heavy gauge
bosons due to their three helicities).

DPS: small-
\boldsymbol{y}
contribution

Let us now discuss the perturbative ingredients in the small-
y
expression
(
6.3.2
) for DPS.
\gamma_{K}
,
\gamma_{a}
and
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK
are the
same as in the SPS formula, and in addition one needs the matrix

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M
, which starts at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
and goes with single
logarithms
\log(Q/q_{T})
. The expression for the matching

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{spl}}
on PDFs involves a coefficient

{}^{R}C_{\text{spl}}
, and the matching of
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muF_{\text{int}}
on
twist-four distributions involves a coefficient
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muC_{\text{int}}
.
The lowest order of
{C}_{\text{spl}}
and
{C}_{\text{int}}
is

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
and
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{0})
, respectively — their
explicit definitions are not needed here.
The hard-scattering subprocesses for DPS are simpler than for SPS with the
same final state. For Drell-Yan and for Higgs boson production the
hard-scattering coefficients
C_{H}^{2}
are known up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
.
They can for instance be found in
Collins:2017oxh
for Drell-Yan
production and in
Ebert:2017uel
for both channels.

The evolution kernel
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M
is known at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
in
the two-quark channel
Vladimirov:2016qkd
; its
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})

expressions for all channels are given in section
7.2
. The
matching coefficients
{}^{R}C_{\text{spl}}
have been calculated at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
in
Diehl:2011yj
, and the related kernels

{}^{R}T
are compiled in section
7.4
here.
For
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\mskip-1.5muC_{\text{int}}
, only the trivial order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
is
currently known, which corresponds to matching
F_{\text{int}}=G
. The
kernels for the DGLAP evolution of the distributions
G
are known at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
and given in
Bukhvostov:1985rn
; for a more
general discussion of twist-four evolution we refer to
Braun:2009vc
.
At a given level of accuracy, their order should be the same as the one of

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP
in table
3
.

When denoting the level of perturbative accuracy, one might think of
combining equal orders in
\alpha_{s}
for
C_{\text{spl}}
and

C_{\text{int}}
. On the other hand,
C_{\text{spl}}
starts one order
higher in
\alpha_{s}
than
C_{\text{int}}
, and moreover the different
combinations of
F_{\text{spl}}
and
F_{\text{int}}
in the cross section
have different power behaviour in
\Lambda^{2}/q_{T}^{2}
according to
(
195
). We thus find it more natural to take one power in

\alpha_{s}
more for
C_{\text{spl}}
than for
C_{\text{int}}
. With this
naming convention, the small-
y
expression of DPS can currently be
evaluated to NLL accuracy. Missing ingredients for achieving NNLL in pure
quark channels or NLL
′
in channels with gluons are the matching
coefficients
C_{\text{spl}}
and
C_{\text{int}}
for DTMDs, as well as the
two-loop DGLAP kernels for twist-four evolution.

DPS: large-
\boldsymbol{y}
contribution

We now turn our attention to the large-
y
expression (
6.2.2
) of
DPS. The quantities
\gamma_{K}
,
\gamma_{a}
and
C_{H}^{2}
are the same as in
the small-
y
expressions of DPS. The resummation of single logarithms

\log(Q/q_{T})
requires the Collins-Soper kernels
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK
in the different
colour channels, which can be obtained from the perturbative expression of

{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!M(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})
by taking the limit

|\boldsymbol{z}_{i}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
and using that in this limit one has the
structure (
171
). In the two-quark channel one can thus obtain
the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
expression for
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK
from the results of

Vladimirov:2016qkd
; in all other channels one has the one-loop
expressions, which we list in section
7.2
. For the matching
coefficients
{}^{R}C
we will derive
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
results in
all colour and polarisation channels in the next section; most of them have
been given in the literature before. One can thus evaluate gauge boson pair
production with NNLL accuracy, whereas for DPS processes with one or two Higgs
bosons in the final state, NLL
′
accuracy is currently achievable.

Let us finally take a closer look at the evolution of the DPDFs, which
generates logarithms involving a hadronic starting scale
\Lambda
. The
