situation is now more involved than the one discussed for SPS above. In
analogy to usual DGLAP evolution, there are single logarithms

\log(q_{T}/\Lambda)
, whose resummation requires the
\zeta
independent part
of the evolution kernels
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP
. We will give
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})

expressions for these kernels in all polarisation channels in
section
7.3.5
. The
\zeta
dependence of the DPDFs,
which has no counterpart for ordinary parton distributions, is made explicit
in (
6.2.2
). Expanding
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
in the anomalous
dimension
{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)
around a fixed scale, we can perform
the integral and obtain double logarithms
\log^{2}(Q/\Lambda)-\log^{2}(Q/q_{T})=\log(\Lambda q_{T}/Q^{2})\,\log(\Lambda/q_{T})
in the exponential.
If both
\log(Q/q_{T})
and
\log(q_{T}/\Lambda)
are counted as order

1/\alpha_{s}
, one therefore needs
{}^{R}\gamma_{J}
at the same order as
the cusp anomalous dimension
\gamma_{K}
. For smaller values of

\log(q_{T}/\Lambda)
one may instead be content with
{}^{R}\gamma_{J}
at
the same order as
\gamma_{a}
and
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK
. From the two-loop results in

Vladimirov:2016qkd
one can extract the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
term
of
{}^{8}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}
, whereas for higher colour representations we
currently only know the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
results given in
section
7.2
. We note that in (
6.2.2
) there
are also single logarithms
\log(Q/q_{T})
multiplied with

{}^{R}\!J(\boldsymbol{y})
, which at large
\boldsymbol{y}
is beyond the control of
perturbation theory.

7
One-loop results

In this section, we present the perturbative ingredients necessary for
evaluating DPS at NLL accuracy (see table
3
). Some of the
expressions are quoted from the literature to make the presentation
self-contained, whereas for evolution kernels and matching coefficients we
verify previous and derive new results.

In section
7.1
we discuss the hard-scattering
cross sections for Drell-Yan and for Higgs production via gluon fusion, and in
section
7.2
we give the one-loop expressions of the
Collins-Soper kernels
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}
for all parton and colour
channels. Section
7.3
is devoted to the

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
matching coefficients
{}^{R}C_{ab}
of DTMDs in
the large-
\boldsymbol{y}
region. After explaining a number of computational
details, we present results for all polarisation and colour combinations in
sections
7.3.4
and
7.3.5
. As a corollary
we obtain the one-loop DGLAP kernels (
7.3.5
) for colour non-singlet
DPDFs. In section
7.4
we list the

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
splitting kernels
{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}
of
DTMDs in the small-
\boldsymbol{y}
region, adapting the results of

Diehl:2011yj
to the notation used here.

7.1
TMD hard-scattering cross sections

We define the coefficients
C_{H}^{2}
of the hard-scattering cross section as

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}(Q^{2},\mu^{2})=\hat{\sigma}_{0}(Q^{2})\,C_{H}^{2}(Q^{2},\mu^{2})

(223)

for a given partonic channel, where
\hat{\sigma}_{0}
is the cross section at
lowest order in
\alpha_{s}
. The expressions for
\hat{\sigma}_{0}
can be found
in many places, for instance in
Kasemets:2012pr
for the production of
an electroweak gauge boson
V
and its subsequent decay into a lepton pair,
and in
Echevarria:2015uaa
for Higgs production via
gg
fusion in
the limit of large top mass. The hard-scattering coefficients read

\displaystyle C_{H,\,q\bar{q}\to V}^{2}(Q^{2},\mu^{2})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,\biggl[-\ln^{2}\frac{Q^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+3\ln\frac{Q^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-8+\frac{7\pi^{2}}{6}\mskip 1.5mu\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{H,\,gg\to H}^{2}(Q^{2},\mu^{2})

\displaystyle=\biggl(1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\,C_{A}\,\biggr[-\ln^{2}\frac{Q^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{7\pi^{2}}{6}\mskip 1.5mu\biggr]\biggr)\,\biggl(1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\,\bigl[5C_{A}-3C_{F}\bigr]\biggr)

(224)

and are known up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
, see for instance appendix A of

Ebert:2017uel
. The colour factors are
C_{F}=(N^{2}-1)/(2N)
and
C_{A}=N
as usual. The first factor in
C_{H,\,gg\to H}^{2}
is for two-gluon
fusion via the local current
F^{\mu\nu\mskip 1.5mua}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
and the second
factor for the coupling of this current to the Higgs boson via a top quark
loop. Throughout this section we drop the explicit indication of
higher-order corrections in equations, omitting a term
\!{}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
in the first line of (
7.1
) and so
forth.

An important property of the coefficients
C_{H}^{2}
for Drell-Yan and Higgs
production is their independence of the incoming parton polarisations. This
holds at all orders in
\alpha_{s}
. For
gg\to H
it follows from the fact
that angular momentum conservation only allows for two non-vanishing helicity
amplitudes, which are related by parity invariance. Therefore, all

\alpha_{s}
corrections can be expressed by a single hard-scattering
coefficient. The argument holds for the production of any scalar or
pseudoscalar boson via
gg
fusion.

For
q\bar{q}
annihilation into a boson via the vector current, one
additionally needs the conservation of quark chirality, which together with
angular momentum and parity conservation leaves a single independent helicity
amplitude. To appeal to quark chirality conservation in a calculation using
dimensional regularisation, one can adapt the argument given in

Collins:1999un
. As long as one works in
D=4-2\epsilon
dimensions,
one can avoid specifying the polarisation of the incoming partons and instead
compute the hard scattering as a matrix in Dirac space. Chirality
conservation then technically means that the hard-scattering amplitude
contains an odd number of
\gamma_{\mu}
matrices at any perturbative order.
The same holds for the terms that must be subtracted to remove ultraviolet and
infrared divergences. After these subtractions have been done, one can take

D=4
and use the relation between quark helicity and chirality.

The extension of this argument to
q\bar{q}
annihilation via the axial vector
current is simple in
D=4
dimensions: anticommuting the
\gamma_{5}
matrix
from the current, one finds
A_{\mu}=V_{\mu}\gamma_{5}
for the Dirac matrix of
the hard-scattering amplitudes of the two currents (with their divergences
subtracted). Their independent helicity amplitudes are hence the same (or
opposite in sign). To complete the argument, one needs to discuss the
implementation of the electroweak axial current in dimensional regularisation,
which we shall not do here.

7.2
Collins-Soper kernels and anomalous dimensions

In the limit of small
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
, the soft
matrix for DPS can be directly computed in perturbation theory, as we
derived in (
191
). Setting
U=\mathbb{1}
in (
332
),
we simply have
K=\widehat{K}=dS/dY
at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
. The
one-loop calculation of
S
in the
qq
channel is presented in detail in
section 3.3.2 of
Diehl:2011yj
. We can rewrite the results there
in such a way that we have a separate dependence on the two scales
\mu_{1}

and
\mu_{2}
. Splitting off a diagonal part according to
(
133
), we get

\displaystyle K_{qq}

\displaystyle=C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl[K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1})+K(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2})\bigr]\begin{pmatrix}1\penalty\ &0\\
0\penalty\ &1\end{pmatrix}+M_{qq}

(225)

with

\displaystyle M_{qq}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}{2N}\,K_{d}\\[3.00003pt]
\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}{2N}\,K_{d}\penalty\ &{}-\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5muK_{y}-\frac{1}{N}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\,\end{pmatrix}\,,

(226)

where the matrices are in colour representation space, with
R=(1,8)
. We
recall that
M_{qq}
is renormalisation scale independent. The scale
independent combinations of kernels read

\displaystyle K_{y}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu)+K(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu)-K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)-K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)\,,

\displaystyle K_{d}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)+K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad-K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)-K\Bigl(\boldsymbol{y}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2});\mu\Bigr)\,,

(227)

where

\displaystyle K(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\pi}\,\log\frac{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}\mu^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}

(228)

is the one-loop Collins-Soper kernel for single parton TMDs, with the colour
factor removed. We recall that
b_{0}=2e^{-\gamma_{E}}
.
We note that the two-loop result in section 4 of
Vladimirov:2016qkd

has the form given by (
225
), (
226
) and (
7.2
)
if one replaces
C_{F}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
with the two-loop Collins-Soper kernel
for a single quark TMD.

In the solution (
5.1
) of the evolution equations, one needs
the matrix exponential of
M_{qq}
times
L=\log\sqrt{\zeta/\zeta_{0}}
,
which reads (see section 3.4.2 of
Diehl:2011yj
)

\displaystyle\exp[\mskip 1.5muLM_{qq}\mskip 1.5mu]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{+}-d_{-}}\begin{pmatrix}d_{+}e^{Ld_{-}}-d_{-}e^{Ld_{+}}\penalty\ &\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}{2N}\,K_{d}\,\bigl(e^{Ld_{+}}-e^{Ld_{-}}\big)\,\\[3.00003pt]
\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}{2N}\,K_{d}\,\bigl(e^{Ld_{+}}-e^{Ld_{-}}\big)\penalty\ &d_{+}e^{Ld_{+}}-d_{-}e^{Ld_{-}}\end{pmatrix}

(229)

with

\displaystyle d_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\Biggl[-\frac{N^{2}}{2}K_{y}-K_{d}\pm\sqrt{\biggl(\frac{N^{2}}{2}K_{y}+K_{d}\biggr)^{2}+(N^{2}-1)K_{d}^{2}}\;\Biggr]\,.

(230)

An interesting situation arises if
|K_{d}|\ll\sqrt{N^{2}-1}\;|K_{y}|
, which
holds for instance in the large-
N
limit. As shown in section 3.4.2 of

Diehl:2011yj
, one then finds that
{}^{8}\mskip-1.5muF(\zeta)
is suppressed
compared with
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muF(\zeta)
for
\zeta\gg\zeta_{0}
. Note that the above
condition always holds in the limit
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
where
K_{d}
tends to zero.

It is easy to extend the above results to other parton channels, because the
one-loop graphs remain the same up to colour factors. For the quark-gluon
channels, we obtain

\displaystyle K_{qg}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\bigl[\mskip 1.5muC_{F}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1})+C_{A}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2})\mskip 1.5mu\bigr]\mathbb{1}+M_{qg}

(231)

with

\displaystyle M_{qg}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&0\\[3.99994pt]
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\penalty\ &-\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5mu(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})&\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-4}}{4}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\\[3.99994pt]
0&\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-4}}{4}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&-\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5mu(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})\,\end{pmatrix}\,.

(232)

Here the space of colour representations is
R=(1,A,S)
, corresponding to
the projectors in (
4.1
), and
\mathbb{1}
is the unit matrix in that
space.
For the two-gluon channel we get the kernel

\displaystyle K_{gg}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=C_{A}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl[K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1};\mu_{1})+K(\boldsymbol{z}_{2};\mu_{2})\bigr]\mathbb{1}+M_{gg}

(233)

with

\displaystyle M_{gg}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{N}{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&0&0&0\\[3.00003pt]
\frac{N}{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\penalty\ \penalty\ &-\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5mu(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})&\frac{N}{4}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&0&\sqrt{\frac{3}{8}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\\[3.00003pt]
0&\frac{N}{4}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&-\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5mu(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})&-\frac{3}{\sqrt{10}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&0\\[3.00003pt]
0&0&-\frac{3}{\sqrt{10}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&-3(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})&-\sqrt{\frac{3}{5}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}\\[3.00003pt]
0&\sqrt{\frac{3}{8}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&0&-\sqrt{\frac{3}{5}}\mskip 1.5muK_{d}&-4(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d})\,\end{pmatrix}

(234)

in the space of colour representations
R=(1,A,S,D,27)
, corresponding to
the projectors in (
4.1
). In the rows and columns for
D
and

27
, we have given the numerical coefficients for
N=3
.
The Collins-Soper kernels in channels with antiquarks can be obtained from the
above expressions by using the relation (
90
) and the analogue of
(
4.3
) for
{K}_{a_{1}a_{2}}
.

The matrices
M_{qg}
and
M_{gg}
are non-singular for generic values of the
functions
K_{y}
and
K_{d}
. The eigenvalues of
M_{qg}
can be calculated
analytically, but the expressions are rather lengthy and we refrain from
giving them here. For
N=3
, the eigenvalues of
M_{gg}
read

\displaystyle-3\bigl(K_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}K_{d}\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle-\tfrac{3}{2}K_{y}\,,

\displaystyle-\tfrac{3}{2}(K_{y}+K_{d})\,,

\displaystyle-(2K_{y}+K_{d})\pm\sqrt{(2K_{y}+K_{d})^{2}+3K_{d}^{2}}\,.

(235)

7.2.1
Limit of small
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}
and

\boldsymbol{z}_{2}

We now consider the limit where
|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|,|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|\ll|\boldsymbol{y}|
.
Expanding (
225
), (
231
) and (
233
) in powers of

|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}|/|\boldsymbol{y}|
and
|\boldsymbol{z}_{2}|/|\boldsymbol{y}|
and comparing the
leading terms with the structure in (
171
), where the full
kernel
{}^{RR^{\prime}}\!K_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})
is split into
three separate contributions, we can deduce the LO expressions of the kernels

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}
and
{}^{R}J
. We obtain

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{q}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=C_{F}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5muK_{q}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muK_{g}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=C_{A}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{A}\mskip-1.5muK_{g}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle={}^{S}\mskip-1.5muK_{g}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)=\frac{N}{2}\mskip 1.5muK(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

(236)

and

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muJ

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5muJ(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}\bigl[K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{1})+K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{2})\bigr]

(237)

with
K(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
defined in (
228
). For the higher
gluon representations we find

\displaystyle{}^{D}\mskip-1.5muK_{g}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{}^{27}\mskip-1.5muK_{g}(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

\displaystyle=-K(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)

(238)

and

\displaystyle{}^{D}\mskip-1.5muJ(\boldsymbol{z};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=3\bigl[K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{1})+K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{2})\bigr]\,,

\displaystyle{}^{27}\mskip-1.5muJ(\boldsymbol{z};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=4\bigl[K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{1})+K(\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{2})\bigr]\,,

(239)

where again we have set
N=3
. The expressions for
J
are of course only
valid at perturbatively small
\boldsymbol{y}
.

Using the explicit form of
K(\boldsymbol{z};\mu)
, we obtain anomalous dimensions

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,q}

\displaystyle=2C_{F}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,q}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}

\displaystyle=N\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,.

(240)

The expressions for other colour representations are readily obtained from
(
7.2.1
) and (
237
). We note that
{}^{8\,}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,q}
and

{}^{27\,}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,g}
are negative. Furthermore, we find that

{}^{R\,}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}>0
for all
R\neq 1
.

For completeness we also give the anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{a}
, which
can be taken from the literature for single TMDs, e.g. from

Echevarria:2016scs
(see our equation (
221
) for
notation). The one-loop expressions are

\displaystyle\gamma_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\mskip 1.5muC_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}

(241)

with
\beta_{0}=\frac{11}{3}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}-\frac{2}{3}\mskip 1.5mun_{F}
.
Finally, one has

\displaystyle\gamma_{K,q}

\displaystyle=2C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}+\frac{C_{F}}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\biggl(\frac{67-3\pi^{2}}{9}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}-\frac{10}{9}\mskip 1.5mun_{F}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\biggr)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{K,g}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{A}}{C_{F}}\,\gamma_{K,q}

(242)

for the cusp anomalous dimension at two-loop accuracy. Using the all-order
relation

\displaystyle{}^{8}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{K,g}\,,

(243)

which follows from (
185
), we readily get the two-loop expression of

{}^{8}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{J}
as well.

7.3
Matching coefficients for DTMDs

We now turn to the short-distance coefficients for matching DTMDs on DPDFs in
the large-
\boldsymbol{y}
regime. In the colour singlet channel, they are equal to
the coefficients for matching TMDs on PDFs, which for most polarisation
combinations have been computed by several
groups
Collins:2011zzd
;
Aybat:2011zv
;
Bacchetta:2013pqa
;
Echevarria:2015uaa
;
Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
. In addition to
extending these results to colour non-singlet channels, we have computed the
colour singlet coefficients for all polarisation channels. In the
following two subsections, we describe in detail our method, which allows us
to compute the one-loop matching coefficients from real emission graphs alone
and to handle gluon polarisation without using the Levi-Civita tensor in

D=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. Subtleties of renormalisation in both the quark
and gluon sector are discussed in section
7.3.3
. We give our final
results for the colour singlet coefficients in section
7.3.4

and explain their extension to other colour channels in
section
7.3.5
.

7.3.1
General procedure

To compute the matching coefficients, we take matrix elements of the operator
relations in (
160
) and (
161
), using either the
vacuum or a single parton as external states. We define

\displaystyle 2\pi\delta(p^{+}-p^{\prime+})\,2p^{+}\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{b}}\,\frac{1}{m(R)}\,\bigl\langle b,p^{\prime},r^{\prime}\big|\mskip 1.5mu{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\mskip 1.5mu\big|b,p,r\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{a}(R)\;\frac{\mathcal{N}_{a}}{\mathcal{N}_{b}}\,\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}}{m(R)}\,\bigl\langle b,p^{\prime},r^{\prime}\big|\mskip 1.5muO^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{s}}_{a}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\mskip 1.5mu\big|b,p,r\bigr\rangle\,,\hskip 40.00006pt

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{R}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}}{m(R)}\,\bigl\langle 0\big|\mskip 1.5muO_{S,a}^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r},\underline{s}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})\mskip 1.5mu\big|\mskip 1.5mu0\bigr\rangle\,,

(244)

where
|\mskip 1.5mub,p,r\rangle
is a parton state
b
with momentum
p
and colour
index
r
, and the colour projected operator
{}^{R}O^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r}}_{a}
was
introduced in (
79
). We recall that
\underline{r}=(r,r^{\prime})
and that
repeated colour indices are summed over. We gloss over the issue of parton
polarisation for the time being.
In the colour singlet sector, one has
\varepsilon_{a}(1)/m(1)=1
and the
contraction with
P_{1}^{\underline{r}\,\underline{s}}\,\mathcal{N}_{a}/\mathcal{N}_{b}
in

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ab}
implies a sum over the colour indices of the
operator and an average over the colour of the parton state.
Thus,
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})
is the
unsubtracted TMD for parton
a
in target
b
according to (
3.1
).
Likewise,
{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})
is the soft factor for single
TMDs. In analogy to (
7.3.1
) we define matrix elements

{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ab}(x)
and
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{S,a}
without argument

\boldsymbol{z}
from the operators with
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
; thus

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ab}(x)
is the PDF for parton
a
in target
b
. It is
understood that all operators are renormalised, and we recall that the
operators at
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
require additional renormalisation compared
with those at finite
\boldsymbol{z}
.

According to the operator matching equations (
161
) and
(
160
), we thus have

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{ac}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b}{}^{R}C_{\text{us},ab}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z})\,\underset{x}{\otimes}{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{bc}(x^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle={}^{R}C_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})\,{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}_{S,a}\,.

(245)

We now expand the quantities in these relations up to

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
, denoting the zeroth and first orders with
superscripts
(0)
and
(1)
, respectively. At tree level, the matrix
elements
\langle b,p^{\prime},r^{\prime}\mskip 1.5mu|\mskip 1.5muO^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{s}}_{a}\mskip 1.5mu|b,p,r\rangle
and

\langle 0|\mskip 1.5muO_{S,a}^{\mskip 1.5mu\underline{r},\underline{s}}\mskip 1.5mu|0\rangle
have the colour
structure
\delta_{rs}\,\delta_{r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
, which gives

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{ab}(x)=\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{S,a}=1

(246)

for the matrix elements on both sides of (
7.3.1
), which results in
zeroth-order matching coefficients

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{\text{us},ab}^{(0)}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=1\,.

(247)

Inserting this into the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
terms of (
7.3.1
),
we obtain

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{\text{us},ab}^{(1)}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z})-{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{ab}(x)\,,

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z})-{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{S,a}\,.

(248)

The one-loop matching coefficients are thus given as the difference of

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
matrix elements at finite and at zero
\boldsymbol{z}
.
Virtual graphs cancel in this difference, because they do not depend on

\boldsymbol{z}
, so we can limit our calculation to real graphs.

To obtain the matching coefficient
{}^{R}C_{ab}
of the TMD, we need the
square root of
{}^{R}C_{S,a}
according to (
165
). At

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};2Y)}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};2Y)=1+{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};Y)\,.

(249)

In the second step, we have used that
{}^{R}C_{S,a}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};Y)
is
linear in
Y
, which readily follows from its evolution equation
(
169
) and the fact that
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muK_{a}=\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
.
Inserting this into (
165
), we thus obtain at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})

\displaystyle{}^{R}C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\zeta)=\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}\Bigl[\mskip 1.5mu{}^{R}C^{(1)}_{\text{us},ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};Y_{L})-\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,{}^{R}C^{(1)}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};Y_{C}-Y_{L})\mskip 1.5mu\Bigr]\,,

(250)

where in the present section we use the definition (
61
) of

\zeta
, which refers to a single parton with plus-momentum
xp^{+}
. We have
omitted the argument
\mu
in all functions for brevity.

7.3.2
Calculation of gluon-gluon matching coefficients

We now describe in detail our procedure to compute the one-loop matching
coefficients in the pure gluon channel. Throughout this subsection, we
consider the colour singlet sector,
R=1
, and drop the corresponding index.
In turn, we now make gluon polarisation explicit. We take gluon operators

O_{g}^{jj^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z})
with open polarisation indices, obtained from
(
21
) and (
17
) by dropping the projection operator

\Pi^{jj^{\prime}}_{a}
in the latter equation. We also take open polarisation indices

i
,
i^{\prime}
for the gluons in the corresponding matrix element
(
7.3.1
), which thus carries four Lorentz indices,

\mathcal{M}^{jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}_{gg}
. All of them are restricted to be in the

D-2
transverse dimensions. The subscripts
g
refer of course only to the
parton type here, and not to its polarisation. For the gluon matrix elements,
we thus have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{jj^{\prime}\!,ll^{\prime}}_{gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=C^{jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}_{\text{us},gg}(x^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z})\,\underset{x}{\otimes}\mathcal{M}^{ii^{\prime}\!,ll^{\prime}}_{gg}(x^{\prime})+\text{\lx@text@lbrace quark-gluon mixing terms\lx@text@rbrace}

(251)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(0)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}_{gg}(x)=C^{(0)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z})=\delta^{ij}\,\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\delta(1-x)\,,

(252)

which is combined with the matching of the soft factor into

\displaystyle C^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\zeta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{Y_{L}\to-\infty}\Bigl[\mskip 1.5muC^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{\text{us},gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z};Y_{L})-\delta^{ij}\,\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\delta(1-x)\,C^{(1)}_{S,a}(\boldsymbol{z};Y_{C}-Y_{L})\mskip 1.5mu\Bigr]\,.

(253)

The virtual graphs contributing to the one-loop matrix elements are shown in
figure
11
. They are independent of
\boldsymbol{z}
(the Fourier
conjugated distance to the transverse momentum carried by partons or eikonal
lines) and hence cancel in the matching coefficient. Therefore we only
compute the real graphs in figure
11
. We omit so-called
Wilson line self interactions, where a gluon is exchanged between eikonal
lines along the same direction
v
, referring to chapter 13.7 of

Collins:2011zzd
for further discussion.

Figure 10:
Virtual graphs contributing to the matrix
elements
\mathcal{M}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{gg}
(a and b) and

\mathcal{M}_{S,g}^{(1)}
(c). Not shown are complex conjugate graphs and
the analogue of (a) with a quark instead of a gluon loop.
All eikonal lines are in the adjoint representation.

Figure 11:
Real graphs contributing to the matrix elements

\mathcal{M}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii}_{gg}
(a and b) and
\mathcal{M}_{S,g}^{(1)}

(c). Not shown are the complex conjugates of graphs (b) and (c).

We work in Feynman gauge and compute cut graphs (where the emitted gluon is
explicitly put on shell), using the Feynman rules given in
appendix
D
. For graph
11
b, we then
obtain

\displaystyle C_{\text{us},gg}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime},\,ii^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{z};Y_{L}\to-\infty)\mskip 1.5mu\big|_{\text{fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:Cgg-real}b}}=\frac{1}{xp^{+}}\int\!\frac{d^{4-2\epsilon}k}{(2\pi)^{4-2\epsilon}}\,\bigl(e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{z}}-1\bigr)\;2\pi\delta\big((p-k)^{2}\big)\,\delta(k^{+}-xp^{+})\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\delta_{de^{\prime}}}{N^{2}-1}\;\bigl(g\mu^{\epsilon}n^{\nu}f^{dbc}\bigr)\;\bigl(-g_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\delta_{bb^{\prime}}\bigr)\;\frac{i\delta_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mug_{\rho^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}}{k^{2}-i0}\,\frac{i\delta_{ce}}{(p-k)\mskip 1.5mun-i0}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigl(g\mu^{\epsilon}f^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\,\bigl[(p-2k)^{i^{\prime}}g^{\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}+(k+p)^{\nu^{\prime}}g^{i^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}+(k-2p)^{\rho^{\prime}}g^{i^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\bigr]\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigl(-i\bigl[kn\,g^{ij}-p^{j}n^{i}\bigr]\,\delta_{ae}\Bigr)\,\Bigl(i\mskip 1.5mu\bigl[kn\,g^{\tau^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-k^{j^{\prime}}n^{\tau^{\prime}}\bigr]\,\delta_{d^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\Bigr)+C_{\text{us},\epsilon}\mskip 1.5mu\big|_{\text{fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:Cgg-real}b}}\,,

(254)

where
n
is the four-vector with
n^{-}=1
,
n^{+}=0
and
\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The assignment of polarisation indices is shown in
figure
11
a. All primed indices refer to the right of the
final-state cut, and
a,b,\ldots,e
are colour octet indices. The factor

e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{z}}-1
represents the difference between the matrix
element for nonzero and zero
\boldsymbol{z}
. In accordance with our choice of
light-cone coordinates, the components
p^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
of the target
momentum are zero. The factor
(xp^{+})^{-1}
comes from the definition
(
21
) of the operator
O_{g}
. The ultraviolet counterterm
C_{\text{us},\epsilon}
will be discussed below.

In (
254
), we have taken the limit
Y_{L}\to-\infty
by setting
v_{L}=n
in the eikonal propagator. (Only then can
we use the Feynman rule given in figure
14
).
This gives a factor
p^{+}-k^{+}=p^{+}(1-x)
in the denominator. The
resulting rapidity divergences in the convolution of
C_{\text{us},gg}
with a PDF
or a DPDF are cancelled by corresponding divergences in the soft factor, as
we shall see shortly. In this sense, one should understand the limit
Y_{L}\to-\infty
in
C_{\text{us},gg}
as being taken in the combination
(
253
).

Adding the complex conjugate of (
254
) and
the expression for graph
11
a, we get

\displaystyle C_{\text{us},gg}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{z};Y_{L}\to-\infty)=\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{\pi^{2}}\,(2\pi\mu)^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{2-2\epsilon}\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}\,\bigl(e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{z}}-1\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigg\{\bigg[\frac{x}{1-x}+\frac{(1-x)(1+x^{2})}{x}\bigg]\,\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon)}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\biggl[\frac{x}{1-x}+\frac{2(1-x)}{1-\epsilon}\bigg]\,\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\Big(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{x}{1-x}\;\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\biggl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+x(1-x)\,\frac{k^{ii^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}+\frac{1-x}{x}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\,\frac{k^{jj^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+(1-x)\,\biggl(\delta^{ij}\frac{k^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}+\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\,\frac{k^{ij}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\,\frac{k^{i^{\prime}j}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}-\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\,\frac{k^{ij^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{k}^{2}}\biggr)\Bigg\}+C_{\text{us},\epsilon}\,,

(255)

where we define the symmetric and traceless tensor

\displaystyle v^{ij}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{v}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}^{j}-\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{2}}{2(1-\epsilon)}\;\delta^{ij}

(256)

for a given vector
\boldsymbol{v}
in
2(1-\epsilon)
transverse dimensions.
Using the integral relations in appendix
E
, we
perform the transverse integration and obtain

\displaystyle C_{\text{us},gg}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{z};-\infty)=-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{\pi}\,(2\pi\mu)^{2\epsilon}\;\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\,\biggl(\frac{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}{4\pi}\biggr)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigg\{\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\biggl[\frac{x}{1-x}+\frac{(1-x)(1+x^{2})}{x}\biggr]\,\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon)}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\biggl[\frac{x}{1-x}+\frac{2(1-x)}{1-\epsilon}\biggr]\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\frac{x}{1-x}\;\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\biggl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+x(1-x)\,\frac{z^{ii^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}+\frac{1-x}{x}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\,\frac{z^{jj^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+(1-x)\,\biggl(\delta^{ij}\,\frac{z^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}+\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\,\frac{z^{ij}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\,\frac{z^{i^{\prime}j}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}-\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\,\frac{z^{ij^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}\biggr)\Bigg\}+C_{\text{us},\epsilon}\,.

(257)

In (
255
) and (
257
) we have organised the tensor
structure into terms that are diagonal or antisymmetric or symmetric and
traceless in the index pair
jj^{\prime}
, for reasons that will become clear in
section
7.3.3
. We note that the tensor in parentheses in the
last line of (
257
) is nonzero in a generic number
2-2\epsilon
of transverse dimensions but vanishes for
\epsilon=0
. This
is easily seen by contracting this tensor with itself, which gives an
expression proportional to
\epsilon
.

We see that (
257
) has poles in
1/\epsilon
. They correspond
to ultraviolet divergences of the matrix element at
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}

and are removed by the counterterm
C_{\text{us},\epsilon}
. If one splits the

\boldsymbol{k}
integration in (
255
) into the parts going with

e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{z}}
and
-1
, then the
1/\epsilon
poles arise as
infrared divergences of the term with
e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{z}}
, which is
ultraviolet finite. The term with
-1
involves scaleless integrals, in
which the ultraviolet and infrared divergences add up to zero in dimensional
regularisation. In the sum of terms, the infrared divergences cancel and the
ultraviolet ones remain. The
\overline{\text{MS}}
counterterm reads

\displaystyle C_{\text{us},\epsilon}=\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{\pi}\,\frac{S_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon}\;

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\bigg[\frac{x}{1-x}+\frac{(1-x)(1+x^{2})}{x}\bigg]\,\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon)}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\frac{x}{1-x}+2(1-x)\bigg]\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{x}{1-x}\;\frac{1}{2}\,\biggl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\biggr)\Bigg\}\,,

(258)

where

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}=\bigl(4\pi e^{-\gamma_{E}}\bigr)^{\epsilon}\,.

(259)

The origin of the factor
1/(1-\epsilon)
multiplying

\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}
is explained in
section
7.3.3
; note that the naive implementation of

\overline{\text{MS}}
counterterms as “the residue of
1/\epsilon
times

S_{\epsilon}
” would lead to an incorrect result.

We now turn to the matching coefficient of the soft factor, which comes from
the graph in figure
11
c and its complex conjugate. Taking
again the limit
Y_{L}\to-\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{S,g}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};Y_{C}+\infty)=\int\frac{d^{4-2\epsilon}\ell}{(2\pi)^{4-2\epsilon}}\;\bigl(e^{-i\boldsymbol{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}}-1\bigr)\;2\pi\delta\bigl(\ell^{2}\bigr)\,\theta\bigl(\ell^{+}\bigr)\;\frac{\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\delta_{cc^{\prime}}}{N^{2}-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{i}{\ell\mskip 1.5muv_{C}+i0}\;\bigl(-g\mu^{\epsilon}v_{C}^{\nu^{\prime}}f^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\bigr)\;\bigl(-g_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\delta_{bb^{\prime}}\bigr)\;\bigl(g\mu^{\epsilon}n^{\nu}f^{cba}\bigr)\;\frac{i}{\ell\mskip 1.5mun-i0}+\text{c.c.}+C_{S,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{4\pi^{2}}\,(2\pi\mu)^{2\epsilon}\int d^{2-2\epsilon}\boldsymbol{\ell}\;\bigl(e^{-i\boldsymbol{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}}-1\bigr)\,\Bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\ell^{+}}{\ell^{+}}\,\frac{2v_{C}^{+}}{2(\ell^{+})^{2}\,v_{C}^{-}+\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}\,v_{C}^{+}-i0}

\displaystyle\hskip 180.00027pt-\frac{2}{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}}\int_{0}^{xp^{+}}\frac{d\ell^{+}}{\ell^{+}}+\frac{2}{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}}\int_{0}^{xp^{+}}\frac{d\ell^{+}}{\ell^{+}}\mskip 1.5mu\Bigg]+\text{c.c.}+C_{S,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi^{2}}\,(2\pi\mu)^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{2-2\epsilon}\boldsymbol{\ell}}{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}}\;\bigl(e^{-i\boldsymbol{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}}-1\bigr)\,\Bigg[\log\frac{2(xp^{+})^{2}\,e^{-2Y_{C}}}{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}}-2\int_{0}^{xp^{+}}\frac{d\ell^{+}}{\ell^{+}}\mskip 1.5mu\Bigg]+C_{S,\epsilon}\,,

(260)

where
C_{S,\epsilon}
is the ultraviolet counterterm and “c.c.” denotes
the complex conjugate term (which results in an extra factor of
2
in the
last line). The eikonal propagator depending on
n
gives rise to the
divergent integral over
1/\ell^{+}
in the third line. To combine this
efficiently with the divergence from
C_{\text{us},gg}
, we have subtracted and
added a divergent integral in the fourth line. The first two terms in
square brackets then combine to a finite integral, which gives the logarithm
in the last line. Having rewritten
v_{C}^{-}/v_{C}^{+}=-e^{-2Y_{C}}
, we
recognise the variable
\zeta
from (
61
) appearing in
this logarithm.

In the remaining divergent integral, we now change variables as
\ell^{+}=p^{+}(1-x^{\prime})
, motivated by the corresponding form of the gluon plus-momentum

p^{+}-k^{+}=p^{+}(1-x)
crossing the cut in figure
11
b.
Performing the Fourier transform using (
344
) and
(
345
), we then obtain

\displaystyle C_{S,g}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};Y_{C}+\infty)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\,(4\pi)^{\epsilon}\,\biggl(\frac{\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}{4}\biggr)^{\epsilon}\;\frac{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigg[\log\frac{4}{\zeta\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}-\gamma_{E}+\psi(-\epsilon)+2\int_{1-x}^{1}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{1-x^{\prime}}\Bigg]+C_{S,\epsilon}\,,

(261)

where
\psi(z)=\frac{d}{dz}\log\Gamma(z)
is the digamma function. Its
expansion around
\epsilon=0
gives
\psi(-\epsilon)=1/\epsilon+\ldots\,
, so that overall we obtain a double pole in
\epsilon
. We recall
that for a one-loop quantity that has the form

\displaystyle\frac{R_{-2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{R_{-1}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

(262)

the
\overline{\text{MS}}
-counterterm is given by

\displaystyle-S_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{R_{-2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{R_{-1}-S_{1}\mskip 1.5muR_{-2}}{\epsilon}\right)

(263)

times the tree-level term
C_{S,g}^{(0)}=1
, where the coefficient
S_{1}

comes from the Taylor expansion
S_{\epsilon}=(4\pi\exp(-\gamma_{E}))^{\epsilon}=1+S_{1}\mskip 1.5mu\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
. The additional contribution
S_{\epsilon}\mskip 1.5muS_{1}\mskip 1.5muR_{-2}/\epsilon
compensates the
1/\epsilon
term that arises
from Taylor expanding
S_{\epsilon}\mskip 1.5muR_{-2}/\epsilon^{2}
. Implementing
this prescription, we obtain the final form for the soft matching
coefficient:

\displaystyle C_{S,g}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{z};Y_{C}+\infty)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\,\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}L^{2}+L\,\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{\zeta}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}+2L\int_{1-x}^{1}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{1-x^{\prime}}\bigg]

(264)

with

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}\,,

(265)

where
b_{0}
is given in (
180
). We note in passing that
C_{S,q}
is
given by the same expression, with the colour factor
C_{A}
replaced by
C_{F}
.
Defining

\displaystyle S_{L}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}L^{2}+L\,\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{\zeta}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}

(266)

and combining
C_{\text{us},gg}
with
C_{S,g}
according to
(
253
), we obtain the full matching coefficient in tensor
form:

\displaystyle C_{gg}^{(1)\,jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad\!=\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\,\biggl\{-2L\,\bigg[\mskip 1.5mu\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+\frac{(1-x)(1+x^{2})}{x}\bigg]+S_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\mskip 1.5mu\biggr\}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\!+\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\,\biggl\{-2L\,\bigg[\mskip 1.5mu\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+2(1-x)\bigg]-4(1-x)+S_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\mskip 1.5mu\biggr\}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\!+\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\,\biggl\{-2L\;\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+S_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\mskip 1.5mu\biggr\}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Bigl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}-\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\!-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\;2x(1-x)\,\frac{z^{ii^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}-\frac{\alpha_{s}\mskip 1.5muC_{A}}{2\pi}\;\frac{2(1-x)}{x}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\,\frac{z^{jj^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}\,.

(267)

The rapidity divergences in the
C_{\text{us},gg}
and
C_{S,g}
have been
combined into the plus-distribution

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-x)_{+}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-x}-\delta(1-x)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{1-x^{\prime}}\,,

(268)

which gives a finite result in convolution integrals. The lower integration
limit of the
x^{\prime}
integral has changed from
1-x
in (
264
) to

0
in (
268
) because
C_{S,g}
is multiplied with

\delta(1-x)
in the combination formula (
253
).

Note that the method we have used avoids introducing the antisymmetric
tensor
\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}
in
D=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. With
the final result (
267
) in the
D=4
physical dimensions, it is
straightforward to perform a projection onto unpolarised or longitudinally
polarised gluons. On the operator side, one uses the spin projectors

\delta^{jj^{\prime}}
or
i\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}
from (
18
), and for the
gluon states in the matrix element, one contracts with
\delta^{ii^{\prime}}/2
or

-i\epsilon^{ii^{\prime}}/2
, which gives the average or half the difference of the
two helicity states, respectively.
14
14

14

The sign difference for
longitudinal polarisation reflects that one has polarisation vectors

\varepsilon
for incoming gluons and
\varepsilon^{*}
for outgoing
ones.

For linear gluon polarisation, we see that
\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,kk^{\prime}}
in
(
18
) projects on the symmetric and traceless part in the
indices
jj^{\prime}
, with the result depending on two transverse indices
kk^{\prime}
.
Matching coefficients involving linear gluon polarisation thus carry
indices, as already remarked earlier.

We will give the results of projecting (
267
) onto definite gluon
polarisations after discussing in more detail the renormalisation
counterterms we have used in our calculations.

7.3.3
Subtleties of renormalisation

As explained earlier, the renormalisation of TMDs involves only virtual
graphs, which drop out in the calculation of the one-loop matching
coefficients. It thus remains to discuss the renormalisation of the matrix
elements
\mathcal{M}^{jj^{\prime}\!,ll^{\prime}}_{ab}(x)
and
\mathcal{M}_{S,a}
,
which are respectively related with PDFs and with the soft factor at

\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Gluon polarisation.

We begin with renormalisation of gluon PDFs, which gives the counterterm
(
258
) for the real graphs in figure
11
. We
recall that the renormalisation of a scalar PDF operator (e.g. the one for
unpolarised gluons) reads

\displaystyle{O}(x)=Z(x^{\prime})\underset{x}{\otimes}{O}_{B}(x^{\prime})

(269)

and gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle=Z^{(0)}(x^{\prime})\underset{x}{\otimes}\mathcal{M}_{B}^{(1)}(x^{\prime})+Z^{(1)}(x^{\prime})\underset{x}{\otimes}\mathcal{M}_{B}^{(0)}(x^{\prime})\,,

(270)

where
Z^{(0)}(x)=\delta(1-x)
and the first-order term
Z^{(1)}(x)
is
given by the
\overline{\text{MS}}
prescription. Bare quantities are
denoted with a subscript
B
, and we ignore mixing with quark operators for
the time being. For the gluon operators
O^{jj^{\prime}}(x)
with open indices,
which we are now interested in, the renormalisation factor
Z
in
(
269
) turns into a tensor with four indices. As explained in
section 6.5 of
Collins:1984xc
, renormalisation is significantly
simplified by first splitting the tensor operator into its antisymmetric,
traceless symmetric diagonal terms, which in
2-2\epsilon
transverse
dimensions reads

\displaystyle O^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(O^{jj^{\prime}}-O^{j^{\prime}j}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(O^{jj^{\prime}}+O^{j^{\prime}j}+\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}O^{kk}\right)-\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon)}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}O^{kk}\,.

(271)

The three terms of this decomposition correspond to different irreducible
representations of the rotation group. Therefore, each of them is
renormalised by a scalar renormalisation factor, and there is no mixing
between them. To implement (
269
) in the tensor case, we project
all tensors in
jj^{\prime}
in the same way, which in particular gives

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\right)+\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon)}\,\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}

(272)

for the tree-level tensors in (
252
). This explains the
appearance of the factors
1/(1-\epsilon)
in the counterterm
(
258
). We note that the coefficients of the diagonal,
antisymmetric and traceless symmetric tensors in that term are proportional
to the well-known DGLAP kernels for the associated gluon polarisation (apart
from the
\delta(1-x)
terms from virtual graphs), which confirms the
consistency of the procedure. Contracting the last term in
(
272
) with the projector
\delta^{jj^{\prime}}
for unpolarised
gluons and taking the average over incoming gluon polarisations by
contracting with
\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\big/\bigl(2(1-\epsilon)\bigr)
, one
obtains unity, which corresponds to the correct normalisation of the
tree-level matrix element.

Notice also that the three terms in (
272
) have the same
symmetry properties in the index pairs
jj^{\prime}
and
ii^{\prime}
, which reflects that
there are no transitions between states corresponding to different
representations of the rotation group.

Quark polarisation.

We now turn our attention to quark distributions and their renormalisation.
The graphs to be computed for the matching coefficients are as in
figure
11
, with appropriate replacements of gluons by quarks
and with eikonal lines in the fundamental representation. The spinor
indices at the top of the graphs are to be contracted with the Dirac matrix
of the relevant operator
O_{q}
,
O_{\Delta q}
or
O_{\delta q}
, and
corresponding contractions are done for the Dirac indices of the quark
states in the matrix element.

As is well known, the treatment of quark polarisation in this context
requires a consistent definition of
\gamma_{5}
and of the
\epsilon
tensor
in dimensional regularisation. We use the scheme of ’t Hooft, Veltman and
Breitenlohner, Maison (HVBM)
tHooft:1972tcz
;
Breitenlohner:1977hr
,
where
\gamma_{5}=\frac{i}{4!}\mskip 1.5mu\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{\lambda}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\rho}
. Here

\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}
is the usual antisymmetric tensor if all
indices are in the four physical dimensions (with
\epsilon_{0123}=+1
)
and zero otherwise. We also introduce its counterpart
\epsilon^{ij}=\epsilon^{+-ij}
, where
i,j
take values in the
D-2
transverse
dimensions, as well as the tensor
\bar{\delta}^{ij}
, which equals

\delta^{ij}
if
i,j=1,2
and is zero for one or both of
i,j
in the
unphysical dimensions.

For transverse quark polarisation, we avoid the use of
\gamma_{5}
by taking
the Dirac matrix
\Gamma_{\delta q}^{j}
in the operator

\mathcal{O}_{\delta}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\delta q}^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}\sigma^{j^{\prime}+}=\frac{i}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}\gamma^{j^{\prime}}\gamma^{+}\,,

(273)

which is equal to the form
\tfrac{i}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\sigma^{j+}\gamma_{5}
in
(
15
) in the physical dimensions,
j=1,2
, whereas it
differs for values of
j
in the unphysical sector. The fermion trace in
the matching coefficient
C_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}
then contains two
matrices
\sigma^{j+}
and
\sigma^{i-}
. This gives exactly the same
result as one would obtain for the trace with
\sigma^{j+}\gamma_{5}
and

\sigma^{i-}\gamma_{5}
using the “naive dimensional regularisation”
prescription, where one assumes that
\gamma_{5}
has square
1
and
anticommutes with all
\gamma^{\mu}
. That prescription is often used in the
context of transverse quark polarisation, but we prefer the formulation with
(
273
). Note that with “naive dimensional regularisation”
one cannot treat all polarised matching coefficients on an equal footing,
since it can only be used to compute traces with an even number of

\gamma_{5}
matrices. The matrix
\sigma^{i+}
has also been used to define
quark transversity in the recent work
Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
, where
the matching coefficients for polarised TMDs were revisited in a systematic
way.

For longitudinal quark polarisation, the matrix
\gamma_{5}
is
unavoidable. In the HVBM scheme, one can rewrite the Dirac matrix in the
operator
\Gamma_{\Delta q}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{+}\gamma_{5}=-\frac{i}{4}\mskip 1.5mu\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{[j^{\prime}}\gamma^{j]}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{+}\,,

(274)

where we define
\gamma^{[i}\gamma^{j]}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\gamma^{i}\gamma^{j}-\gamma^{j}\gamma^{i}\bigr)
. A corresponding replacement
can be made for the matrix
\gamma^{-}\gamma_{5}
that appears in the fermion
trace for matrix elements with incoming longitudinally polarised quarks.
Postponing the contraction with the
\epsilon
tensor to the end of the
calculation, one can hence represent longitudinally polarised quarks by an
operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta q}^{jj^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{4}\mskip 1.5mu\bar{q}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{[j^{\prime}}\gamma^{j]}\gamma^{+}\mskip 1.5muq
with two transverse indices. This
makes it easy to see that one has mixing under renormalisation with
longitudinally polarised gluons, represented by the operator

\mathcal{O}_{\Delta g}^{jj^{\prime}}=G^{+[j^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5muG^{+j]}
that has the same
transformation behaviour under rotations. For all matching coefficients
with longitudinal polarisation,
{\Delta q\Delta q}
,
{\Delta g\Delta q}
,
{\Delta q\Delta g}
and
{\Delta g\Delta g}
, one then has a
tensor matching coefficient
C_{ab}^{jj^{\prime},ii^{\prime}}
that is antisymmetric in

jj^{\prime}
. As the only terms with ultraviolet divergences in a matching
coefficient arise from the matrix elements at
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
, they
must be independent of
\boldsymbol{z}
. This leaves
\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}
as the only possible tensor structure of the UV
divergent part of the matching coefficient. This is also the tensor
structure of the tree-level matrix element, which multiplies the one-loop
counterterm according to (
270
). For the renormalised matching
coefficient one thus has

\displaystyle C^{(1)\mskip 1.5mujj^{\prime},ii^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\bigr)\,B^{(1)}(x,\epsilon)-\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\delta^{ij}\delta^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\delta^{ij^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}j}\bigr)\,Z^{(1)}(x,\epsilon)

\displaystyle\quad+\{\text{terms finite for $\epsilon\to 0$}\}\,,

(275)

where
B^{(1)}
contains all ultraviolet divergent parts of the bare matrix
element and
Z^{(1)}
is the relevant one-loop renormalisation factor. The

\overline{\text{MS}}
prescription fixes the latter to be
S_{\epsilon}

times the pole part of
B^{(1)}
. The outcome of this discussion is that
one obtains the same counterterm (and hence the same renormalised result)
when using the tensor form (
7.3.3
) or when contracting it with

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}\epsilon^{ii^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\bar{\delta}^{ij}\bar{\delta}^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\bar{\delta}^{ij^{\prime}}\bar{\delta}^{i^{\prime}j}\bigr)

(276)

and working with scalar matching coefficients, which corresponds to using
the conventional operators
{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mskip 1.5mu\bar{q}\mskip 1.5mu\gamma^{+}\gamma_{5}\mskip 1.5muq
and

i\epsilon^{jj^{\prime}}G^{+j^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5muG^{+j}
in the HVBM scheme. Working with
(
7.3.3
) has the advantage that one does not need to distinguish
between physical and unphysical dimensions during the computation.

In the computations of
Echevarria:2015uaa
and

Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
, the tensor
\bar{\delta}
on the r.h.s. of
(
276
) was replaced by the full transverse metric
\delta
in

D-2
dimensions, as a modification of the proposal by Larin

Larin:1993tq
, where
\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\epsilon^{\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}}
was replaced by products of
D

dimensional instead of
4
dimensional metric tensors
(cf.
Moch:2015usa
for a recent discussion). We see that one obtains
the same result when one contracts (
7.3.3
) with
{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\bigl({\delta}^{ij}{\delta}^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-{\delta}^{ij^{\prime}}{\delta}^{i^{\prime}j}\bigr)

instead of
(
276
) and imposes
\overline{\text{MS}}
subtraction. The
essential point for this is that
B^{(1)}
and
Z^{(1)}
have the

same

\epsilon
dependent prefactor in this case. Since poles in

1/\epsilon
cancel in
B^{(1)}-Z^{(1)}
by construction, only the

\epsilon\to 0
limit of their prefactor enters in the renormalised matching
coefficient. The preceding argument readily generalises to order

\alpha_{s}^{n}
, where one should replace
B^{(1)}-Z^{(1)}
with
\sum_{m}B^{(n-m)}\otimes Z^{(m)}
, where
B^{(0)}=Z^{(0)}=\delta(1-x)
. If,
however, the divergent part of a quantity involves different tensor
structures, then the procedure just described is no longer guaranteed to
work correctly.

We note that (
274
) is a special case of the identity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mskip 1.5mu\bigl(\gamma_{\lambda}\gamma_{5}-\gamma_{5}\gamma_{\lambda}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{3!}\,\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\,\gamma^{[\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\rho]}

(277)

in
D
dimensions, which has been used for a long time when discussing
the axial current in the HVBM scheme
Collins:1984xc
;
Larin:1993tq
. The
explicit antisymmetrisation on the l.h.s. of (
277
), necessary
to make the current Hermitian, can be omitted in (
274
) because

\{\gamma^{+},\gamma_{5}\}=0
. As shown in

Collins:1984xc
;
Larin:1993tq
, the divergence of the flavour nonsinglet
axial current is nonzero if one uses
\overline{\text{MS}}
renormalisation
and the HVBM scheme, but a zero divergence can be achieved by an additional
finite renormalisation. The resulting nonsinglet current has zero anomalous
dimension. At order
\alpha_{s}
, the same finite renormalisation achieves
that the divergence of the flavour singlet axial current is given by the
Adler-Bell-Jackiw anomaly, as shown in
Larin:1993tq
(at higher
orders, the required renormalisation factors differ for the flavour singlet
and nonsinglet currents). In
Vogelsang:1996im
;
Matiounine:1998re
this
finite renormalisation was extended to the nonlocal axial current

\mathcal{O}_{\Delta q}
that defines longitudinally polarised quark
distributions, see also
Weber:1991wd
;
Stratmann:1995fn
. The finite
renormalisation can be written as

\displaystyle O^{\text{NS}}_{\Delta q}

\displaystyle=Z_{5}^{\text{NS}}\otimes O^{\text{NS}}_{\Delta q,\overline{\text{MS}}}\,,

\displaystyle O^{\text{S}}_{\Delta q}

\displaystyle=Z_{5}^{\text{S}}\otimes O^{\text{S}}_{\Delta q,\overline{\text{MS}}}\,,

\displaystyle O_{\Delta g}

\displaystyle=O_{\Delta g,\overline{\text{MS}}}

(278)

for the flavour singlet and nonsinglet operators in
x
space, where the
currents on the r.h.s. are renormalised by the standard

\overline{\text{MS}}
prescription. At order
\alpha_{s}
one has

\displaystyle Z_{5}^{\text{NS}}(x)

\displaystyle=Z_{5}^{\text{S}}(x)=1-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,4C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu(1-x)\,;

(279)

higher orders can be found in
Moch:2014sna
.
15
15

15

Note that
Z_{5}
,
called
Z
in
Moch:2014sna
, is different from
Z^{5}
given
in
Matiounine:1998re
. In that work, the renormalisation of
the bare current is written in terms of the product
Z^{-1}Z^{5}
of two
renormalisation factors (or matrices in the singlet sector), where

Z^{-1}
does
not
correspond to the
\overline{\text{MS}}

prescription.

This finite renormalisation achieves in particular that the DGLAP kernels

P_{qq}
and
P_{\Delta q\Delta q}
agree up to order
\alpha_{s}^{2}

Vogelsang:1996im
;
Matiounine:1998re
. It also achieves that the
hard-scattering coefficients of the Drell-Yan process in collinear
factorisation satisfy
W_{\Delta q\mskip 1.5mu\Delta\bar{q}}=-W_{q\bar{q}}
at
order
\alpha_{s}

Weber:1991wd
;
Ravindran:2002na
, where
W_{\Delta q\mskip 1.5mu\Delta\bar{q}}
is for the incoming quark and antiquark both being
longitudinally polarised and
W_{q\bar{q}}
for both being unpolarised. In
the results for the matching kernels given in the next subsection, we have
included the finite renormalisation (
278
). At the order we
are working at, this only affects the coefficient
C_{\Delta q\Delta q}
.

\overline{\text{MS}}
variants.

All results given in this paper so far refer to the prescription that a
renormalisation factor
Z
has the form

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(S_{\epsilon}\mskip 1.5mu\alpha_{s})^{n}\,Z^{(n)}\,,

(280)

where
\alpha_{s}
is the renormalised coupling and
Z^{(n)}
for
n\geq 1
is
a finite sum of poles in
\epsilon
, fixed uniquely by the requirement that
the renormalised quantity be finite at
\epsilon=0
. Equivalently, one may
express all quantities in terms of the rescaled coupling
\bar{\alpha}_{s}=S_{\epsilon}\mskip 1.5mu\alpha_{s}
and use minimal subtraction, where all counterterms
are sums of poles. The factor
S_{\epsilon}
is given in (
259
)
and was introduced in
Bardeen:1978yd
to simplify the finite terms
left after renormalisation.

A variant of the scheme has been proposed in chapter 3.2.6 of

Collins:2011zzd
, where instead of
S_{\epsilon}
in (
259
)
one uses

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}^{\text{JCC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\,.

(281)

The difference between the two versions is

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}-S_{\epsilon}^{\text{JCC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\,\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\,.

(282)

For one-loop quantities with only a single pole in
\epsilon
, this is of no
consequence, but it does matter for quantities with a double pole. From
(
263
) we see that the counterterm is shifted by
\pi^{2}/12
times
the coefficient of the double pole if one uses
S_{\epsilon}^{\text{JCC}}

instead of
S_{\epsilon}
. For the one-loop matching coefficients, this
scheme change affects the soft factor and simply removes the term
-\pi^{2}/12
in the quantity
S_{L}
defined in (
266
).

Like any choice of renormalisation prescription, the choice of
S_{\epsilon}

must drop out in physical quantities such as the overall cross section. At

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
this choice affects the matching coefficients

{}^{R}C_{ab}
, the hard-scattering coefficients
C_{H}^{2}
(with squared
logarithms in (
7.1
) indicating the presence of double poles

1/\epsilon^{2}
before renormalisation), as well as DPDFs in colour non-singlet
channels and DTMDs in any colour channel (via one-loop renormalisation of the
soft factors in their definitions). Collins-Soper and DGLAP kernels, as well
as anomalous dimensions, are not affected by the choice of
S_{\epsilon}
at

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
.

7.3.4
Matching coefficients for DTMDs on DPDFs

We now give our final results for the matching coefficients for DTMDs at large

\boldsymbol{y}
in the colour singlet sector, which are equal to the matching
coefficients for single parton TMDs. We have computed them not only with the
rapidity regulator used in this paper, but also with the so-called
\delta

regulator specified in
GarciaEchevarria:2011rb
, and we find complete
agreement between the two versions.

In the gluon sector, we start from the tensor form (
267
) and
project onto unpolarised or longitudinally polarised gluons, which gives

\displaystyle C_{gg}(x,\boldsymbol{z})=\delta(1-x)

\displaystyle-L\,\biggl[\,P_{gg}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}\,\biggr]+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{A}\mskip 1.5muS_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\,,

(283)

\displaystyle C_{\Delta g\Delta g}(x,\boldsymbol{z})=\delta(1-x)

\displaystyle-L\,\biggl[\,P_{\Delta g\Delta g}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}\,\biggr]+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{A}\mskip 1.5muS_{L}\,\delta(1-x)

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,4C_{A}\,(1-x)\,,

(284)

where we recall that

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle S_{L}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}L^{2}+L\,\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{\zeta}-\sigma\mskip 1.5mu\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}

with
\sigma=1
if one takes
S_{\epsilon}
from (
259
) for
the
\overline{\text{MS}}
counterterms, whereas
\sigma=0
if one takes

S_{\epsilon}^{\text{JCC}}
from (
281
). The DGLAP splitting
kernels
P_{gg}(x)
and
P_{\Delta g\Delta g}(x)
include a term
proportional to the anomalous dimension
\gamma_{g}=\beta_{0}\mskip 1.5mu\alpha_{s}/(2\pi)
, which comes from virtual graphs (see figure
11
).
This term is subtracted again in the above matching coefficients, which only
receive contributions from real graphs as explained earlier. We list all
kernels
P_{ab}
at the end of this subsection.

Matching coefficients involving linear gluon polarisation carry tensor
indices, and we extract from (
267
) the forms

\displaystyle C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}^{jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}\mskip 1.5muC_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}\,,\phantom{\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\biggr)}

(285)

\displaystyle C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\boldsymbol{z}^{j}\boldsymbol{z}^{j^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\biggr)\,C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}\,,

(286)

\displaystyle C_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}^{ii^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{2\boldsymbol{z}^{i}\boldsymbol{z}^{i^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{z}^{2}}-\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\biggr)\,C_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}\,,

(287)

where
\tau^{jj^{\prime}\!,ii^{\prime}}
is defined in (
19
) and the scalar
coefficient functions read

\displaystyle C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\delta(1-x)-L\,\biggl[\,P_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}\,\biggr]+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{A}\mskip 1.5muS_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\,,

(288)

\displaystyle C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{A}\,\frac{1-x}{x}\,,

(289)

\displaystyle C_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{A}\,x(1-x)\,.

(290)

The prefactor in (
286
) has been chosen such that
C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}
coincides with the corresponding matching coefficient defined
for single gluon TMDs in
Echevarria:2015uaa
;
Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
.

In the quark sector, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{qq}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\delta(1-x)-L\,\biggl[\,P_{qq}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{q}\,\biggr]+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\mskip 1.5muS_{L}\,\delta(1-x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\hskip 8.50006pt+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,(1-x)\,,

(291)

\displaystyle C_{\Delta q\Delta q}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=C_{qq}(x,\boldsymbol{z})\,,

(292)

with
\gamma_{q}=3C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\alpha_{s}/(2\pi)
. For transverse
quark polarisation, one has again a tensor valued coefficient

\displaystyle C_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}^{j,i}

\displaystyle=\delta^{ji}_{\phantom{\delta q}}C_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}^{\phantom{j}}

(293)

with

\displaystyle C_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}(x,\boldsymbol{z})=\delta(1-x)

\displaystyle-L\,\biggl[\,P_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{q}\,\biggr]+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\mskip 1.5muS_{L}\,\delta(1-x)\,.

(294)

We note that the tensor structure
\boldsymbol{z}^{j}\boldsymbol{z}^{i}
would be allowed
in (
293
) but does not appear at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
.
For transitions between quarks and gluons, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{qg}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-L\,P_{qg}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2T_{F}\,x(1-x)\,,

(295)

\displaystyle C_{\Delta q\Delta g}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-L\,P_{\Delta q\Delta g}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2T_{F}(1-x)

(296)

and

\displaystyle C_{gq}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-L\,P_{gq}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,x\,,

(297)

\displaystyle C_{\Delta g\Delta q}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-L\,P_{\Delta g\Delta q}(x)-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{F}(1-x)\,,

(298)

where we have re-introduced the normalisation factor
T_{F}=1/2
.
The analogues of (
289
) and
(
290
) for quark-gluon transitions are

\displaystyle C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5muq}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{F}\,\frac{1-x}{x}\,,

(299)

\displaystyle C_{q\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}(x,\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\;2T_{F}\,x(1-x)\,,

(300)

where the scalar coefficients are defined as in (
286
) and
(
287
) with the subscript
g
replaced by
q
for the
unpolarised parton.

DPDs associated with linear gluon or transverse quark polarisation are tensor
valued and can be decomposed into basis tensors that multiply scalar (or
pseudoscalar) distributions. We give these decompositions in
appendix
F
and list the resulting matching equations
between DTMDs and DPDFs in appendix
G
.

Matching coefficients
C_{ab}
with exactly one longitudinal polarisation
(
\Delta g
or
\Delta q
) are zero due to parity invariance. Coefficients
with exactly one transverse quark polarisation
\delta q
vanish because the
relevant graphs involve the trace of an odd number of
\gamma
matrices. Due
to charge conjugation invariance, the coefficients
C_{ab}
remain the same if
one replaces all quark indices by antiquark ones. These symmetry
relations hold at all orders in
\alpha_{s}
. At one-loop level, there are no
transitions between quarks and antiquarks or between quarks of different
flavours. These appear starting at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
.

Most of the above coefficients have been calculated in the literature
before. The coefficients in (
283
),
(
284
),
(
289
) and (
297
) to
(
299
) were calculated in
Echevarria:2015uaa
, using SCET
and the
\delta
regulator in the version of
GarciaEchevarria:2011rb
.
We find agreement between our results and those in the arXiv version 5 of

Echevarria:2015uaa
. Our results in
(
291
) to (
296
) agree with
Aybat:2011zv
(which
contains unpolarised results only) and with
Bacchetta:2013pqa
. The
results in (
283
), (
286
),
(
297
) and (
299
) agree with
Catani:2013tia
and

Becher:2012yn
. Apart from
C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
,

C_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
and
C_{q\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
, all matching coefficients were
recently calculated in
Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
, using the
\delta

regulator briefly described in our appendix
B
. We agree
with the results in the arXiv version 2 of
Gutierrez-Reyes:2017glx
.

To the best of our knowledge,
C_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
,
C_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
and
C_{q\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}
in (
288
),
(
290
) and (
300
) have not been
given in the literature before. They are not relevant for single parton
TMDs in a nucleon, since the PDF for a linearly polarised gluon vanishes in
that case. This is because linear gluon polarisation corresponds to the
interference between gluons with helicity
+1
and
-1
in the amplitude and
its conjugate.

Let us finally list the leading-order DGLAP splitting functions, which were
first derived in
Altarelli:1977zs
;
Artru:1989zv
. They are given by

\displaystyle P_{qq}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,\frac{1+x^{2}}{(1-x)_{+}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{q}\,,

(301)

\displaystyle P_{\Delta q\Delta q}(x)

\displaystyle=P_{qq}(x),

(302)

\displaystyle P_{\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta q}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,\frac{2x}{(1-x)_{+}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{q}

(303)

for quark-quark transitions and by

\displaystyle P_{gg}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{A}\,\biggl[\,\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+\frac{(1-x)(1+x^{2})}{x}\,\biggr]+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}\,,

(304)

\displaystyle P_{\Delta g\Delta g}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{A}\,\biggl[\,\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+2(1-x)\,\biggr]+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}\,,

(305)

\displaystyle P_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,2C_{A}\,\frac{x}{(1-x)_{+}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{g}

(306)

for gluons. The splitting functions mixing quarks and gluons are

\displaystyle P_{qg}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,T_{F}\Big[x^{2}+(1-x)^{2}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle P_{gq}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,\frac{1+(1-x)^{2}}{x}\,,

(307)

\displaystyle P_{\Delta q\Delta g}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,T_{F}\,\Big[x^{2}-(1-x)^{2}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle P_{\Delta g\Delta q}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,C_{F}\,\frac{1-(1-x)^{2}}{x}\,.

(308)

The symmetry properties of the matching coefficients
C_{ab}
detailed below
(
300
) also hold for the splitting functions
P_{ab}
. In
addition,
P_{g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}=P_{\delta g\mskip 1.5mug}=0
because the collinear
operators
O_{g}(x)
and
O_{\delta g}^{jj^{\prime}}(x)
transform differently
under rotations and hence cannot mix under renormalisation.

7.3.5
Colour non-singlet channels

It is easy to derive the matching coefficients in colour non-singlet channels
from the results we have just presented. All real graphs in
figure
11
have the same colour factor, so that the colour
projections in (
7.3.1
) affect all graphs in the same way. The same
holds for the graphs for quark-quark transitions and for transitions between
quarks and gluons (in the latter case, the soft factor does not contribute to
the one-loop matching coefficients and one only needs the analogues of
graphs
11
a and b). We can therefore write

\displaystyle{}^{R}C^{(1)}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=c_{ab}(R)\;{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muC^{(1)}_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\,.

(309)

The ratios
c_{ab}(R)
of colour factors between the representation
R
and
the singlet channel can be determined from the basic ladder graphs,
which have the topology of figure
11
a. They are readily
obtained from the evolution kernels given in section 5.1.3 in

Diehl:2011yj
and read

\displaystyle c_{qq}(8)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N^{2}-1}\,,

\displaystyle c_{gq}(A)

\displaystyle=c_{qg}(A)=\sqrt{\frac{N^{2}}{2(N^{2}-1)}}\,,

\displaystyle c_{gg}(A)

\displaystyle=c_{gg}(S)=\frac{1}{2}\,,

\displaystyle c_{gq}(S)

\displaystyle=c_{qg}(S)=\sqrt{\frac{N^{2}-4}{2(N^{2}-1)}}

(310)

and for
N=3

\displaystyle c_{gg}(D)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle c_{gg}(27)

\displaystyle=-1/3\,.

(311)

For transitions involving antiquarks one has

c_{\bar{q}\bar{q}}(8)=c_{qq}(8)
,

c_{g\bar{q}}(S)=c_{\bar{q}g}(S)=c_{qg}(S)
and

c_{g\bar{q}}(A)=c_{\bar{q}g}(A)=-c_{qg}(A)
. The minus sign in the last
case reflects the fact that two gluons coupled to an antisymmetric octet have
negative charge parity.

We can now derive the explicit form of the LO DGLAP kernels in a general
colour representation
R
. For this, we use the evolution equation
(
6.2.2
) for the matching coefficients
{}^{R}C_{ab}
. At order

\alpha_{s}
, we can replace
{}^{R}C_{ab}
on the r.h.s. by its
lowest-order value
\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x^{\prime})
and thus obtain

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ac}(x;\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{ac}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{F,c}(\mu,\zeta)-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}{}^{R}C_{ac}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)\,.

(312)

Combining this with (
309
), we readily find that the leading-order
DGLAP evolution kernels of collinear DPDs in colour non-singlet channels are
given by

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)-\frac{c_{ab}(R)}{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\log\mu}{}^{1}C_{ab}(x,\boldsymbol{z};\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-c_{aa}(R)}{2}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{F,a}(\mu,\zeta)+c_{ab}(R)\,{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,{}^{R}\gamma_{J}(\mu)\,\log\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}}{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1-c_{aa}(R)}{2}\,\delta_{ab}\,\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{a}(\mu)+c_{ab}(R)\,{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muP_{ab}(x;\mu)\,.

(313)

They are hence easily recovered from their counterparts in the colour
singlet channel. In the last step we have brought the kernel into the form
(
143
), using the explicit
\zeta
dependence
(
5
) of
\gamma_{F,a}
, the general relation (
172
)
between anomalous dimensions, and the one-loop relation

{}^{R}\gamma_{K,a}=c_{aa}(R)\,{}^{1}\mskip-1.5mu\gamma_{K,a}
, which follows
from (
309
). We recall that the colour singlet kernels

{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muP_{aa}(x;\mu)
contain a contribution
\delta(1-x)\,\gamma_{a}/2

from virtual graphs, see (
301
) to (
306
). The last line
of (
7.3.5
) simply reflects that these graphs have the same colour
factor for all channels
R
.

Using (
7.3.5
), one can rewrite the
\zeta
independent
distributions
\widehat{F}
introduced in (
5.2
) as

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widehat{F}_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\exp\,\biggl[\bigl(1-c_{a_{1}a_{1}}(R)\bigr)\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\gamma_{a_{1}}(\mu)

\displaystyle\qquad\,+\bigl(1-c_{a_{2}a_{2}}(R)\bigr)\int_{\mu_{0}}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\gamma_{a_{2}}(\mu)\mskip 1.5mu\biggr]\;{}^{R}\mskip-1.5mu\widetilde{F}_{a_{1}a_{2},\,\mu_{0},\zeta_{0}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{y};\mu_{i})\,,

(314)

where
\widetilde{F}
satisfies the evolution equation (
5.2
) with
kernels
c_{a_{1}b_{1}}(R)\,{}^{1}\mskip-1.5muP_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime};\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{2})
instead
of
{}^{R}\mskip-1.5muP_{a_{1}b_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime};\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{2})
. One can thus use
numerical code for the one-loop evolution of colour singlet DPDs by rescaling
the evolution kernels.

7.4
Splitting kernels for DPDs

Both DTMDs and DPDFs can be matched on single-parton distributions at small

\boldsymbol{y}
as specified in (
154
), (
193
) and
(
6.3.1
). At
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
, all three matching
equations involve the same kernels
{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}^{ll^{\prime}}
, which
were computed in section 5.2.2 of
Diehl:2011yj
. We list them here
in the notation of
Diehl:2017kgu
(arXiv version 2), where compared to

Diehl:2011yj
the kernels
T
have the opposite order of
indices and include a colour factor. Thus one has for instance

\displaystyle{}^{1}T^{ll^{\prime}}_{g\to q\bar{q}}\,\big|_{\text{here}}

\displaystyle=T_{F}\mskip 1.5muT^{l^{\prime}\mskip-1.5mul}_{g\to q\bar{q}}\,\big|_{\text{Ref.\penalty\ \smash{\cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Diehl:2011yj}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T^{ll^{\prime}}_{q\to g\mskip 1.5mu\Delta{q}}\,\big|_{\text{here}}

\displaystyle=C_{F}\mskip 1.5muT^{l^{\prime}\mskip-1.5mul}_{q\to g\mskip 1.5mu\Delta{q}}\,\big|_{\text{Ref.\penalty\ \smash{\cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Diehl:2011yj}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}}}\,.

(315)

The new assignment of indices is such that
l
refers to the amplitude and

l^{\prime}
to its complex conjugate. We must note a mistake in equation (5.62) of

Diehl:2011yj
, where the correct order of indices in the kernels
T

and
U
is
l^{\prime}\mskip-1.5mul
and not
ll^{\prime}
.

We only give the kernels for the splitting of an unpolarised parton
a_{0}

here. In the matching equation (
154
) for DTMDs on TMDs, one can
also have transverse quark or linear gluon polarisation for
a_{0}
, but this
possibility is absent if one matches on collinear PDFs in an unpolarised
proton.

We start with the kernels for DPDs in the colour singlet channel. For a
gluon splitting into a quark and an antiquark, one has

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{g\to q\bar{q}}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle={}-{}^{1}T_{g\to\Delta q\Delta\bar{q}}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=T_{F}\mskip 1.5mu(u^{2}+\bar{u}^{2})\,\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{g\to\Delta q\mskip 1.5mu\bar{q}}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle={}-{}^{1}T_{g\to q\Delta\bar{q}}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=-iT_{F}\mskip 1.5mu(u-\bar{u})\,\epsilon^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Bigl[{}^{1}T_{g\to\delta q\mskip 1.5mu\delta\bar{q}}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)\Bigr]^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-2T_{F}\mskip 1.5muu\bar{u}\;\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\delta^{jj^{\prime}}\,,

(316)

where
\bar{u}=1-u
. For a quark splitting into a gluon and a quark, the
kernels are given by

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{q\to gq}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{1+\bar{u}^{2}}{u}\,\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{q\to\Delta g\mskip 1.5muq}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=iC_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{1+\bar{u}^{2}}{u}\,\epsilon^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{q\to\Delta g\Delta q}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu(1+\bar{u})\,\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{q\to g\Delta q}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=iC_{F}\mskip 1.5mu(1+\bar{u})\,\epsilon^{ll^{\prime}}\,,\phantom{\frac{1^{2}}{1}}

\displaystyle\left[{}^{1}T_{q\to\delta g\mskip 1.5muq}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)\right]^{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2C_{F}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{\bar{u}}{u}\,\tau^{ll^{\prime},jj^{\prime}}

(317)

with
\tau^{ll^{\prime},jj^{\prime}}
defined in (
19
). The kernels for an
antiquark splitting into a gluon and antiquark have the same form due to
charge conjugation invariance. Finally, for a gluon splitting into two
gluons one has

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{g\to gg}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=2C_{A}\left(\frac{u}{\bar{u}}+\frac{\bar{u}}{u}+u\bar{u}\right)\,\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{g\to\Delta g\Delta g}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=2C_{A}(2-u\bar{u})\,\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\,,\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle{}^{1}T_{g\to g\Delta g}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)

\displaystyle=2iC_{A}\left(2\bar{u}+\frac{u}{\bar{u}}\right)\,\epsilon^{ll^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\left[{}^{1}T_{g\to\delta g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)\right]^{jj^{\prime},kk^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=C_{A}\mskip 1.5muu\bar{u}\;\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\tau^{jj^{\prime},kk^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\left[{}^{1}T_{g\to g\mskip 1.5mu\delta g}^{ll^{\prime}}(u)\right]^{kk^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2C_{A}\mskip 1.5mu\frac{u}{\bar{u}}\,\tau^{ll^{\prime},kk^{\prime}}\,.

(318)

All other kernels that cannot be obtained from those above by interchanging

a_{1}
and
a_{2}
or by changing quarks into antiquarks in (
7.4
)
are zero.
In appendix
H
we give the matching equations that
follow from these results for the scalar and pseudoscalar DTMDs or DPDFs
defined in appendix
F
.

Each of the above kernels corresponds to exactly one Feynman graph, so that
the results for other colour channels can be obtained by changing the
overall factor as

\displaystyle{}^{R}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}

\displaystyle=c_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}(R)\;{}^{1}T_{a_{0}\to a_{1}a_{2}}

(319)

with

\displaystyle c_{q\to gq}(A)

\displaystyle=-\frac{N}{\sqrt{2}}\,,

\displaystyle c_{q\to gq}(S)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{N^{2}-4}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle c_{g\to q\bar{q}}(8)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}\,,

\displaystyle c_{g\to gg}(A)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle c_{g\to gg}(S)

\displaystyle=-c_{g\to gg}(A)\,,

\displaystyle c_{g\to gg}(D)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle c_{g\to gg}(27)
